# MIN183 — Relationships & Ethics in Ministry

## Course objectives

- 1. Explain the biblical foundations for ministerial ethics.
- 2. Describe the areas of a minister’s personal life that must be strengthened to ensure long-term ministry and avoid moral transgression.
- 3. Identify common pitfalls of moral transgression and practical steps a minister can take to avoid them.
- 4. Describe the principles involved in working with other ministers and churches in a healthy, courteous, and life-giving manner.
- 5. Define the concept of Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship and the relationship a minister has with the Fellowship.
- 6. Identify the healthy characteristics of both being married and being single in ministry.
- 7. Describe the key values of raising children who love the Lord and His church.
- 8. Describe ways a church or ministry can incorporate systems in the church to protect people and resources.
- 9. Explain how to execute key ministry responsibilities in an ethical manner that builds the body of Christ.
- 10. Identify how a twenty-first century minister can maintain proper boundaries in digital communications and social media.
- 11. Describe a process for making ethical decisions.

## 1

### A Basic Understanding of Ethical Systems

#### 1.1.1 — Define teleological and deontological ethics and explain how Christian ethics draws from both while being grounded in Scripture.

Before exploring ministerial ethics in detail, students need to understand the broader framework of ethical systems. This lesson introduces two major approaches to ethics— teleological and deontological —and explains how Christian ethics align with a deontological system that is based on God’s moral principles rather than the outcome of actions.

Two Views on Moral Decision-Making

To understand ministerial ethics, begin with a basic overview of general ethical systems, including the foundation of Christian ethics. Although people approach ethics in various ways, most ethical systems fall into two broad categories: teleological and deontological.

The term teleological comes from the Greek word telos , meaning “end” or “goal.” A teleological system evaluates actions based on their outcomes. This approach is often summed up in the phrase, “The ends justify the means.” In this view, if an action results in a good outcome, it is considered right. This system is also known as consequentialism. Clark and Rakestraw explain: “If following the rule produces maximum happiness or pleasure, then the rule is obligatory” (Clark and Rakestraw 1994, 20).

Two common types of teleological ethics are:

Egoism, which focuses on what benefits the individual.

Utilitarianism, which emphasizes what benefits the greatest number of people or the common good (Clark and Rakestraw 1994, 20).

By contrast, deontological ethics is based on duty and moral principles. In this system, actions are considered right or wrong based on moral standards, not on the results they produce. For Christians, ethics are clearly deontological, because the standard for what is right is based on the principles found in God’s Word.

These two ethical systems often lead to very different conclusions. Ministers need to recognize that the ethical system used to evaluate right and wrong influences the way we make decisions and apply truth in our lives.

**Q:** How does the ethical system we use affect the way we understand and apply truth to our lives and decisions?

**A:** Those who adopt a deontological view of ethics typically believe that truth is absolute, based on what is right and wrong according to an external moral standard. Those who follow a teleological approach often see truth as relative, and they evaluate right and wrong based on outcomes—believing the ends justify the means.

### The Biblical Foundation for Ministerial Ethics

#### 1.2.1 — Identify the biblical books that provide specific wisdom regarding ministerial ethics, particularly the Pastoral Epistles.

Ministerial ethics are not simply professional standards or personal preferences. These ethics are rooted in Scripture, particularly in the teachings found in the Pastoral Epistles, which provide timeless guidance for how ministers should live, lead, and guard the message of the gospel.

Biblical Foundations from the Pastoral Epistles

This course focuses on ministerial ethics rather than general Christian ethics. Although we will not conduct a full Bible study on ethics in this lesson, students need to recognize that the entire Bible presents a consistent call to ethical living. Key passages such as the Ten Commandments and the Sermon on the Mount provide clear moral instruction, and spiritual leaders are called to live out these teachings as examples to others.

The Pastoral Epistles—1 Timothy, 2 Timothy, and Titus—are particularly helpful in shaping our understanding of ethical ministry. These letters highlight many aspects of pastoral leadership, including:

The desire to serve in ministry (1 Timothy 3:1)

The testing of ministry candidates (1 Timothy 3:10)

The team-based nature of ministry (1 Timothy 4:14)

The use of spiritual gifts, prophecy, and discernment (1 Timothy 4:1, 14)

The importance of accountability, even for elders (1 Timothy 5:19)

In addition to these topics, Paul gives instructions to his apostolic delegates and the early church on the importance of Christian character in the ministerial ranks.

### Guarding the Gospel Message

#### 1.3.1 — Explain Paul's instructions to Timothy about guarding the gospel and describe what it means for ministry to be incarnational.

Every minister is entrusted with something sacred—the message of the gospel. This lesson explores how Paul instructed Timothy to guard this spiritual deposit, and how that same responsibility applies to all ministers today.

Paul exhorted Timothy, “What you heard from me, keep as the pattern of sound teaching, with faith and love in Christ Jesus. Guard the good deposit that was entrusted to you—guard it with the help of the Holy Spirit who lives in us” (2 Timothy 1:13–14). Paul’s instruction echoes his earlier words: “Timothy, guard what has been entrusted to your care” (1 Timothy 6:20).

William Kay and John Moxon explain that in Paul’s time, the word deposit referred to something valuable—often money, property, or even a person—that was entrusted to another for safekeeping while the owner was away (Kay and Moxon 2022, 187). In the previous verses, the deposit is the gospel of Jesus Christ. When Jesus ascended to heaven, He entrusted His followers with the mission of making disciples of all nations.

Ministers are still responsible for protecting the sacred trust of the gospel. Paul gave Timothy specific guidance on how to do this: “Watch your life and doctrine closely. Persevere in them, because if you do, you will save both yourself and your hearers” (1 Timothy 4:16). Paul emphasized that both life and doctrine matter. The minister’s example is inseparable from the message. In addition, Paul wrote to the Philippians, “Whatever happens, conduct yourselves in a manner worthy of the gospel of Christ” (Philippians 1:27). The way we live must reflect the truth we proclaim.

Ministry is incarnational by nature. Jesus modeled this by becoming human and living among us. He not only taught truth—He embodied it. Paul expressed the same idea to the Thessalonian church: “Because we loved you so much, we were delighted to share with you not only the gospel of God but our lives as well” (1 Thessalonians 2:8). Ministers cannot separate their message from their lifestyle. If their conduct is unethical, the message they preach loses credibility.

James warned, “Not many of you should become teachers, my fellow believers, because you know that we who teach will be judged more strictly” (James 3:1). Ministers are held to a higher standard; however, they do not bear their responsibility alone. Paul reminded Timothy, “Guard the good deposit that was entrusted to you—guard it with the help of the Holy Spirit who lives in us” (2 Timothy 1:14). The Holy Spirit empowers ministers to remain faithful. As ministers walk in obedience and pay close attention to both their lives and biblical doctrine, they will fulfill the sacred task of guarding the gospel.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister is responsible to guard the good deposit of the gospel?

**A:** A minister guards the gospel by preserving sound doctrine and living a life that reflects God’s Word. This includes maintaining personal integrity, resisting cultural compromise, and faithfully teaching the truth of Scripture.

### Avoiding Stumbling Blocks in Ministry

#### 1.4.1 — Describe Paul's model of integrity in ministry and identify the rights he set aside for the sake of the gospel.

God calls ministers to live in a way that supports the gospel and avoids creating obstacles for others. This lesson explores the importance of removing stumbling blocks through ethical conduct, personal sacrifice, and cultural sensitivity.

Paul demonstrated great care in ensuring that neither his actions nor his ministry caused others to stumble. He wrote, “We put no stumbling block in anyone’s path, so that our ministry will not be discredited” (2 Corinthians 6:3).

Ministers must avoid placing hindrances that would block other people from receiving the message of salvation. One of the most harmful stumbling blocks is a lifestyle that does not reflect high ethical standards. Some of the behaviors tackled in this course can be clearly identified as right or wrong. However, many situations that occur within ministry duties require discernment. The minister’s goal must be to choose the highest ethical path available. Reaching the highest ethical path often means making personal sacrifices to protect one’s witness and influence. Personal integrity is essential.

He (Paul) expanded on this principle in his first letter to the Corinthians. In chapter 9, Paul (he) explained how he gave up many of his rights as an apostle to better serve others. He wrote, “To the weak I became weak, to win the weak. I have become all things to all people so that by all possible means I might save some” (1 Corinthians 9:22).

Furthermore, be mindful of cultural or regional behaviors that could cause unnecessary offense. Sensitivity in these areas reflects both wisdom and love.

One story illustrates these ideas well:

A man was preparing to hire a driver for a dangerous mountain road. The road was narrow and wound along the edge of steep cliffs. He asked each candidate, “How close can you drive to the edge and still keep me safe?” The first candidate confidently said he could drive within eighteen inches of the edge. The second said he could manage twelve inches. The third responded differently: “You are asking the wrong question. My goal is to drive as close to the mountain as possible and stay far from the edge.” Unsurprisingly, the third candidate received the job.

The lesson is clear: ministers should not ask, “How much can I get away with and still be ethical?” Instead, they should ask, “What is the wisest and most God-honoring decision I can make in this situation?” That attitude protects God’s message and the people God has called ministers to serve.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister can avoid being a stumbling block to others?

**A:** A minister can avoid being a stumbling block by living with integrity and choosing the highest ethical path. The highest ethical path includes being honest, consistent, and sensitive to cultural and personal boundaries so no one is hindered from receiving the gospel.

### Shepherding the Flock of God

#### 1.5.1 — Summarize Peter's instruction to church leaders and describe the posture of eagerness and servanthood in ministry.

The heart of ministerial work is people. In his first epistle, the apostle Peter gives a command to the church leaders. This lesson explores Peter’s instruction to shepherd the with humility, willingness, and godly motivation.

Peter wrote, “To the elders among you, I appeal as a fellow elder and a witness of Christ’s sufferings who also will share in the glory to be revealed: Be shepherds of God’s flock that is under your care, watching over them—not because you must, but because you are willing, as God wants you to be; not pursuing dishonest gain, but eager to serve; not lording it over those entrusted to you, but being examples to the flock. And when the Chief Shepherd appears, you will receive the crown of glory that will never fade away” (1 Peter 5:1–4).

Peter’s words, written nearly two thousand years ago, remain highly relevant. First, it is important to understand the language used. Sometimes, people think of an elder in terms of age. However, in the early church, terms like elder , presbyter , and bishop were used interchangeably to describe those serving as pastors or overseers in local congregations ( Horton 1986, 309 ). Today, this instruction applies to a wide range of ministerial positions—like pastors, staff pastors, educators, chaplains, and other spiritual leaders or guides.

Peter’s first instruction is to shepherd the flock of God. Ministry is always about people. Although some jokingly say, “Ministry would be great if it were not for the people,” this kind of statement is misguided. True shepherding involves leading, feeding, caring for, and equipping people for ministry. What makes this role even more serious is the realization that we are not shepherding our own flock—we are shepherding God’s flock. He has entrusted His people to ministerial care.

Additionally, Peter addresses the attitude of the shepherd. Ministry should be done willingly, not under compulsion. When God calls someone to serve, the response should be joyful and wholehearted. Ministers must remember the privilege of serving the Lord. When Susan and I received our first ministry assignment as youth pastors in Sitka, Alaska, we felt such joy and gratitude for the opportunity to serve. The joy of serving the Lord willingly has never faded even though our roles have changed over the years.

Next, Peter emphasizes that ministers’ motivations should not be for shameful gain. It is biblical for ministers to receive financial support for their labor, as “the worker deserves his wages” (Luke 10:7). It is right to earn a living and provide for one’s family. Churches and ministries should do their best to offer generous compensation when possible. However, the decision to become a minister should not be driven by financial opportunity. Ministry is not a career ladder. It is a call from God. When we are obedient to follow the path God sets before us, He will provide what we need. Peter reminds us that the true reward is eternal: “The crown of glory that will never fade away” (1 Peter 5:4).

Peter concludes with a vital principle: how we lead matters. Leaders must not dominate or control those under their care. Instead, they are called to be examples. Leadership is not about asserting authority or claiming more rights. It is about setting a standard others can follow. A faithful leader says, as Paul did, “Follow my example, as I follow the example of Christ” (1 Corinthians 11:1).

**Q:** Describe your motivation for ministry considering 1 Peter 5.

**A:** Answers will vary. Ministers should be motivated by a desire to serve the Lord and care for His people. Ministry is not about personal gain or power but about obedience, love for others, and faithfulness to God’s call.

### Qualifications for Ministry Leadership

#### 1.6.1 — Explain the qualifications for ministry found in the Pastoral Epistles, including the importance of temperament, emotional health, and spiritual maturity.

Ministers are called to lives of integrity and example. In his letters to Timothy and Titus, the apostle Paul outlines the qualifications for those who serve as overseers in the church. These standards, rooted in godly character and spiritual maturity, remain essential for those seeking to lead today.

Paul provided a detailed list of qualifications for overseers in 1 Timothy 3:1–7 and Titus 1:7–9. As discussed earlier, Paul appears to use the terms overseer ( episkopos ) and elder ( presbuteros ) interchangeably. These terms describe someone who functions as a pastor or ministry leader. Within the Assemblies of God, these passages are referenced when evaluating candidates for ordination. Any study of ministerial ethics would be incomplete without reviewing these qualifications.

Paul begins by emphasizing the significance of the calling: “Here is a trustworthy saying: Whoever aspires to be an overseer desires a noble task” (1 Timothy 3:1). The call to vocational ministry is noble and should be honored, as in the early church, “The harvest is plentiful but the workers are few” (Matthew 9:37). When God calls someone into ministry, it is our responsibility to encourage that calling and nurture it.

Paul then outlines the character requirements of a minister. He writes, “Now the overseer is to be above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2). Most scholars agree that Paul’s statement means the minister should live in such a way that accusations cannot be easily brought against them from inside or outside the church. The minister’s life must be marked by integrity.

One qualification is that a minister must be “faithful to his wife” (1 Timothy 3:2), or literally, a one-woman man. However, the minister can choose to be unmarried, as Paul and likely Timothy were. Additionally, this does not exclude women from ministry, as affirmed by the Assemblies of God. (For more information, see the position paper “Women in Ministerial Leadership” at https://ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Women-in-Ministerial-Leadership.) “The General Council of the Assemblies of God has adopted the interpretation that the ‘one-woman man’ restriction applies to individuals in a heterosexual, monogamous marriage for which divorce is not an option except under specific conditions. If those conditions do exist, remarriage becomes allowable for divorced ministers” (https://www.ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Divorce-and-Remarriage). If married, the minister must be fully devoted to his or her spouse.

Paul continues with qualifications such as being “temperate, self-controlled, respectable, hospitable, able to teach” (1 Timothy 3:2). These traits reflect a person who is not governed by selfish desires but who models Christlike character. Hospitality was especially important in the early church because leaders often welcomed traveling ministers into their homes. Even today, ministers are called to live generously and attentively to the needs of others.

The “able to teach” phrase is the only qualification on Paul’s list that indicates a duty to be performed. Paul expands on this in his letter to Titus: “He must hold firmly to the trustworthy message as it has been taught, so that he can encourage others by sound doctrine and refute those who oppose it” (Titus 1:9). Teaching sound doctrine and correcting error are essential responsibilities. Ministers must be lifelong learners who continually deepen their understanding of Scripture. In the Advanced Internship course, students are asked to develop a personal plan for continued learning and growth in ministry.

Paul adds to his list of qualifications for ministers: “Not given to drunkenness” (1 Timothy 3:3). While this may not prohibit all use of alcohol, Timothy likely chose full abstinence due to the context in Ephesus. Today, ministers in the Assemblies of God abstain from alcohol. The Fellowship’s position paper on this subject affirms that abstinence allows ministers to set a clear example and confront social problems caused by alcohol abuse. As stated in the position paper: “The long-standing Assemblies of God position on abstinence should be reaffirmed. . . to faithfully witness to each generation and to confront unjust and destructive social ills that harm people whom God loves. By any measure, the use and abuse of alcohol continues to take an enormous toll on people, and entire societies, around the world” (https://www.ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Abstinence-from-Alcohol).

Paul continues that a minister must “not [be] violent but gentle, not quarrelsome” (1 Timothy 3:3). Leadership in the church requires emotional and relational maturity. Ministry regularly involves conflict and problem-solving. If a pastor is hot-tempered or argumentative, the situation worsens. For this reason, God excludes such individuals from spiritual leadership.

Another key ministerial qualification is not being a lover of money (1 Timothy 3:3). Motivation for ministry matters. Paul warns later in the same letter about false teachers who viewed godliness as a means of financial gain (1 Timothy 6:5–10). The desire for wealth led the false teachers to their ruin. This is why Paul includes warnings about greed in both Timothy and Titus, as well as in his list of qualifications for deacons.

Paul concludes with two final cautions: “He must not be a recent convert, or he may become conceited and fall under the same judgment as the devil. He must also have a good reputation with outsiders, so that he will not fall into disgrace and into the devil’s trap” (1 Timothy 3:6–7). Later, Paul writes, “Do not be hasty in the laying on of hands” (1 Timothy 5:22). The Assemblies of God reflects this principle by requiring ministers to be licensed and in ministry for two years before becoming ordained. The allotted time allows for proven faithfulness and maturity and minimizes the mistake of ordaining someone too quickly.

God continues to call men and women to the noble task of ministry. My hope is that more ministers continue to help gather the end-time harvest. The ethical demands and qualifications for ministry found in Scripture remain just as true and relevant today as they were in Timothy’s time. However, while the calling has not changed, the challenges have. My hope is that this course will serve as a navigational chart for ministers in the twenty-first century—helping them avoid being shipwrecked by sin and unethical behavior and instead remain faithful to biblical standards. Ministers must continue to guard the deposit that has been entrusted to them as they watch their lives and biblical doctrine closely.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister can “fan into flame the gift of God” (2 Timothy 1:6)?

**A:** Ministers can fan into flame the gift of God by practicing spiritual disciplines such as prayer, Bible study, and fasting. They can also remain active in ministry, seek out mentors, and place themselves in environments that encourage spiritual growth and boldness.

**Q:** Why does Paul encourage: “Do not be hasty in the laying on of hands” (1 Timothy 5:22)?

**A:** Paul cautions against placing someone into ministry leadership too quickly. A new convert or untested leader may lack the maturity and character needed for sustained ministry. Time allows for spiritual growth, proven faithfulness, and a reputation that reflects readiness to lead.

## 2

### Different Types of Ethical Issues

#### 2.1.1 — Explain why developing transferable ethical decision-making skills is essential for remaining ethical in a changing world.

Ministers face a wide variety of ethical challenges. Some are clearly defined in Scripture while others require prayerful discernment. In this lesson, we will explore the kinds of ethical decisions ministers face and how certain boundaries can serve as safeguards in ministerial occupations.

We live in a fast-paced and constantly changing world. Thirty years ago, few people could have predicted the dangers that would emerge from internet pornography, online gambling, or the influence of artificial intelligence. Even after this course is completed, new ethical challenges will continue to appear. Understanding specific ethical concerns and learning how to make wise, biblical decisions is essential for ministers.

Retired Illinois District Superintendent and General Council Executive Presbyter Larry Griswold uses the terms guardrails and speed bumps to describe different types of ethical issues.

Guardrails are designed to protect the driver—in this case, the minister—from veering off the path and ending up in dangerous territory. Even if a decision does not lead to an immediate negative outcome, that does not mean the choice was right. Many of the ethical boundaries covered in this course are designed to teach ministers how to live with integrity and finish well in their calling.

Speed bumps, in contrast, are meant to protect others because they slow a vehicle down to prevent accidents in areas where people are nearby. Likewise, some ethical boundaries may seem to slow ministry progress, but they protect those who serve—especially the church. For example, if a minister engages in spiritual or sexual abuse, the harm to others is often deep and long-lasting. Ethical boundaries exist for personal holiness and to protect the people under a minister’s care.

This distinction helps explain why ethical behavior is not only about avoiding personal sin. It is about creating a safe and healthy environment for others. Similar to how a lighthouse warns ships of danger, ethical standards guide ministers and protect both the leader and everyone on board.

**Q:** What are some guardrails you have established in your life to protect yourself from engaging in sinful behavior?

**A:** Answers will vary but should include the Bible, the Holy Spirit, and other believers.

#### 2.1.2 — Identify the role of spiritual leadership (e.g., district or network leaders) in guiding ethical decision-making for ministers.

Ministers face a wide variety of ethical challenges. Some are clearly defined in Scripture while others require prayerful discernment. In this lesson, we will explore the kinds of ethical decisions ministers face and how certain boundaries can serve as safeguards in ministerial occupations.

We live in a fast-paced and constantly changing world. Even after this course is completed, new ethical challenges will continue to appear. Understanding specific ethical concerns and learning how to make wise, biblical decisions is essential for ministers.

### A Process for Making Ethical Decisions

#### 2.2.1 — Identify what must guide the ethical decision-making process (prayer and dependence on God).

Ministers make decisions every day. Some decisions are practical, and others carry significant ethical weight. In this lesson, we will walk through a step-by-step process for making ethical decisions, adapted from the Northwest Ministry Network’s Ethical Foundations for Church Leaders . This model helps ministers respond wisely to both clear moral issues and complex situations that arise in ministry.

Dr. Don Detrick and Mark Putnam developed an excellent decision-making model in the Northwest Ministry Network’s manual titled Ethical Foundations for Church Leaders: NWMN New Minister’s Ethics Manual . With their permission, this course adapts their process for a broader ministerial context. Their approach provides practical guidance not only for avoiding sin but also for making wise, healthy, and godly decisions. Pastors and ministry leaders must regularly make decisions that affect not only their lives, but those of their congregations and their communities—many of which involve ethical considerations.

The ethical decision-making process must be bathed in prayer. Wisdom and guidance come from the Holy Spirit who leads believers into truth. James writes, “If any of you lacks wisdom, you should ask God, who gives generously to all without finding fault, and it will be given to you” (James 1:5). Jesus promised, “When he, the Spirit of truth, comes, he will guide you into all the truth” (John 16:13). Prayer aligns our hearts with God’s purposes and sharpens our discernment.

Case Study

To help apply the decision-making model, a brief case study is provided. While the correct ethical response may seem obvious, walking through each step will help develop the skills necessary for applying the process to more complex situations.

Julio and Maria had been married for six years and had two children. Julio served as the lead pastor of Delta Assembly of God in Dallas, Texas. Recently, he noticed that Maria seemed distant—both emotionally and physically. At the same time, Julio’s church secretary, Carmen, was experiencing similar struggles in her own marriage. As Julio and Carmen began sharing their frustrations, they developed a deeper emotional connection with each other. Julio felt a renewed sense of connection and began to imagine what life might be like if he were married to Carmen. They started exchanging notes, and Julio realized he was standing at a crossroads. He had a decision to make: Should he continue down this path with Carmen?

Step 1: Clarify the Situation

The first step in making an ethical decision is to clarify the situation. Ministers must begin by gathering the facts because many ethical dilemmas are intensified by misunderstandings or assumptions that go unchallenged. Ministers must separate emotion from reality.

In the case study, Julio should speak openly with his wife and try to understand the reasons behind her emotional distance. There may be unmet needs, unspoken hurts, or stressors he has not recognized. Honest communication could help resolve the problem before it worsens.

Consider another example. A senior pastor hears a rumor that one of his staff pastors spoke negatively about him to others in the church. The senior pastor becomes angry and considers firing the staff member. However, before acting, he should allow time for his emotions to settle and clarify the situation. He should speak directly with the accused to understand exactly what was said and in what context. The entire conflict may be the result of miscommunication. Scripture reminds us that “human anger does not produce the righteousness that God desires” (James 1:20).

Secondly, clarifying the situation involves identifying stakeholders—the people, groups, and entities affected by the decision. Ministers must look beyond themselves and consider who else might be impacted.

In Julio’s case, the list of stakeholders is long. His wife and children would be deeply affected. Carmen’s family would be affected as well. The church congregation would be shocked, and some members might leave or lose their faith in the process. The incident would damage Julio’s reputation, the church’s witness, and possibly the reputation of the broader ministry network. Sin has ripple effects. In moments of temptation, people often fail to consider the long-term consequences for others.

Step 2: Understand the Values and Priorities

Next, ministers need to identify the values and principles at stake. First, ask: What personal values may be influencing this decision? Second, ask: What biblical, moral, or theological principles apply?

It is also helpful to think in terms of God’s purposes for ministry: worship, discipleship, fellowship, service, and evangelism. Ask how each of these might be affected by the decision being made.

In the case study, several values and principles are clear. The first is faithfulness—to God, to one’s spouse, and to the church. If Julio decides to have an affair, he will violate his marriage covenant and harm his family. A second value is related to ministerial responsibility. As a pastor, Julio is called to be a shepherd and an example to the flock (1 Peter 5:2). Pursuing a romantic relationship with a member of the church violates that trust and would represent a clear abuse of his position.

These decisions are not merely about personal happiness. They are about representing Christ, serving others, and living a life worthy of the calling.

Step 3: Know the Rules

Knowing where to find authoritative guidance is essential in any ethical situation. Just as a ship’s captain relies on nautical charts and lighthouses, ministers must rely on trustworthy sources to navigate difficult decisions.

1. The Bible

The Bible is the highest authority for Christian ethics. When Scripture gives a clear command, the issue is settled. In the case study, the Bible clearly teaches that adultery is a sin. God will never guide someone to violate His Word. At times, people claim a “special word” from God to justify sinful behavior. For example, Julio might say that God wants him to be happy. However, God is not double-minded and never contradicts Himself.

Even when Scripture does not name a modern issue—such as marijuana use—it provides principles like sobriety, holiness, and self-control. These principles guide our decisions.

2. Fellowship Documents

For Assemblies of God ministers, another authority to consult is the Constitution and Bylaws of the General Council and applicable district or network bylaws. Credential holders agree to abide by these documents when they are credentialed and when they renew each year.

The General Council documents contain doctrinal and ethical standards, some of which are based directly on Scripture, while others reflect our Fellowship’s agreed application of biblical principles. Teaching contrary doctrine or engaging in disapproved conduct is an ethical violation.

Assemblies of God position papers also provide valuable ethical guidance. These documents are often underused but provide thoughtful, Scripture-based insight into relevant issues. Being aware of these papers can be instructive in navigating difficult ethical dilemmas of our day. They are available online at https://www.ag.org/beliefs/position-papers, and selected papers appear in Appendix A of this course.

3. Governmental Law

In addition to the Scriptures and our Fellowship documents, governmental laws should be followed, including local, state, and federal. Paul wrote, “Let everyone be subject to the governing authorities, for there is no authority except that which God has established” (Romans 13:1).

Practical areas where ministers must follow legal requirements include payroll taxes, nonprofit regulations, zoning, and child safety. A minister who fails to comply with legal obligations can damage the church’s witness and credibility in the community.

Ministers need to keep in mind the order of authoritative sources. The Bible first, Fellowship documents second, and then governmental laws. Ministers must obey civil laws unless they contradict Scripture. In the case study, Julio’s affair would not have broken any governmental law, but it clearly violates Scripture and Fellowship ethics.

As society continues to drift from biblical values, ministers may face increasing tension between obeying God and obeying the government. When laws conflict with the Bible, Christians must follow Scripture. In situations where obeying the law would appear to conflict with the Scriptures, it is wise to pray and consult with spiritual leaders to ensure a believer has a proper understanding of the situation.

Spiritual Leaders

In addition to the Bible, Fellowship documents, and governmental laws, ministers should also seek guidance from their spiritual leaders. Scripture teaches, “Have confidence in your leaders and submit to their authority, because they keep watch over you as those who must give an account. Do this so that their work will be a joy, not a burden, for that would be of no benefit to you” (Hebrews 13:17).

If you serve on staff at a church or ministry, then your lead pastor or ministry leader functions as your primary spiritual authority. If you serve in a lead role, such as a senior pastor or ministry director, then your district or network leadership provides that oversight. Spiritual leaders play a vital role in offering counsel, encouragement, and accountability.

However, it is important to follow spiritual leaders in the correct order of authority. We follow their guidance only when it aligns with the Bible, the Fellowship’s Constitution and Bylaws, and applicable laws. When spiritual advice contradicts any of these higher authorities, ministers must choose to follow God’s truth first.

When facing ethical decisions, especially those not directly addressed in this course, it is essential to know where to look. Begin with Scripture. Consult the documents of your Fellowship. Obey governmental laws that do not violate biblical commands. Seek wisdom from trusted spiritual leaders. As believers prayerfully engage these sources, the Holy Spirit will provide the guidance needed to live and lead with integrity.

Step 4: Look at the Options

Once you have clarified the situation, identified the values and principles involved, and reviewed the relevant sources of authority, the next step is to consider the available ethical options. In every situation, there may be several courses of action that appear reasonable or justifiable. These can be referred to as Plan A, Plan B, or Plan C.

In some cases—such as the situation in our case study—the right choice is clear. Julio should not continue his relationship with Carmen. To do so would be to violate Scripture, his marriage vows, and his ministerial responsibilities. However, many ethical dilemmas encountered in ministerial positions are not so clear-cut. There may be multiple acceptable paths forward, each with different implications.

When considering your options as a minister, take time to reflect carefully. Avoid rushing the process. Consider the facts, seek the counsel of trusted leaders, and pay attention to what the Holy Spirit is prompting in your heart. It is also wise to examine your own motivations and ask whether your decision is truly based on biblical principles.

Be thorough. Evaluate each option not only for what it will accomplish, but also for how it aligns with Scripture, your calling, and the long-term well-being of others. Do not ignore wise counsel, and do not override your conscience. Often, God will confirm a course of action through peace, Scripture, and th e voice of trusted spiritual leaders.

Step 5: Make a Decision

After evaluating your options, it is time to decide. Your decision should be based on what you have learned through prayer, Scripture, godly counsel, and your understanding of the situation.

Ethical decisions may not always be easy, but they must be made with courage and conviction . Be willing to take responsibility for the outcome. If, over time, you realize that the decision was incorrect, have the humility to acknowledge it and make the necessary course correction.

In our case study, Julio spent time in prayer, studied Scripture, and sought advice from his spiritual leaders. As a result, he recognized that his emotional and relational involvement with Carmen was sinful. He chose to repent before God, confess his actions to his wife and leadership, and resign from his pastoral role. He planned to meet with his district leaders the next day to determine the next steps for his restoration and accountability process .

Making the right decision does not mean avoiding consequences; however, by making the right decision you return to integrity, honor God, and begin the journey toward healing and restoration.

To conclude this chapter, it is helpful to examine some of the key missteps in Pastor Julio’s ethical journey and consider how they might have been avoided. His story reminds ministers that ethical failure often begins long before the moment of public consequence. Pastor Julio likely ignored several guardrails—spiritual boundaries that could have kept him from straying.

One of the most effective guardrails is to hide God’s Word in your heart, grow in the fruit of the Spirit, and learn to recognize the voice of the Holy Spirit. The Spirit likely brought conviction to Julio early on, warning him that he was moving in the wrong direction. As is often attributed to Dr. Richard Dobbins, “I have never seen God expose someone’s sin publicly without first speaking to them privately.” God offers multiple opportunities for repentance before judgment comes.

A crucial turning point occurred when Julio and Carmen began to share personal struggles in their marriages. When Julio confided in Carmen, they formed an emotional bond that crossed a previously healthy boundary. This was the beginning of an emotional affair—a relationship marked by emotional intimacy and connection that mirrors a romantic one. Rather than responding to the Holy Spirit’s warning and fleeing temptation as Joseph did in Genesis, Julio ignored those internal speed bumps and continued down a dangerous path.

His choices suggest that his spiritual health weakened. Although his wife seemed distant, the reasons were unclear. Instead of investing more deeply in his walk with the Lord and his marriage, he sought emotional fulfillment elsewhere. A healthier response would have been renewing his personal spiritual disciplines and inviting his wife to pursue licensed Christian counseling with him.

When ministers ignore ethical speed bumps, the consequences can be devastating. Julio not only violated his marriage covenant, he failed to properly steward the power gap in his professional relationship with Carmen. As her pastor and supervisor, Julio held a position of influence and authority. When such power is misused, it places the other person in a vulnerable position. In some cases, this dynamic may lead to accusations of sexual harassment, especially if the individual feels pressured to engage in behavior to preserve their job or standing. Julio’s actions not only harmed Carmen, but they also exposed the church to potential legal liability.

This situation did not need to reach the point of resignation and disciplinary action. With greater self-awareness and humility, Julio could have corrected his course much earlier. A verse from Proverbs gives wise counsel: “Above all else, guard your heart, for everything you do flows from it” (Proverbs 4:23). Most ethical failures in ministry are not the result of a single, catastrophic choice but an outcome of a series of small compromises.

As the course progresses, prepare to encounter a variety of ethical topics. Keep in mind that one of our greatest safeguards when navigating ethical dilemmas is learning to listen to the voice of the Holy Spirit. He faithfully warns us before we veer off course and calls us back to integrity before damage is done.

**Q:** What are some examples of when it would be helpful to get the facts straight and clarify the situation?

**A:** In situations where there are misunderstandings or not all the facts are known by you. Sometimes we assume we have a clear understanding, but we are missing important information that shapes our perspective.

**Q:** If someone becomes addicted to gambling and loses all their money, what values and principles are being disregarded by the individual?

**A:** One principle is stewardship. God has entrusted us to steward the resources He has given to us. A person who gambles is not being a good steward. Another principle is that of providing for your family. A third principle is that gambling has a disproportionate negative effect on the poor. When you win, someone else loses.

**Q:** Describe some scenarios where, in the future, governmental laws could be opposed to the Bible.

**A:** In many places around the world, it is against the law to share your faith. In the future, laws could arise that require churches to allow same-sex weddings. In these situations, and others like it, our obedience to the Scripture comes ahead of our obedience to the government.

**Q:** What should you do if a spiritual leader asks you to do something contrary to the Bible? (Hint: Walk through the ethical process.)

**A:** First, clarify the situation. Ask questions to make sure you understand correctly. Next, identify the values and principles at play. It is important to respect and obey your leaders, but not at the expense of disobedience to Scripture. This value is important to understand because if you find yourself needing to disobey your leader, it should be done respectfully. Next, study the applicable rules. It may be good to get guidance from another respected leader to make sure your interpretation of the applicable Scripture is accurate. Then, identify the options and make a decision. If the leader is truly asking you to do something contrary to Scripture, you should not follow their instruction. Do so prayerfully, respectfully, and with the input of other leaders.

**Q:** Explain the role of the Holy Spirit in protecting us from sin and unethical behavior.

**A:** The Bible tells us that the Holy Spirit guides us into all truth and convicts us of sin. When we are tempted to sin, the Spirit will speak to us and give guidance. It is vital to train ourselves to listen to the Spirit. UNIT PROGRESS EVALUATION 1 Now that you have finished Unit 1, review the lessons in preparation for Unit Progress Evaluation 1. #### 2.2.2 — Describe the steps of the ethical decision-making process (Clarify, Understand, Know the Rules, Look at Options, Act) and explain each step.

Ministers make decisions every day. Some decisions are practical, and others carry significant ethical weight. In this lesson, we will walk through a step-by-step process for making ethical decisions, adapted from the Northwest Ministry Network’s Ethical Foundations for Church Leaders . This model helps ministers respond wisely to both clear moral issues and complex situations that arise in ministry.

Dr. Don Detrick and Mark Putnam developed an excellent decision-making model in the Northwest Ministry Network’s manual titled Ethical Foundations for Church Leaders: NWMN New Minister’s Ethics Manual . With their permission, this course adapts their process for a broader ministerial context. Their approach provides practical guidance not only for avoiding sin but also for making wise, healthy, and godly decisions. Pastors and ministry leaders must regularly make decisions that affect not only their lives, but those of their congregations and their communities—many of which involve ethical considerations.

The ethical decision-making process must be bathed in prayer. Wisdom and guidance come from the Holy Spirit who leads believers into truth. James writes, “If any of you lacks wisdom, you should ask God, who gives generously to all without finding fault, and it will be given to you” (James 1:5). Jesus promised, “When he, the Spirit of truth, comes, he will guide you into all the truth” (John 16:13). Prayer aligns our hearts with God’s purposes and sharpens our discernment.

Case Study

To help apply the decision-making model, a brief case study is provided. While the correct ethical response may seem obvious, walking through each step will help develop the skills necessary for applying the process to more complex situations.

Julio and Maria had been married for six years and had two children. Julio served as the lead pastor of Delta Assembly of God in Dallas, Texas. Recently, he noticed that Maria seemed distant—both emotionally and physically. At the same time, Julio’s church secretary, Carmen, was experiencing similar struggles in her own marriage. As Julio and Carmen began sharing their frustrations, they developed a deeper emotional connection with each other. Julio felt a renewed sense of connection and began to imagine what life might be like if he were married to Carmen. They started exchanging notes, and Julio realized he was standing at a crossroads. He had a decision to make: Should he continue down this path with Carmen?

Step 1: Clarify the Situation

The first step in making an ethical decision is to clarify the situation. Ministers must begin by gathering the facts because many ethical dilemmas are intensified by misunderstandings or assumptions that go unchallenged. Ministers must separate emotion from reality.

In the case study, Julio should speak openly with his wife and try to understand the reasons behind her emotional distance. There may be unmet needs, unspoken hurts, or stressors he has not recognized. Honest communication could help resolve the problem before it worsens.

Consider another example. A senior pastor hears a rumor that one of his staff pastors spoke negatively about him to others in the church. The senior pastor becomes angry and considers firing the staff member. However, before acting, he should allow time for his emotions to settle and clarify the situation. He should speak directly with the accused to understand exactly what was said and in what context. The entire conflict may be the result of miscommunication. Scripture reminds us that “human anger does not produce the righteousness that God desires” (James 1:20).

Secondly, clarifying the situation involves identifying stakeholders—the people, groups, and entities affected by the decision. Ministers must look beyond themselves and consider who else might be impacted.

In Julio’s case, the list of stakeholders is long. His wife and children would be deeply affected. Carmen’s family would be affected as well. The church congregation would be shocked, and some members might leave or lose their faith in the process. The incident would damage Julio’s reputation, the church’s witness, and possibly the reputation of the broader ministry network. Sin has ripple effects. In moments of temptation, people often fail to consider the long-term consequences for others.

Step 2: Understand the Values and Priorities

Next, ministers need to identify the values and principles at stake. First, ask: What personal values may be influencing this decision? Second, ask: What biblical, moral, or theological principles apply?

It is also helpful to think in terms of God’s purposes for ministry: worship, discipleship, fellowship, service, and evangelism. Ask how each of these might be affected by the decision being made.

In the case study, several values and principles are clear. The first is faithfulness—to God, to one’s spouse, and to the church. If Julio decides to have an affair, he will violate his marriage covenant and harm his family. A second value is related to ministerial responsibility. As a pastor, Julio is called to be a shepherd and an example to the flock (1 Peter 5:2). Pursuing a romantic relationship with a member of the church violates that trust and would represent a clear abuse of his position.

These decisions are not merely about personal happiness. They are about representing Christ, serving others, and living a life worthy of the calling.

Step 3: Know the Rules

Knowing where to find authoritative guidance is essential in any ethical situation. Just as a ship’s captain relies on nautical charts and lighthouses, ministers must rely on trustworthy sources to navigate difficult decisions.

1. The Bible

The Bible is the highest authority for Christian ethics. When Scripture gives a clear command, the issue is settled. In the case study, the Bible clearly teaches that adultery is a sin. God will never guide someone to violate His Word. At times, people claim a “special word” from God to justify sinful behavior. For example, Julio might say that God wants him to be happy. However, God is not double-minded and never contradicts Himself.

Even when Scripture does not name a modern issue—such as marijuana use—it provides principles like sobriety, holiness, and self-control. These principles guide our decisions.

2. Fellowship Documents

For Assemblies of God ministers, another authority to consult is the Constitution and Bylaws of the General Council and applicable district or network bylaws. Credential holders agree to abide by these documents when they are credentialed and when they renew each year.

The General Council documents contain doctrinal and ethical standards, some of which are based directly on Scripture, while others reflect our Fellowship’s agreed application of biblical principles. Teaching contrary doctrine or engaging in disapproved conduct is an ethical violation.

Assemblies of God position papers also provide valuable ethical guidance. These documents are often underused but provide thoughtful, Scripture-based insight into relevant issues. Being aware of these papers can be instructive in navigating difficult ethical dilemmas of our day. They are available online at https://www.ag.org/beliefs/position-papers, and selected papers appear in Appendix A of this course.

3. Governmental Law

In addition to the Scriptures and our Fellowship documents, governmental laws should be followed, including local, state, and federal. Paul wrote, “Let everyone be subject to the governing authorities, for there is no authority except that which God has established” (Romans 13:1).

Practical areas where ministers must follow legal requirements include payroll taxes, nonprofit regulations, zoning, and child safety. A minister who fails to comply with legal obligations can damage the church’s witness and credibility in the community.

Ministers need to keep in mind the order of authoritative sources. The Bible first, Fellowship documents second, and then governmental laws. Ministers must obey civil laws unless they contradict Scripture. In the case study, Julio’s affair would not have broken any governmental law, but it clearly violates Scripture and Fellowship ethics.

As society continues to drift from biblical values, ministers may face increasing tension between obeying God and obeying the government. When laws conflict with the Bible, Christians must follow Scripture. In situations where obeying the law would appear to conflict with the Scriptures, it is wise to pray and consult with spiritual leaders to ensure a believer has a proper understanding of the situation.

Spiritual Leaders

In addition to the Bible, Fellowship documents, and governmental laws, ministers should also seek guidance from their spiritual leaders. Scripture teaches, “Have confidence in your leaders and submit to their authority, because they keep watch over you as those who must give an account. Do this so that their work will be a joy, not a burden, for that would be of no benefit to you” (Hebrews 13:17).

If you serve on staff at a church or ministry, then your lead pastor or ministry leader functions as your primary spiritual authority. If you serve in a lead role, such as a senior pastor or ministry director, then your district or network leadership provides that oversight. Spiritual leaders play a vital role in offering counsel, encouragement, and accountability.

However, it is important to follow spiritual leaders in the correct order of authority. We follow their guidance only when it aligns with the Bible, the Fellowship’s Constitution and Bylaws, and applicable laws. When spiritual advice contradicts any of these higher authorities, ministers must choose to follow God’s truth first.

When facing ethical decisions, especially those not directly addressed in this course, it is essential to know where to look. Begin with Scripture. Consult the documents of your Fellowship. Obey governmental laws that do not violate biblical commands. Seek wisdom from trusted spiritual leaders. As believers prayerfully engage these sources, the Holy Spirit will provide the guidance needed to live and lead with integrity.

Step 4: Look at the Options

Once you have clarified the situation, identified the values and principles involved, and reviewed the relevant sources of authority, the next step is to consider the available ethical options. In every situation, there may be several courses of action that appear reasonable or justifiable. These can be referred to as Plan A, Plan B, or Plan C.

In some cases—such as the situation in our case study—the right choice is clear. Julio should not continue his relationship with Carmen. To do so would be to violate Scripture, his marriage vows, and his ministerial responsibilities. However, many ethical dilemmas encountered in ministerial positions are not so clear-cut. There may be multiple acceptable paths forward, each with different implications.

When considering your options as a minister, take time to reflect carefully. Avoid rushing the process. Consider the facts, seek the counsel of trusted leaders, and pay attention to what the Holy Spirit is prompting in your heart. It is also wise to examine your own motivations and ask whether your decision is truly based on biblical principles.

Be thorough. Evaluate each option not only for what it will accomplish, but also for how it aligns with Scripture, your calling, and the long-term well-being of others. Do not ignore wise counsel, and do not override your conscience. Often, God will confirm a course of action through peace, Scripture, and th e voice of trusted spiritual leaders.

Step 5: Make a Decision

After evaluating your options, it is time to decide. Your decision should be based on what you have learned through prayer, Scripture, godly counsel, and your understanding of the situation.

Ethical decisions may not always be easy, but they must be made with courage and conviction . Be willing to take responsibility for the outcome. If, over time, you realize that the decision was incorrect, have the humility to acknowledge it and make the necessary course correction.

In our case study, Julio spent time in prayer, studied Scripture, and sought advice from his spiritual leaders. As a result, he recognized that his emotional and relational involvement with Carmen was sinful. He chose to repent before God, confess his actions to his wife and leadership, and resign from his pastoral role. He planned to meet with his district leaders the next day to determine the next steps for his restoration and accountability process .
