## 3

### Keeping Your Spiritual Tank Filled

#### 3.1.1 — Identify the importance of community, Sabbath, and spiritual disciplines in maintaining a minister's overall health.

One of the greatest dangers a minister faces is spiritual neglect. Just as a car cannot run without fuel, a ministry leader cannot sustain faithful service without a full spiritual tank. In the pressure of ministry, it is easy to allow urgent needs to crowd out time with God. However, the personal spiritual life of a minister is the foundation of his or her public effectiveness and integrity. When that foundation is ignored, burnout and moral compromise are more likely.

Jesus modeled the importance of staying spiritually full. He often withdrew from the crowds to pray and spend time with the Father. Paul demonstrated the same focus when he wrote, “I consider everything a loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord” (Philippians 3:8). For both Jesus and Paul, a vibrant relationship with God was not optional—it was essential.

As ministers, our influence flows from our connection to Christ. Jesus said, “Remain in me, as I also remain in you. No branch can bear fruit by itself; it must remain in the vine” (John 15:4). Ministry apart from abiding in Christ leads to exhaustion, emptiness, and eventually, to ethical failure. Leaders who fail spiritually often begin by neglecting personal time with God.

The Power of Scripture in the Minister’s Life

Spiritual health begins with Scripture. Ministers must read the Bible, not just for sermon preparation but for personal transformation. Rev. Owen C. Carr, a 102-year-old ordained minister, has maintained a daily habit of reading the Bible for an hour each morning. His long-term faithfulness reminds us of the life-giving nature of God’s Word.

Although “The Scriptures Inspired” has long been one of our Fundamental Truths, the Assemblies of God has recently placed renewed emphasis on the importance of Bible engagement. In a study commissioned by Barna, researchers found that 68 percent of people who regularly engage with the Bible reported experiencing joy even in hard times. Only 16 percent of those with low Bible engagement reported the same. The study also found that 78 percent of those who consistently read Scripture felt confident sharing their faith with others, compared to just 16 percent among those with low engagement (Barna Group 2017a).

As ministers, we receive great spiritual strength by faithfully reading Scripture. A consistent, systematic reading plan—such as one that takes you through the Bible in a year—can help establish this important habit. God continues to speak through His Word, not only to inform our sermons but to shape our hearts. We must read the Bible to encounter the living God, to hear His voice, and to strengthen our spirits. When Jesus was tempted, He responded by quoting Scripture each time. If we do not know God’s Word, we will not have that same spiritual resource in moments of testing. The Psalmist declared, “I have hidden your word in my heart that I might not sin against you” (Psalm 119:11). Immersing ourselves in Scripture equips us to walk in obedience and integrity.

Prayer as a Daily Practice

In addition to reading Scripture, ministers must commit to daily prayer. E. M. Bounds spoke plainly when he said, “Every preacher who does not make prayer a mighty factor in his own life and ministry is weak as a factor in God’s work and is powerless to project God’s cause in this world” (Bounds 1907, 13). Through prayer, we encounter the presence of God, and in those moments, His ways begin to shape our own. Jesus, our model for ministry, regularly withdrew from the crowds to pray (Luke 5:16). He considered prayer so essential that He intentionally taught His disciples to pray. I find the Lord’s Prayer to be a helpful structure for personal prayer time.

In Leveling the Praying Field , former General Secretary Donna Barrett compares the discipline of prayer to building a muscle. She writes, “Growing in your prayer life is like developing a muscle. You must be intentional to be effective in praying. Intentional prayer is made with a plan, a place, and a personal style” (2019, 27–28). A vibrant prayer life does not happen automatically. It must be protected, developed, and scheduled. Choose a consistent time each day to be alone with the Lord, and treat that appointment with the same seriousness as any other ministry responsibility.

Prayer not only transforms the minister personally but also becomes a powerful part of his or her public ministry. As the prayer life of a leader grows stronger, it becomes an example others will follow. In his book In Jesus’ Name: 5 Altars of Prayer that Move Heaven and Earth , Assistant General Superintendent Rick DuBose shared how he developed a Saturday night tradition of praying in the sanctuary before Sunday services. He wrote, “I would walk to the church and then pace the empty sanctuary praying for the next day’s service and the men and women who would be in attendance. The truth is, I also prayed for myself. I prayed that God would do in those services what I knew I was not capable of doing by my own talent or ability. I was desperate for God to move, and I was not sure what else to do but pray” (2023, 48–49).

One evening, a man from the church walked in and asked, “What are you doing?” DuBose answered, “Praying.” The man paused and then asked, “May I join you?” Over time, others began to join. What followed was not a program or a formal prayer meeting. It was simply a group of people who consistently gathered to seek God. Eventually, the Saturday night prayer tradition grew to include as many as one hundred people. That space became an altar—a place where the church met with God (2023, 48–49).

Other Spiritual Practices for Health and Growth

Fasting is another important discipline. Taking time to fast—even occasionally—creates space to hear from God more clearly and grow in sensitivity to the Spirit. Use this time to pray and spend quiet moments with God.

Worship music can also lift your spirit and help you focus on God’s presence. Be mindful of the media and voices you allow into your life each day, as they shape your thoughts and attitudes. Make a list of the practices that help you feel close to God, and schedule them regularly.

Spiritual health is strengthened through intentional rhythms. Daily or weekly practices, such as journaling, listening to worship music, and spending time in silence allow the Spirit to speak in fresh ways. These habits do not just happen—they must be pursued and protected.

Spiritual Community and Accountability

Remain connected to other believers and ministry leaders. God never intended for us to serve in isolation. While pastors and ministry leaders often build strong relationships with the people they serve, they may still experience loneliness in leadership. That is why it is important to cultivate friendships with others who are also in ministry. These relationships provide encouragement, shared understanding, and opportunities for growth.

Healthy spiritual friendships strengthen both our personal lives and our ministry. Think of someone who loves basketball. They might study the game and practice on their own, but their skills will improve significantly when they play with others. Ministry is similar. We are meant to walk with others, to worship in community, and to serve as part of the body of Christ. Make space in your life for both private time with God and intentional time with trusted peers.

Spiritual community also offers protection. If a fisherman who falls asleep at the wheel has someone else on board, that person can wake them and help prevent disaster. Likewise, God places us in community not only for encouragement but also for correction when needed. My wife and I often walk along a nearby track. When I glance over at her while talking, I sometimes drift into her lane. She will say, “You are not walking straight.” It reminds me how valuable it is to have someone who loves you enough to point out when you are drifting. In the ministry, we need people who can gently correct us before we cross into dangerous or unethical territory. Spiritual community is essential for keeping our spiritual tanks full and staying on course.

Seven Actions to Keep Your Spiritual Tank Filled

1. Spend daily time in prayer—talk with God regularly, not just about needs, but to build your relationship with Him.

2. Read and reflect on Scripture—let God’s Word renew your mind and guide your decisions.

3. Practice Sabbath rest—set aside regular time for rest, reflection, and worship without ministry pressures.

4. Engage in corporate worship—attend services where you are not leading so you can receive and be refreshed.

5. Stay connected to life-giving community—build honest relationships with people who encourage your faith and hold you accountable.

6. Journal or reflect spiritually—write down prayers, Scriptures, or thoughts that help you recognize God’s work in your life.

7. Participate in spiritual retreats or conferences—step away from daily demands to renew your calling and hear from God in a fresh way.

**Q:** Explain how abiding in the vine can fuel ministry and help prevent unethical behavior.

**A:** Jesus said that apart from Him we can do nothing. Abiding in the vine means staying deeply connected to Christ. This connection fuels our ministry, keeps us sensitive to the Holy Spirit, and gives us strength to resist temptation.

**Q:** What practices can you incorporate into your life to keep your spiritual tank filled?

**A:** Answers may include daily Bible reading, consistent prayer, Sabbath rest, participation in corporate worship, spiritual journaling, fasting, or attending spiritual retreats.

### Keeping Your Physical Tank Filled

#### 3.2.1 — Explain what Paul said to Timothy about physical training and articulate the appropriate balance between spiritual and physical health.

Ministry is a lifelong calling, and physical health plays a key role in how long and how well a leader can fulfill that calling. The demands of serving others, preaching, counseling, and leading require energy, stamina, and resilience. While spiritual health is the highest priority, Scripture also affirms the value of caring for our physical bodies. Just as a car needs regular maintenance to run well, so does the body that carries out God’s work. This lesson focuses on the practical steps ministers can take to keep their physical tank full, helping them serve more effectively and live with strength and balance.

Paul reminded Timothy, “Physical training is of some value, but godliness has value for all things, holding promise for both the present life and the life to come” (1 Timothy 4:8). While Paul clearly prioritized spiritual development, he also acknowledged the worth of caring for one’s body. For ministry leaders, physical health is a matter of stewardship. God has entrusted each of us with one body, and it is the instrument through which we carry out His calling. Taking care of it helps us serve longer and with greater vitality.

Beyond ministry effectiveness, maintaining physical health allows us to enjoy life more fully and to be present with our loved ones for as long as possible. While some health issues are beyond our control, many areas—such as diet, exercise, rest, and routine care—are within our reach and can make a significant difference over time.

Incorporating healthy habits into your daily routine is essential. Find a rhythm that works for your schedule. For example, some leaders prefer to pray in the morning and exercise in the evening. Develop a balanced diet, maintain consistent sleep habits, and create space for rest. These changes are simple in theory but require discipline and intentionality to maintain.

Think of how we care for a vehicle. We know a car needs regular oil changes, tire rotations, and maintenance to run well. If neglected, it will break down early and underperform. The same principle applies to our physical bodies. When we fail to care for ourselves, we shorten our time and reduce our ability to serve well. We also become more vulnerable to stress, poor judgment, and even temptation.

Here are seven actions to help keep your physical tank filled:

1. Get enough sleep—aim for seven to eight hours each night to support focus, mood, and overall health.

2. Eat nutritious meals—fuel your body with balanced, healthy foods that give lasting energy.

3. Stay hydrated—drink plenty of water throughout the day to keep your body functioning well.

4. Exercise regularly—even light activity like walking can boost energy, reduce stress, and improve health.

5. Take breaks and rest—step away from work throughout the day to reset your mind and body.

6. Listen to your body—pay attention to signs of fatigue, stress, or illness, and respond with care.

7. Get regular checkups—routine visits to a doctor or healthcare provider help catch and prevent health issues early.

**Q:** What are some activities you can incorporate into your life to fill your physical tank?

**A:** Examples include getting regular sleep, eating nutritious meals, staying physically active, staying hydrated, and taking time to rest throughout the day.

### Keeping Your Emotional and Mental Health Tank Filled

#### 3.3.1 — Describe how God uses the Sabbath to teach trust and rest, and identify its role in building a sustainable ministry lifestyle.

Ministry can be deeply rewarding, but it can also be emotionally draining. Over time, the pressures of constant responsibility, conflict, and unmet expectations can lead to discouragement, fatigue, and even depression. Emotional and mental exhaustion is one of the leading reasons ministers leave the ministry. In this lesson, we will explore warning signs, biblical insights, and helpful habits for maintaining emotional and mental wellness. By embracing God’s design for rest, reflection, and relationships, ministers can serve from a place of health rather than depletion.

Ministers often experience emotional exhaustion not only from the intensity of their work but from a gradual loss of meaning and motivation. William Willimon uses the term blackout to describe this condition. He writes, “I was never happy with the metaphor of ‘burnout’ . . . Our pastoral problem of constancy is more a matter of ‘blackout’. . . the gradual dissipation of meaning in ministry. . . . We wake up one day and no longer have a reason or purpose for doing the things the church expects us to do” (Trull and Creech 2017, 69). Emotional fatigue can cloud one’s calling and affect personal well-being.

This emotional strain is magnified by the instability of the world around us. Hal Donaldson highlights findings from the American Psychological Association’s Stress in America 2022 report: 76 percent of adults experienced health impacts from stress in the prior month, and 27 percent reported feeling so overwhelmed they could not function most days (Donaldson 2024, 69–70). Ministers are not exempt from this reality. A Barna Group study from 2022 found that 42 percent of pastors had considered leaving full-time ministry in the past year. Among those, 56 percent cited the immense stress of the job as a key reason (Barna Group 2022). These statistics show the urgent need to care for our emotional and mental health.

God gave us a clear model for managing stress and sustaining health: Sabbath rest. After six days of creation, God rested on the seventh—not because He was tired but to set an example for humanity. Rest is not weakness; it is wisdom. The fourth commandment—“Remember the Sabbath day by keeping it holy” (Exodus 20:8)—is positioned alongside other foundational commands like not committing adultery or murder. Jesus emphasized that the Sabbath was created for our benefit, not as a burden (Mark 2:27). In His day, legalism distorted the purpose of Sabbath. In our day, we often neglect it altogether.

Many ministers struggle to take a true day off. The urgency of the ministry makes rest feel irresponsible. However, like tithing, Sabbath rest is a matter of trust and stewardship. Just as giving 10 percent of our income reflects our trust in God’s provision, giving one day each week for rest demonstrates our trust in His ability to sustain us. By honoring the Sabbath, we actually gain strength and productivity for the other six days.

Regular Sabbath rest is one of the most effective tools for maintaining emotional health. Dr. Don Lichi has observed that rest is also a powerful safeguard against addictive behavior (Fleagle and Lichi 2011). Without healthy rest, stress accumulates and often finds destructive outlets. God designed our bodies, minds, and spirits to need downtime. Sabbath is not optional for sustained ministry—it is essential.

Sundays are often the busiest day of the week for ministers, so it may not serve well as a Sabbath. Choose a different day and protect it with intentionality. Say no when needed. On that day, engage in life-giving activities—enjoy God’s presence, spend time with family and friends, and do things that refresh your soul.

Resting well does not mean working less. In the creation account, God worked diligently for six days and then rested. Ministry requires hard work and commitment. A healthy rhythm involves daily rest, weekly Sabbath, and occasional extended breaks, such as vacations. Every person has different needs and energy levels. Ask God to help you find a sustainable pace and pray that He will expand your capacity to lead effectively without sacrificing your health.

One helpful way to care for emotional health is by developing a hobby. Hobbies give your mind a break from ministry and help renew your energy. For example, I personally discovered a passion for nature photography about eight years ago. Living in Alaska, it became a natural fit. I would photograph wildlife from spring to fall and capture the Northern Lights in winter. This activity allowed me to relax, think about something other than work, and recharge. Find a hobby that fits your personality and gives you joy. Just remember to keep it in balance.

Friendships are another key part of emotional health. While most ministry leaders have relationships within their local church or ministry, these connections often involve responsibilities. Ministers also need relationships where there is no expectation to perform or lead. Sadly, many pastors report feelings of loneliness, even while surrounded by people. This should not be the case. Pursue meaningful friendships outside your ministry role. It will require effort, but having a friend is one of the best investments you can make for your well-being.

Beyond friendships, other supportive relationships are also essential. Mentors help guide your personal and spiritual growth. A coach can ask the right questions and challenge you to keep moving forward. In addition, many ministers benefit from working with a professional counselor who can help them process the emotional weight of the ministry. These relationships bring perspective, healing, and accountability. We were not meant to carry the burden of ministry alone.

Seven Actions to Keep Your Emotional and Mental Health Tank Filled

1. Talk to someone you trust—share your thoughts and feelings with a friend, mentor, or counselor to avoid carrying burdens alone.

2. Set healthy boundaries —learn to say no when needed and protect your time and energy.

3. Take time for things you enjoy—make space for hobbies, laughter, and moments that bring you joy.

4. Practice gratitude—regularly reflect on what you’re thankful for to shift your focus from stress to blessing.

5. Limit negative input—be mindful of media, conversations, and environments that drain or discourage you.

6. Be kind to yourself—replace self-criticism with grace; remember that everyone has limits and needs care.

7. Recognize when to get help—seek professional support when you feel overwhelmed, anxious, or stuck.

**Q:** Explain some of the factors that lead to stress in ministry.

**A:** Common stress factors include a demanding schedule, unrealistic expectations, congregational conflict, emotional fatigue, time away from family, and the weight of caring for others’ spiritual and emotional needs.

**Q:** What steps can you take to improve the work-rest balance in your life?

**A:** Steps may include scheduling a weekly Sabbath, planning vacations, creating daily rhythms of rest, setting clear boundaries, and asking God to help establish a sustainable pace for ministry and life.

### Keeping Your Intellectual Tank Filled

#### 3.4.1 — Explain why building a lifestyle of learning is important for ministers and describe how it helps maintain sharpness and readiness.

God has called ministers to serve with both heart and mind. While spiritual passion is essential, intellectual growth enables us to serve with wisdom, clarity, and skill. Ministry leaders who commit to learning are better equipped to teach, lead, and defend the truth. In a world filled with misinformation and shifting values, we must sharpen our thinking to lead others effectively. This lesson will explore why continuing to learn is not just helpful—it is an essential part of ethical, Spirit-empowered ministry.

Albert Einstein once said, “Intellectual growth should commence at birth and cease only at death.” This statement captures an important truth for ministry leaders: if we are not moving forward in knowledge and understanding, we are at risk of falling behind. Stagnation can lead to loss of focus, increased vulnerability to deception, and diminished ministry effectiveness.

Ministers must develop a plan for intellectual growth. This includes deepening your knowledge of the Bible, theology, leadership, and other relevant topics. Growth can happen through formal education or informal means such as reading books, attending conferences, listening to podcasts, or participating in group discussions. Each of these adds tools to your ministry toolbox and helps you serve with confidence and competence. In the Advanced Internship course, you will work with your supervising minister to create a personalized, lifelong learning plan.

Stewardship applies to more than finances. We are called to steward the entirety of the life God has given us—our time, energy, gifts, and mind. Whether you are exercising your body, observing a Sabbath, reading Scripture, or engaging in study, you are honoring God by developing what He has entrusted to you. Paul encouraged Timothy, “Do your best to present yourself to God as one approved, a worker who does not need to be ashamed and who correctly handles the word of truth” (2 Timothy 2:15). Ministers are called to prepare well, not only spiritually, but intellectually.

A lack of preparation in the ministry can become an ethical concern. An incompetent minister may misrepresent Scripture, give poor counsel, or fail to lead effectively. Jesus’ “Parable of the Talents” reminds us that each servant was responsible for using what the master entrusted to them (Matthew 25:14–30). The one who buried his talent was not praised but rebuked for wasting the opportunity. Ministry is a sacred responsibility, and we are accountable for developing the gifts and calling God has placed in our lives.

Commitment to lifelong learning positions us to lead with wisdom and serve with excellence. It honors the call of God and helps us stay mentally sharp, spiritually grounded, and practically prepared.

Seven Actions to Keep Your Intellectual Tank Filled

1. Read regularly—choose books, articles, or journals that challenge your thinking and expand your knowledge.

2. Listen to podcasts or watch educational videos—use commutes or downtime to grow in your understanding of ministry, leadership, or other areas of interest.

3. Attend workshops, conferences, or classes—invest in learning opportunities that sharpen your skills and expose you to new ideas.

4. Engage in thoughtful conversations—discuss current topics or biblical issues with peers to hear different perspectives and refine your thinking.

5. Ask questions and stay curious—don’t settle for surface-level understanding; dig deeper into subjects that matter.

6. Write or reflect on what you’re learning—journaling, blogging, or even teaching others helps solidify what you’ve learned.

7. Set learning goals—plan to learn something new each month or quarter to keep your mind active and growing.

**Q:** In what ways can a minister obey the admonition, “Do your best to present yourself to God as one approved, a worker who does not need to be ashamed and who correctly handles the word of truth” (2 Timothy 2:15)?

**A:** Ministers can study the Word regularly, pursue ongoing learning, remain teachable, seek feedback from mentors, and practice integrity in both message and conduct.

### The Enemy of Our Souls

#### 3.5.1 — Describe the core of building an ethical, intentional lifestyle (staying in tune with the Holy Spirit) and identify the role of spiritual disciplines in achieving spiritual health.

No matter how strong, disciplined, or experienced we are, ministers face a spiritual enemy. Satan’s goal is not simply to discourage but to destroy. He looks for weaknesses and waits for moments when we are vulnerable. That is why a strong personal life—spiritually, emotionally, physically, and intellectually—is not just wise; it is vital for survival. In this final lesson, we will focus on the reality of spiritual warfare and the importance of being alert, equipped, and grounded in Christ. Victory comes not from our own strength but through the power of God and the armor He provides.

One critical element in maintaining a healthy personal life is recognizing the presence of a spiritual enemy. Scripture is clear that Satan actively seeks to destroy the lives and ministries of God’s people. First Peter 5:8 warns us, “Be alert and of sober mind. Your enemy the devil prowls around like a roaring lion looking for someone to devour.” This enemy looks for those who are unguarded—those who have let their spiritual, emotional, physical, or intellectual defenses wear down. When we neglect these areas, we become more susceptible to temptation and deception.

The apostle Paul offers insight into how we can stand firm against these attacks. In his letter to the Ephesians, he writes, “Finally, be strong in the Lord and in his mighty power. Put on the full armor of God, so that you can take your stand against the devil’s schemes” (6:10–11). Paul does not suggest that we can resist the enemy with our own willpower. In fact, the Greek verb for “be strong” is in the passive voice, meaning we are to “be strengthened” by God. We cannot stand on our own. Three times in this passage Paul calls us to “stand”: “stand against,” “stand your ground,” “stand firm”—and we can only do that when we draw strength from the Lord.

Paul then describes the armor that God has provided for our defense. Each piece symbolizes a specific resource or discipline that helps us withstand temptation. These include the belt of truth, the breastplate of righteousness, the readiness of the gospel of peace, the shield of faith, the helmet of salvation, and the sword of the Spirit. This armor is not symbolic only—it must be actively put on and used. Each piece requires intentional action on our part. The armor protects us only when it is worn. Let us be diligent to wear the armor provided, strengthen ourselves in the Lord, and stand firm.

Spiritual warfare is real. Ministry leaders who understand this prepare themselves by walking closely with God and by guarding every area of life. The enemy thrives where there is weakness, distraction, or compromise. We cannot afford to be careless. Instead, we must stay vigilant, equipped, and fully surrendered to the strength God provides.

As you reflect on this chapter, take time to develop a personal action plan. Make it your goal to keep your spiritual, physical, emotional, and intellectual tanks full. Evaluate where you may be running low, and create rhythms that allow you to replenish those areas. Doing so will help you resist temptation and avoid moral failure. While it requires discipline, the long-term rewards of faithfulness far outweigh the cost. Do not fall asleep at the wheel. Stay alert, stay filled, and stand firm.

Case Study Analysis

Eric serves as a clear example of a minister who, through exhaustion and overcommitment, fell asleep at the wheel and ultimately shipwrecked on the rocks of moral failure. His desire to turn the church around was sincere, but the pressure he felt led him to overload his schedule and neglect critical areas of his personal health. As his time with the Lord was crowded out by daily demands, he lost the spiritual vitality and strength needed to sustain his ministry.

In addition to spiritual neglect, Eric ignored his physical and emotional well-being. He was not getting adequate sleep and had abandoned the Sabbath as a regular rhythm of rest. He had no time for life-giving activities, meaningful connection with family and friends, or hobbies that could have refreshed his soul. Over time, his reserves were depleted. In this weakened state, he became more vulnerable to temptation and eventually gave in to gambling as a form of emotional escape.

Eric’s failure might have been avoided had he set realistic boundaries and shared the responsibilities of leadership with others. Though his motives may have been noble, trying to lead every ministry himself was not sustainable or wise. God never intended for pastors to carry the entire burden of ministry alone. Eric needed to protect his prayer life, observe a Sabbath, and tend to the personal areas that keep a minister spiritually and emotionally strong.

It is likely that the Holy Spirit was prompting Eric to make changes along the way. On the Saturday he first gave in to temptation, he was feeling exhausted and spiritually dry. In those moments, the Spirit was undoubtedly speaking. However, when our tanks are empty, we may not hear clearly. Staying filled—spiritually, physically, emotionally, and intellectually—helps us remain sensitive to the voice of the Spirit and positions us to resist temptation when it comes.

**Q:** In what ways will putting on the armor of God protect us from the schemes of the enemy?

**A:** Putting on the armor of God equips us to stand against temptation and spiritual attack. Each piece—truth, righteousness, faith, salvation, the Word, and prayer—provides a defense and enables us to remain strong in spiritual battle. 4 CHAPTER

## 4

### Origins of Power

#### 4.1.1 — Explain what it means to exercise servant leadership modeled after Jesus, and describe how Jesus used His power to serve.

Power is not inherently bad. In fact, all power originates with God and is given for the purpose of blessing others. However, when power is misunderstood or misused, it can become a tool for harm rather than good. In ministry, understanding the origin and purpose of power is essential to using it in a way that honors God and serves people. This lesson will explore what Scripture teaches about the source of power, how God delegates it, and how leaders are called to use it with humility and responsibility.

All true power and authority come from God. Diane Langberg, in Redeeming Power: Understanding Authority and Abuse in the Church , highlights two key Scriptures that shape a biblical understanding of power. In Matthew 28:18–19, Jesus declares, “All authority in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Therefore go.” This statement affirms that authority originates with God. Any power we have as humans is not our own—it is a shared authority, granted by God for His purposes.

Jesus modeled this perfectly. He said, “The Son can do nothing by himself; he can do only what he sees his Father doing” (John 5:19). Langberg reflects on this by saying, “The state of heart manifested by the Son of the Father should abound in those of us who follow Him. We taught our own teachings, our own writings, our own organizations, and our own reputations. But Jesus did nothing of the sort. We seek a share of glory and power for ourselves. He humbled himself before both God and humans and became a servant. We seek to build our own little kingdoms. He came to build the Father’s kingdom. God gives power to us as His creatures to be held in trust. Its purpose is to bless” (Langberg 2020, 10–11).

God delegates power to people in various ways. One of the first examples is found in Genesis, where God gives humanity dominion over creation “so that they may rule over the fish in the sea and the birds in the sky, over the livestock and all the wild animals, and over all the creatures that move along the ground” (Genesis 1:26). This assignment is not a license for domination but a charge to steward God’s world responsibly.

God also delegates authority within civil government. Paul writes, “Let everyone be subject to the governing authorities, for there is no authority except that which God has established. The authorities that exist have been established by God” (Romans 13:1). Earthly authority structures, when operating correctly, are meant to promote justice and order for the good of society.

In addition to creation and government, God entrusts authority to spiritual leaders. Hebrews 13:17 says, “Have confidence in your leaders and submit to their authority, because they keep watch over you as those who must give an account. Do this so that their work will be a joy, not a burden, for that would be of no benefit to you.” Spiritual authority is a sacred trust. Leaders are not given power to control or elevate themselves but to care for people’s souls and help them grow in faith.

Every position of authority—whether in creation, society, or the church—comes with the opportunity to bless or to abuse. Before examining what spiritual abuse looks like, it is important to first understand what healthy leadership and proper use of authority should be. The next lesson will focus on these traits and how they reflect the character of Christ.

**Q:** What are some of the ways humans use power for good purposes?

**A:** Humans use God-given power appropriately when they steward the earth responsibly, serve others through wise leadership in government and society, and lead within the church for the spiritual benefit of others.

### Good Leadership

#### 4.2.1 — Identify the ethical implications of empowering others in ministry leadership.

Leadership is a powerful gift, and when used well, it reflects the character of Christ. In contrast to abusive or self-serving leadership, godly leaders use their influence to serve, encourage, and uplift others. Jesus set the standard for this kind of leadership by redefining greatness—not as control or dominance but as service. Before identifying the warning signs of spiritual abuse, it is essential to understand what healthy leadership looks like. This lesson will focus on the qualities of Christlike leaders who steward their authority with humility, service, and accountability.

George Paul Wood recently interviewed Dr. James Bradford, pastor of Central Assembly of God in Springfield, Missouri, and former General Secretary of the Assemblies of God, on the Influence Podcast. The topic was spiritual abuse, and during the conversation, Dr. Bradford shared a powerful picture of what good spiritual leadership looks like (Wood 2024).

Dr. Bradford began with the words of Jesus: “The kings of the Gentiles lord it over them; and those who exercise authority over them call themselves Benefactors. But you are not to be like that. Instead, the greatest among you should be like the youngest, and the one who rules like the one who serves” (Luke 22:25–26). Jesus redefined greatness in leadership. Instead of exercising power for personal gain, leaders are called to serve others.

Based on this passage, Dr. Bradford identified several characteristics of godly leaders. First, godly leaders are not controlling. They use their authority to bring out the best in the people they serve. They do not coerce, manipulate, or dominate. Instead, they empower others to grow and flourish.

Second, godly leaders are not self-centered. Their focus is not on how others can serve them but on how they can serve others. Ministry is not about building a platform or personal brand—it is about humility, sacrifice, and lifting others up.

Finally, godly leaders embrace accountability. They welcome feedback and recognize that they are stewards, not owners, of their leadership role. True spiritual leaders understand that authority comes with responsibility and that they will give an account of how they have treated others.

**Q:** In what ways have good leaders had an impact on you, and which of these traits have you observed in them?

**A:** Answers will vary. Possible observations may include humility, service, encouragement, integrity, and accountability.

### Spiritually Abusive Practices

#### 4.3.1 — Describe the role of team dynamics and collaboration in ethical ministry leadership.

While spiritual leadership is meant to reflect Christ’s humility and care, it can be distorted when power is misused. Spiritual abuse occurs when leaders manipulate, control, or dominate those under their care. These practices betray the trust of followers and damage the body of Christ. As ministry leaders, it is important not only to recognize abusive behaviors but also to actively avoid them and establish healthy patterns of accountability and trust. This lesson highlights several spiritually abusive practices that must be rejected and replaced with Christlike leadership.

We now turn our attention to spiritually abusive behaviors that should be avoided. This section draws from not only the General Presbytery’s newly adopted position paper, “Misuse of Spiritual Leadership” (see Appendix A), but also from the research of Dr. Allen Tennison, Theological Counsel for the General Council of the Assemblies of God. With his permission, the following insights are paraphrased from his unpublished work.

The first abusive practice is spiritual manipulation and control. Jesus clearly warned His disciples not to “lord it over” others. Abusive leaders often use the phrase God told me as a means of exerting control, claiming to speak for God in situations where they should instead offer counsel with humility. This form of manipulation may include misusing Scripture or presenting personal opinions as divine commands. A common tactic is alternating between praise and shame to create confusion and dependence. These leaders may also use fear and intimidation to maintain control. Congregants should never feel afraid of their pastor.

A second abusive practice is excessive accountability. While it is good for believers to seek wise counsel, spiritual leaders must teach people to hear from the Lord for themselves. Abuse occurs when a leader oversteps by micromanaging a person’s life, requiring a person to seek their permission for daily decisions, or overprogramming their time. In our case study, Pastor John crossed this line by trying to control Brandon’s decision to propose to his girlfriend. While pastoral guidance may be appropriate, decisions should never be forced or controlled.

A third destructive pattern is correction avoidance. Abusive leaders often refuse accountability. They isolate themselves, reject input, and silence those who raise concerns. This creates a culture of fear and unhealthy loyalty where no safeguards are in place to address misuse of power. By contrast, healthy leaders welcome correction and establish systems of accountability. The Assemblies of God strongly encourages all ministers to remain accountable to their district or network leaders, their church board, and trusted spiritual peers. Isolation from these supports often precedes moral or leadership failure.

**Q:** Describe to whom you are accountable to and how that accountability can protect you.

**A:** Answers will vary. Effective accountability may include mentors, supervisors, spiritual leaders, church boards, or family members who provide counsel, correction, and protection.

### Assumption of Dictatorial Authority

#### 4.4.1 — Define dictatorial authority and explain why it violates ethical ministry principles.

Healthy leadership includes accountability, shared decision-making, and respect for governing structures. When a minister ignores these principles and centralizes control, it can lead to ethical violations and harm to the church body. The Assemblies of God has identified the “assumption of dictatorial authority over an assembly” as a disciplinary offense (General Council of the Assemblies of God 2023, 66). This lesson will explore what that means, how it can be recognized, and why it is important for ministers to honor accountability and legal requirements in both spiritual and organizational leadership.

One of the serious offenses for which a credentialed minister may be disciplined is the assumption of dictatorial authority over an assembly. This occurs when a minister bypasses proper leadership structures and assumes complete control over the local church. Rather than functioning in collaboration with a governing board or respecting church bylaws, such a leader makes decisions unilaterally and disregards accountability.

Common signs of dictatorial leadership include the failure to hold regular board meetings or annual membership meetings as required by church bylaws and state nonprofit laws. A pastor who operates in this way may believe God is their only authority, ignoring the systems of accountability designed to protect both the church and the pastor. This pattern of leadership often accompanies other forms of spiritual abuse and can include mismanagement of finances and disregard for organizational procedures.

To help determine whether a minister is demonstrating dictatorial authority, the General Presbytery of the Assemblies of God has adopted three guiding questions. These questions provide clarity for identifying unhealthy leadership practices:

1. Is the minister demonstrating a willingness to exercise personal accountability and to operate within the guidelines of the church’s governing documents and the state’s nonprofit law requirements?

2. Is there a clearly articulated process for conflict resolution as it pertains to the church body, the governing board, and the pastor?

3. Has there been an observed pattern of manipulation or abuse of power that would indicate dictatorial authority?

These questions help discern whether a leader is honoring their role as a servant-shepherd or abusing their authority. Ministers must guard against any pattern of leadership that removes shared governance, silences accountability, or centralizes power without transparency.

### The Effect of Narcissism

#### 4.5.1 — Identify how insecurity and fauxnerability undermine authentic leadership.

One major contributor to spiritually abusive leadership is narcissism . Chuck DeGroat, in his book When Narcissism Comes to Church: Healing Your Community from Emotional and Spiritual Abuse , outlines ten traits that frequently appear in narcissistic pastors. These behaviors harm individuals, degrade team morale, and distort the purpose of ministry.

All decision-making centers on them. Narcissistic leaders struggle to delegate responsibility or empower others. Instead, they involve themselves in every decision and justify it by claiming spiritual authority. Others are excluded from meaningful leadership or decision-making roles.

Impatience and an inability to listen to others. Narcissistic pastors often display impatience with those around them. They may rush conversations and overlook what others are saying. They interpret their own impatience as strength or decisiveness. They tend to avoid personal responsibility and may dehumanize staff or volunteers who fail to meet their high expectations. DeGroat provides a telling example: One Friday afternoon, a lead pastor assigned several staff members the task of creating visual displays for the church’s upcoming budget presentation on Sunday. The team reacted with visible discouragement, but the pastor, showing little empathy, reminded them of the importance of the mission and mentioned that he still had to preach, perform two baptisms, and lead the meeting. Despite their hesitation, the team agreed. However, unforeseen circumstances kept two team members from helping, and the remaining person became overwhelmed. She eventually texted the pastor to say the visuals would not be finished in time. The pastor responded with what seemed like understanding. Yet during Sunday’s meeting, he stood before the congregation and said with a smirk, “Unfortunately, Jan, Melanie, and Jeff were responsible for designing the visuals, but they did not get the job done.” Then, winking at the team, he added, “Perhaps we know where the budget cuts are coming from this year.” The public comment left the team feeling anxious, embarrassed, and confused. Behavior like this quickly damages morale and creates a toxic work environment (2020, 72–73).

Delegation with limits or without true authority. Staff under narcissistic leaders are often given tasks but not the freedom or clarity to lead. Job roles are frequently changed, structures shifted, and authority withheld. This keeps team members in a constant state of confusion and discouragement.

Feelings of entitlement. Narcissistic pastors often carry a strong sense of entitlement , believing they deserve special treatment or privileges. This mindset can show itself in financial ways, such as expecting an unusually high salary or taking unauthorized benefits under the belief that they are underpaid and deserving of extra consideration. Entitlement may also be expressed through a deep desire for constant recognition, honor, or special status within the church community. Such attitudes stand in contrast to the humility and servant leadership modeled by Christ.

Feeling threatened or intimidated by other talented staff. Narcissistic pastors are often driven by deep insecurity. Rather than celebrating the strengths of others, they may feel threatened by talented staff members who show leadership potential or creative ideas. DeGroat notes that these leaders are constantly on the lookout for perceived disloyalty, and when they believe they have found it, they often respond harshly. They may use their position to issue threats—warning that individuals will be blacklisted, lose their positions, or be discredited. In extreme cases, they may even threaten to reveal personal information to maintain control and silence dissent.

Needing to be the best and brightest in the room. Narcissistic leaders thrive on praise and position themselves as the most spiritual, intelligent, or visionary person in any setting. They seek constant affirmation from those around them.

Inconsistency and impulsiveness. Narcissistic pastors often foster an unpredictable and unstable work environment. They may introduce new projects with little warning, expecting staff to respond immediately and without question. This constant change creates confusion and stress as staff members struggle to keep up with shifting priorities. DeGroat explains that this behavior stems from a need for continual ego reinforcement. The steady, often unglamorous work of building something long-term can feel discouraging to the narcissistic leader. By frequently launching new initiatives, they can position themselves to receive praise for success while blaming staff for any shortcomings.

Praising and withdrawing. Narcissistic pastors may offer public praise one moment, and the next, they withhold it entirely. This emotional manipulation controls people, especially those who seek approval, leaving them insecure and anxious.

Intimidation of others. Narcissistic pastors may use both direct and indirect tactics to exert control through fear. DeGroat notes that this can include condescending remarks, threats of termination, prolonged glares during meetings, harsh comments about a person’s work or appearance, or the removal of individuals from leadership roles. Indirect forms of intimidation often involve social isolation—those who challenge or disagree with the leader may find themselves excluded or sidelined. This is what Brandon experienced after respectfully confronting Pastor John. Once the relationship was fractured, the imbalance of power became painfully clear. In such cases, the leader often uses his or her position to silence or marginalize the individual, leaving lasting emotional and spiritual harm.

Fauxnerability. Narcissistic pastors often display a false form of vulnerability as a means of control or manipulation. DeGroat refers to this as “ fauxnerability .” One sign is that their disclosures tend to focus only on the distant past rather than current struggles. Another is staged vulnerability—emotion is put on display in public settings, such as during sermons, but there is little evidence of empathy or care in private. This trait also includes adopting a victim mindset, blaming others such as a spouse or team members for personal failures. Lastly, they may overshare personal details, not out of transparency, but to gain sympathy or draw attention. While appearing open, this behavior lacks sincerity and is often used to maintain influence.

Two Ethical Questions

Narcissistic and spiritually abusive leaders often leave a trail of wounded people behind. Congregants and fellow ministers alike have suffered under this kind of leadership. Some individuals leave their churches, their ministry calling, or even their faith as a result of such harm. Now that we have examined the patterns and effects of spiritual abuse, we must turn to two important ethical questions:

1. How can we avoid these destructive practices in our own ministry?

2. What should we do if we find ourselves under the leadership of a spiritually abusive leader?

These questions will guide our next steps in building ministries marked by humility, integrity, and the heart of Christ.

**Q:** Contrast Jesus’ attitude with the sense of entitlement some feel in ministry.

**A:** Jesus came to serve, not to be served. He laid down His power and position in order to fulfill the will of the Father and care for others. This humble posture is the opposite of entitlement, which demands recognition and privilege. Ministers must remember that they are called to serve in the same way Jesus did.

**Q:** As opposed to “fauxnerability,” what are some ways a minister can display appropriate vulnerability?

**A:** Ministers should be appropriately authentic in their communication, sharing honestly while maintaining healthy boundaries. Vulnerability should be personal and sincere, not staged or excessive. The pulpit should not be used as a place for emotional unloading but for godly instruction. Ministers can model Christlike transparency in trusted relationships and accountability settings.

### Avoiding Spiritually Abusive Practices

#### 4.6.1 — Explain how Jesus modeled power through service and describe the implications for a minister's use of authority and influence.

Spiritually abusive behaviors can often develop subtly over time. For that reason, ministers must commit to proactive, Christ-centered practices that prevent harmful leadership patterns. The best safeguard against spiritual abuse is following the model of Jesus who led through humility, service, and sacrificial love. This lesson encourages students to examine their own leadership approaches, remain open to feedback, and establish safeguards to remain spiritually healthy.

Christlike Servant Leadership

The most effective way to avoid spiritually abusive practices is to model your life and ministry after the example of Jesus as a servant leader. The Gospel of John recounts Jesus washing the feet of His disciples before the Feast of the Passover. After performing this humble act—one usually reserved for servants—Jesus said, “Now that I, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also should wash one another’s feet. I have set you an example that you should do as I have done for you. Very truly I tell you, no servant is greater than his master, nor is a messenger greater than the one who sent him” (John 13:14–16). Ministers are called to serve the Lord and the people entrusted to their care. Members of the church are not there to meet the minister’s needs. Paul wrote, “Do nothing out of selfish ambition or vain conceit. Rather, in humility value others above yourselves, not looking to your own interests but each of you to the interests of the others. In your relationships with one another, have the same mindset as Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be used to his own advantage; rather, he made himself nothing by taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness” (Philippians 2:3–7). Instead of using His power for self-promotion, Jesus used it to serve others.

Practicing Self-Awareness

In addition to following the example of Jesus, it is essential for leaders to develop strong self-awareness. Most people do not intentionally become abusive leaders. Harmful behaviors can emerge from previous experiences with unhealthy authority figures or abusive systems. Some may carry personality traits that make them more vulnerable to narcissistic tendencies. As we recognize the signs of spiritual abuse and compare them with the example of Christ, we can prayerfully examine our own actions and motivations. It can be helpful to ask trusted individuals to speak into our lives. For example, Dr. James Bradford once shared that he asked his wife, “What is it like to be married to me?” Questions like this, posed to people who love us enough to tell the truth, can help expose any behavior that may verge on spiritual abuse.

Embracing Accountability

Finally, healthy leaders must intentionally embrace accountability. Strong accountability structures offer protection and provide opportunities for correction before serious issues arise. Accountability can come from natural relationships like family and close friends, as well as from those who have authority over you, such as pastors, church boards, or district leadership. These relationships create a support system to encourage ethical behavior and spiritual health. Embracing accountability is a vital safeguard that can help ministers avoid moral failure and the destruction that often follows.

**Q:** Reflect on any spiritually abusive tendencies you have observed from other leaders or your upbringing that should be examined in light of this chapter. Are there any ways in which these practices have influenced your own leadership?

**A:** Answers will vary. This question encourages honest self-reflection on learned behaviors, leadership influences, and areas for growth.

### Leaving an Abusive Environment

#### 4.7.1 — Describe the importance of genuine vulnerability in ministry and explain how it fosters authentic relationships.

While this chapter has focused on identifying and avoiding spiritually abusive behavior, it is also important to address what to do when someone finds themselves under such leadership. Remaining in an abusive environment can lead to deep spiritual, emotional, and even physical harm. In these moments, ministers must seek the Lord’s direction and approach their response with both courage and grace. This lesson explores biblical wisdom and practical guidance for leaving an abusive situation in a way that honors God and preserves integrity.

Seeking God’s Guidance and Wise Counsel

Deciding whether to leave a spiritually abusive environment requires serious reflection, much prayer, and wise counsel from trusted leaders. Dr. James Bradford once preached a message using the biblical story of Saul and David to offer insight. Saul, consumed by envy, hurled spears at David. In response, David did not retaliate. Instead, he removed himself from the situation. Dr. Bradford identified two critical lessons: 1) David did not throw spears back, and 2) David “made good his escape.” These lessons suggest that retaliation is not the answer but neither is it remaining in a situation that causes lasting harm. A minister who realizes they cannot be spiritually healthy while remaining in an abusive environment may need to make the difficult decision to leave.

Leaving with Integrity

When it becomes necessary to leave an unhealthy ministry environment, it should be done with maturity and integrity. Like David, the minister should avoid speaking destructively about the abusive leader or the church. Choosing not to return verbal or public attacks demonstrates spiritual strength and prevents further division. However, there may be situations in which it is appropriate and even necessary to have honest conversations with church or district leadership. These discussions can help bring clarity, protection, and, in some cases, lasting change within the church. Leaving quietly does not mean ignoring problems but rather handling them through appropriate channels and with a spirit of peace.

Case Study Analysis

Pastor John exhibited several patterns of spiritually abusive behavior. One of the most damaging actions was his attempt to control Brandon’s personal decision to marry his girlfriend. What should have been a joyful conversation quickly turned into a moment of pain and discouragement. While pastors should offer godly counsel, it is not appropriate for them to cross the line into control or hyper-accountability. Brandon had support from his family and mentors, yet Pastor John’s response reflected an abuse of authority rather than pastoral care.

The case study reveals additional concerns. Pastor John demonstrated classic patterns of narcissistic leadership, such as giving and withdrawing praise as a method of control. He publicly shamed his staff and used intimidation rather than encouragement. When Brandon respectfully raised his concerns, Pastor John responded defensively and retaliated, further isolating him and damaging the staff environment. These behaviors often leave lasting emotional and spiritual harm on those who serve under such leadership.

It is critical that Brandon seek support from stable, loving relationships during this season. Given the repeated patterns of abuse, it would be wise for Brandon to prayerfully consider leaving his ministry position and seeking a healthier place to grow and serve. Sadly, some churches suffer under the leadership of pastors who display abusive or narcissistic traits, often leaving behind a trail of hurt individuals.

Although change may be difficult, it is not beyond the reach of God’s grace. With the conviction of the Holy Spirit and intentional intervention from trusted leaders, even a leader like Pastor John could experience repentance and transformation. However, such change requires humility, accountability, and a willingness to be corrected—qualities that must be cultivated through the work of God in the heart.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister can leave a spiritually abusive situation in a healthy manner?

**A:** Healthy responses may include seeking counsel from trusted mentors or district leaders, documenting and reporting concerns appropriately, and avoiding public criticism of the church or leader. Emotional and spiritual healing can be supported through professional counseling, prayer, and fellowship in a healthy church community. 5 CHAPTER

## 5

### The Problem of Clergy Sexual Abuse

#### 5.1.1 — Explain why the church must take clergy sexual misconduct seriously, including the devastating effects it has on congregations.

Clergy sexual misconduct has become a major concern across many denominations. Although public awareness of the problem has increased in recent decades, the underlying issue is not new. Ministry leaders are called to model Christlike behavior, but when they fail morally, the effects are devastating. This lesson will explore the nature of clergy sexual abuse, biblical warnings and consequences, and how improper boundaries can lead to catastrophic outcomes. Ministers must be aware of these dangers and take proactive steps to protect themselves and those they serve.

Clergy sexual misconduct has gained national attention in recent decades. Trull and Creech report that in 2002 the Religion Newswriters Association named the Roman Catholic abuse scandal the top story of the year, with Cardinal Bernard Law as the top newsmaker. That year, sexual abuse scandals affected nearly every diocese in the United States and involved over twelve hundred priests who had harmed more than four thousand minors over six decades (2017, 152). However, this issue is not limited to the Roman Catholic Church or to situations involving minors. Protestant churches have also experienced high-profile moral failures that highlight the urgent need to address this problem.

Clergy sexual misconduct is any sexual behavior between a ministry leader and someone they serve that violates ethical, spiritual, or professional boundaries. This includes adultery, sexual harassment, abuse of minors, and emotional or physical relationships that begin under the guise of spiritual care. The betrayal is not only personal—it is spiritual and communal. When a pastor or church leader engages in misconduct, it damages the sacred trust that people place in their shepherds.

In recent years, more victims have come forward, and churches have been forced to confront this painful issue publicly. Media coverage, lawsuits, and government investigations have made it clear that misconduct is not rare. Some churches have responded with repentance and accountability. Others, unfortunately, have responded by hiding the problem, which only adds to the pain and distrust.

Sexual misconduct is particularly serious because it is often hidden for long periods. Victims may feel shame, fear, or confusion. They may be unsure whether they will be believed or fear the consequences of speaking up. At the same time, the leader involved may justify or minimize the behavior, allowing the situation to continue unchecked. This secrecy allows the sin to grow and do greater harm. When it is finally exposed, the damage has often spread far beyond the original individuals involved.

The church must take this issue seriously. Every church and ministry leader needs to acknowledge that clergy sexual misconduct is not just a problem “out there.” It can happen in any setting where accountability, healthy boundaries, and personal integrity are neglected. Ministers must not only avoid such behaviors but also work to build a culture of safety, transparency, and trust.

One reason clergy sexual misconduct is so damaging is due to the power imbalance between a ministry leader and a congregant. Whether someone is a pastor, staff member, campus minister, or Christian counselor, they are in a position of authority. Stanley Grenz and Roy Bell describe sexual misconduct by pastors as a double betrayal: “It is a violation of a sacred sexual trust, marring the beautiful picture God has given of the relationship of Christ and the church. And it is a violation of a power trust, abusing the privilege of the pastoral position with which the ordained leader has been endowed by the church and its Lord” (Trull and Creech 2017, 154).

The Bible provides examples of leaders who misused their positions for sexual sin. Eli’s sons Hophni and Phinehas engaged in immoral acts with women who served at the tabernacle: “Now Eli, who was very old, heard about everything his sons were doing to all Israel and how they slept with the women who served at the entrance to the tent of meeting” (1 Samuel 2:22). King David used his power to sleep with Bathsheba, another man’s wife. In both situations, God responded with serious judgment. These accounts serve as warnings for ministers today.

When a minister uses their position to engage in an inappropriate relationship, it is not a mutual connection—it is an abuse of authority. Even if the other person appears to be a willing participant, the power imbalance means that true consent is compromised. The minister holds spiritual, emotional, and often social influence over the individual. This influence can cause confusion, dependence, and even manipulation, whether intentional or not. The greater the authority a minister holds, the greater the responsibility to protect the trust placed in them. When boundaries are not clearly defined and upheld, the door is opened for misinterpretation, emotional entanglement, and eventual misconduct.

Sexual misconduct, whether intentional or not, causes immense harm. Victims, their families, the offending minister, the church, and the broader body of Christ all suffer. These failures destroy trust in spiritual leadership and can be a major barrier to someone accepting Christ. As a minister, you will be discredited and will either lose your credentials or be placed in a restoration program with the hope of keeping your credentials. In cases where a minor has been violated, a minister does not qualify for a restoration program and will be dismissed. In the case study, Jason was a youth pastor who did not set out to abuse his role. Yet by ignoring proper boundaries, he caused lasting damage to Tina, his family, and his church.

Most ministers do not begin their calling with the intent to end in disgrace. However, certain patterns can open the door to moral failure. Ministry is inherently compassionate work, and when a minister helps someone experience spiritual breakthrough or healing, that person may become emotionally attached. What begins as gratitude can turn into infatuation or inappropriate affection. If the minister internalizes this admiration to meet personal insecurities, the situation becomes dangerous.

Furthermore, many ministers fail to set clear boundaries. A combination of ministry compassion, unmet emotional needs, and lack of safeguards can result in poor judgment. Some ministers convince themselves that an inappropriate relationship is mutual or even justified, especially if the other person initiated it or has a difficult background. In truth, the minister always holds more authority and responsibility. Even if the congregant is of legal age, a moral line has been crossed when a leader uses that position to begin a romantic or sexual relationship.

The ripple effects of sexual misconduct extend far beyond the individuals directly involved. Congregations often experience deep confusion, anger, and division. Some members may leave the church entirely, disillusioned by the leader’s failure and the damage to their faith. Others may struggle to trust future leaders or feel spiritually abandoned. The broader Christian community can also suffer as unbelievers point to these scandals as evidence of hypocrisy within the church. When ministers fall, the name of Christ is dishonored, and the credibility of the gospel message is weakened in the eyes of the world. This is why Scripture reminds leaders to be “above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2). Their lives represent not only their personal witness, but the reputation of Christ’s church.

**Q:** What are some of the ways sexual misconduct in the church harms the cause of Christ?

**A:** Christians are called to represent Christ with integrity. When a minister commits sexual misconduct, it damages the church’s witness and may turn people away from the gospel. It can harm the credibility of the Church in the community, discourage potential seekers, and cause deep pain to the victim, the minister’s family, and the congregation.
