### What Constitutes Clergy Sexual Misconduct

#### 5.2.1 — Describe how clergy sexual misconduct affects not only victims but also other congregants and the broader church.

Clergy sexual misconduct includes a wide range of behaviors, not all of which involve physical contact or obvious violations. Some actions may seem minor or even innocent at first but can quickly develop into serious ethical failures. This lesson defines key categories of clergy misconduct involving others and addresses a hidden but widespread issue—pornography. Ministers must have a clear understanding of what constitutes misconduct in order to maintain integrity, protect others, and prevent unintended harm. By understanding these definitions and consequences, ministers will be better equipped to maintain healthy boundaries and lead with purity and accountability.

Inappropriate Physical Activity (Consensual)

Inappropriate physical activity occurs when a minister engages in physical intimacy with a congregant. It includes sexual intercourse or inappropriate touching, whether in person or through digital communication. One increasingly common example is “sexting,” which involves sending or receiving sexually explicit images or messages. Even when the behavior appears consensual, the power imbalance makes the relationship unethical.

Emotional Affair

An emotional affair takes place when a minister and a congregant develop a level of emotional intimacy and connection that resembles a romantic relationship. This is especially serious if either person is married as it breaks the covenant of marriage. However, even if both individuals are single, emotional dependence and secrecy can cross boundaries and create an unhealthy dynamic. These types of relationships will be addressed further in a later lesson.

Inappropriate Behavior with a Minor

This is the most serious type of misconduct. It includes any inappropriate behavior—physical or verbal—between a minister and a minor. It can involve fondling, sexually suggestive language, grooming, or digital communication such as texting, video chats, or artificial intelligence interactions.

Understanding Grooming

Grooming is a deliberate and often long-term process in which an offender builds trust with a child in order to abuse them while maintaining secrecy. Offenders may also groom the child’s parents to appear trustworthy. According to the Maryland Coalition Against Sexual Assault (https://www.mcasa.org), the grooming process may include the following stages:

Targeting the victim. Offenders often look for emotional neediness, low self-confidence, or lack of parental attention.

Gaining trust. Offenders observe their victims closely to understand their emotional needs and vulnerabilities, then look for ways to meet those needs in order to build trust. As part of this process, the predator may introduce secrecy into the relationship to strengthen the bond and create distance between the child and their parents. This could include allowing the child to engage in activities the parents would not approve of, further weakening parental influence.

Filling a need. Through gifts, attention, or affection, the predator becomes a significant figure in the child’s life.

Isolating the child . The offender may offer to babysit for free or provide other helpful favors as a way to gain alone time with the child, away from adult supervision. Parents, unaware of the predator’s intentions, may unintentionally support this arrangement by expressing gratitude for what seems like a generous and caring relationship.

Sexualizing interaction. Grooming begins with nonsexual touching, such as accidental or playful touching to desensitize the child so the child does not resist a more sexualized touch. The offender then exploits the child’s curiosity to advance the sexuality of the interaction.

Maintaining control. To keep the child silent, the predator may use fear, guilt, or emotional manipulation. This often includes threats like, “If you tell your mother what happened, she will hate you,” or “If you tell anyone, I will hurt someone in your family.” The offender may also shift the blame onto the child or attempt to justify the abuse by saying, “This is okay,” or “You let this happen.” These tactics are designed to create confusion and prevent the child from seeking help.

Ministers must be vigilant and trained to recognize the signs of grooming, both to avoid unintentional harm and to protect the flock from predators. Being a shepherd includes actively guarding against these dangers.

Unwelcome Behavior

This category includes any inappropriate action—verbal or physical—that is not welcomed by the recipient. It includes both intentional acts like sexual assault and inadvertent-offensive acts such as flirtatious remarks, inappropriate humor, or accidental touches that are not appropriate in ministry settings. A minister may not mean harm but can still deeply offend or confuse a congregant. What some dismiss as “locker room” behavior—jokes, comments, or gestures—can be harmful and are considered misconduct.

It is a mistake to think that sexual assault must be violent to qualify as abuse. Some of the most damaging incidents occur when misconduct is wrapped in religious language or disguised as spiritual guidance, especially when delivered by someone in a trusted role.

Pornography is a hidden sin that has become more accessible than ever before. In the past, a person had to make an effort to obtain a physical magazine or video. Now, a single careless click can lead to an explicit website. Cultural attitudes have also shifted. In a 2016 study by Barna, only 32 percent of teenagers said viewing pornography was wrong compared to 56 percent who said not recycling was wrong. This shift reveals how desensitized society has become (Barna Group 2016).

Christians are not exempt from the struggle with pornography. A Barna study released in 2023 reported that over half of practicing Christians admitted to using pornography to some degree (Barna 2024). Ministers must be especially cautious. Pornography erodes the soul, damages marriages, pollutes the mind, and creates unrealistic and harmful views of sexuality.

There are practical steps to take to guard against this temptation. Set up digital filters on all devices, and avoid websites that may contain suggestive material. Avoid television channels, social media content, or short videos that contain sexually explicit or suggestive scenes.

Be alert to “soft porn,” which may appear in mainstream media, music, advertising, or jokes. It may seem subtle, but it still promotes lust and gradually dulls the conscience. Jesus warned, “Anyone who looks at a woman lustfully has already committed adultery with her in his heart” (Matthew 5:28). What begins in the mind can affect the heart, and eventually, one’s actions. Soft porn is not just a pathway to deeper struggles and possible addiction; it is sinful and harmful in itself.

If pornography is a temptation or struggle, ministers must establish firm accountability. Services such as Covenant Eyes can monitor online activity and send reports to a trusted accountability partner. Ministries like Pure Life, Celebrate Recovery, and Minister Family Care (https://ministerfamilycare.ag.org/) offer tools and counseling for those battling this issue. RightNow Media also offers teaching and support. In more serious cases, seek help from a licensed Christian counselor or therapist. There is no shame in asking for help; it is a sign of strength and a step toward freedom.

**Q:** What are some examples of unwelcome behavior toward others that some may consider “locker room” talk but are truly inappropriate?

**A:** Examples may include flirtatious behavior, sexual humor or jokes, and inappropriate comments about someone’s appearance. Even if intended playfully, such actions can be hurtful or offensive and are not fitting for those in the ministry.

**Q:** What steps have you taken to avoid intentionally or unintentionally viewing pornography?

**A:** Some examples may include setting up internet filters, using accountability software, limiting screen time, or seeking support from an accountability partner or counselor.

#### 5.2.2 — Identify the hidden dangers of pornography and explain how it erodes the soul and promotes harmful views of sexuality.

Clergy sexual misconduct includes a wide range of behaviors, not all of which involve physical contact or obvious violations. Some actions may seem minor or even innocent at first but can quickly develop into serious ethical failures. This lesson defines key categories of clergy misconduct involving others and addresses a hidden but widespread issue—pornography. Ministers must have a clear understanding of what constitutes misconduct in order to maintain integrity, protect others, and prevent unintended harm. By understanding these definitions and consequences, ministers will be better equipped to maintain healthy boundaries and lead with purity and accountability.

Inappropriate Physical Activity (Consensual)

Inappropriate physical activity occurs when a minister engages in physical intimacy with a congregant. It includes sexual intercourse or inappropriate touching, whether in person or through digital communication. One increasingly common example is “sexting,” which involves sending or receiving sexually explicit images or messages. Even when the behavior appears consensual, the power imbalance makes the relationship unethical.

Emotional Affair

An emotional affair takes place when a minister and a congregant develop a level of emotional intimacy and connection that resembles a romantic relationship. This is especially serious if either person is married as it breaks the covenant of marriage. However, even if both individuals are single, emotional dependence and secrecy can cross boundaries and create an unhealthy dynamic. These types of relationships will be addressed further in a later lesson.

Inappropriate Behavior with a Minor

This is the most serious type of misconduct. It includes any inappropriate behavior—physical or verbal—between a minister and a minor. It can involve fondling, sexually suggestive language, grooming, or digital communication such as texting, video chats, or artificial intelligence interactions.

Understanding Grooming

Grooming is a deliberate and often long-term process in which an offender builds trust with a child in order to abuse them while maintaining secrecy. Offenders may also groom the child’s parents to appear trustworthy. According to the Maryland Coalition Against Sexual Assault (https://www.mcasa.org), the grooming process may include the following stages:

Targeting the victim. Offenders often look for emotional neediness, low self-confidence, or lack of parental attention.

Gaining trust. Offenders observe their victims closely to understand their emotional needs and vulnerabilities, then look for ways to meet those needs in order to build trust. As part of this process, the predator may introduce secrecy into the relationship to strengthen the bond and create distance between the child and their parents. This could include allowing the child to engage in activities the parents would not approve of, further weakening parental influence.

Filling a need. Through gifts, attention, or affection, the predator becomes a significant figure in the child’s life.

Isolating the child . The offender may offer to babysit for free or provide other helpful favors as a way to gain alone time with the child, away from adult supervision. Parents, unaware of the predator’s intentions, may unintentionally support this arrangement by expressing gratitude for what seems like a generous and caring relationship.

Sexualizing interaction. Grooming begins with nonsexual touching, such as accidental or playful touching to desensitize the child so the child does not resist a more sexualized touch. The offender then exploits the child’s curiosity to advance the sexuality of the interaction.

Maintaining control. To keep the child silent, the predator may use fear, guilt, or emotional manipulation. This often includes threats like, “If you tell your mother what happened, she will hate you,” or “If you tell anyone, I will hurt someone in your family.” The offender may also shift the blame onto the child or attempt to justify the abuse by saying, “This is okay,” or “You let this happen.” These tactics are designed to create confusion and prevent the child from seeking help.

Ministers must be vigilant and trained to recognize the signs of grooming, both to avoid unintentional harm and to protect the flock from predators. Being a shepherd includes actively guarding against these dangers.

Unwelcome Behavior

This category includes any inappropriate action—verbal or physical—that is not welcomed by the recipient. It includes both intentional acts like sexual assault and inadvertent-offensive acts such as flirtatious remarks, inappropriate humor, or accidental touches that are not appropriate in ministry settings. A minister may not mean harm but can still deeply offend or confuse a congregant. What some dismiss as “locker room” behavior—jokes, comments, or gestures—can be harmful and are considered misconduct.

It is a mistake to think that sexual assault must be violent to qualify as abuse. Some of the most damaging incidents occur when misconduct is wrapped in religious language or disguised as spiritual guidance, especially when delivered by someone in a trusted role.

Pornography is a hidden sin that has become more accessible than ever before. In the past, a person had to make an effort to obtain a physical magazine or video. Now, a single careless click can lead to an explicit website. Cultural attitudes have also shifted. In a 2016 study by Barna, only 32 percent of teenagers said viewing pornography was wrong compared to 56 percent who said not recycling was wrong. This shift reveals how desensitized society has become (Barna Group 2016).

Christians are not exempt from the struggle with pornography. A Barna study released in 2023 reported that over half of practicing Christians admitted to using pornography to some degree (Barna 2024). Ministers must be especially cautious. Pornography erodes the soul, damages marriages, pollutes the mind, and creates unrealistic and harmful views of sexuality.

There are practical steps to take to guard against this temptation. Set up digital filters on all devices, and avoid websites that may contain suggestive material. Avoid television channels, social media content, or short videos that contain sexually explicit or suggestive scenes.

Be alert to “soft porn,” which may appear in mainstream media, music, advertising, or jokes. It may seem subtle, but it still promotes lust and gradually dulls the conscience. Jesus warned, “Anyone who looks at a woman lustfully has already committed adultery with her in his heart” (Matthew 5:28). What begins in the mind can affect the heart, and eventually, one’s actions. Soft porn is not just a pathway to deeper struggles and possible addiction; it is sinful and harmful in itself.

If pornography is a temptation or struggle, ministers must establish firm accountability. Services such as Covenant Eyes can monitor online activity and send reports to a trusted accountability partner. Ministries like Pure Life, Celebrate Recovery, and Minister Family Care (https://ministerfamilycare.ag.org/) offer tools and counseling for those battling this issue. RightNow Media also offers teaching and support. In more serious cases, seek help from a licensed Christian counselor or therapist. There is no shame in asking for help; it is a sign of strength and a step toward freedom.

**A:** Examples may include flirtatious behavior, sexual humor or jokes, and inappropriate comments about someone’s appearance. Even if intended playfully, such actions can be hurtful or offensive and are not fitting for those in the ministry.

### Establishing Healthy Boundaries

#### 5.3.1 — Explain the value of boundaries and describe how they provide clarity and protect emotional and relational health.

Ministers rarely begin ministry intending to fall into sexual misconduct. Yet failure often comes not from sudden decisions but from neglecting safeguards. Just as a ship’s captain does not plan to crash into rocks but may do so when tired or ignoring warning systems, ministers must not assume they are immune to temptation. Healthy boundaries are like lighthouses that protect ministers from hidden dangers and guide them safely in their calling.

Boundaries do more than prevent immoral actions. They also protect a minister’s reputation from false accusations and the appearance of impropriety. Scripture instructs ministers to be “above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2), which means living in such a way that others cannot reasonably question their character. When ministers recognize warning signs, respect others’ boundaries, and build a culture of accountability, they protect themselves and strengthen those around them.

This lesson will explore the value of boundaries, practical safeguards in ministry settings, and how to recognize potential warning signs. It will also address special considerations for single ministers and those working with youth and children.

The Value of Boundaries

Boundaries are essential tools for wise and effective ministry. They provide clarity about what is appropriate, protect emotional and relational health, and help ministers avoid compromising situations. Clear boundaries also build trust among those served, demonstrating that the minister can be relied upon to act with integrity and wisdom.

In addition to guarding against personal failure, boundaries protect others. They create safe spaces where people can receive care without confusion or concern. Boundaries also reduce the likelihood of misinterpretation or suspicion, which is especially important in settings where power dynamics are present. The call to be “above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2) includes not only avoiding sin but also avoiding situations that could be misread by others.

Finally, when ministers are intentional about their own boundaries, they are better equipped to encourage healthy patterns in others. Being sensitive to signals of discomfort, inappropriate behavior, or emotional dependence helps foster a culture of accountability and grace. In this way, boundaries do not limit ministry; they strengthen it by preserving the credibility, safety, and spiritual health of both the minister and the community.

Safeguards Related to Being Alone with Another Person

One key boundary is to avoid being alone with a person of the opposite sex in a private setting, especially during pastoral care. When possible, it is better to meet in groups or public spaces. This protects against temptation and helps others feel safe. It also guards against perceptions of impropriety. For example, some ministers choose not to ride in a vehicle alone with someone of the opposite gender who is not a family member—not because of distrust, but to protect the reputations of both.

This does not mean a minister should never meet alone with someone of the opposite sex. A pastor is called to serve the entire congregation, not only those of the same gender. A pastoral meeting in a church office is generally considered a professional and safe setting. However, even in such settings, it is wise to implement appropriate safeguards.

If possible, include a trusted third person in the meeting. This provides an added layer of protection for both the minister and the other individual, and it may also help the person feel more at ease. If a third party is not available or appropriate, be sure that others are present in the building and that the office used for the meeting has windows. Visibility is a key element in maintaining accountability.

Additionally, if you are offering pastoral care but are not a licensed professional counselor, it is advisable to refer the individual to a qualified counselor after the third visit and discontinue further one-on-one sessions. This step helps ensure that the person receives the support they need while also protecting the integrity of the pastoral relationship.

Men and women are both called by God to serve in ministry, and it is important to normalize working together in healthy, respectful ways. I have served alongside women on staff, have led teams that included women, and have also served under the leadership of a woman. These experiences have demonstrated that men and women can partner effectively in ministry when clear boundaries are in place.

Creating a culture where gender cooperation is normal requires intentionality. Appropriate guardrails help protect everyone involved, promote accountability, and support trust within the team. We should not shy away from hiring or serving with staff members of the opposite sex. Instead, we should celebrate the shared call to the ministry while remaining committed to practices that ensure integrity. General Secretary Donna Barrett will speak more on this important topic in a later chapter.

Pay Attention to Warning Signs

Ignoring warning lights on a vehicle can lead to a breakdown. Similarly, failing to recognize warning signs in ministry relationships can lead to disaster. The Holy Spirit often gives quiet prompts when boundaries are being crossed. Ministers must remain sensitive to His voice and open to correction from trusted people. If a spouse or close friend expresses concern about a relationship, it should be taken seriously, not dismissed.

Lebacqz and Barton provide a checklist of warning signs for ministers (Trull and Creech 2017, 169):

The publicity test: what would others think?

Physical arousal: one’s own or the other’s

Inordinate sexual fantasy

Sexual gestures or body language

Intuition, instinct, or not feeling right

Wanting to share intimacies that are not called for

A parishioner wanting too much time or attention

Wanting to shift the focus to sexual subjects

Recognizing and responding to these signs early can help prevent moral failure.

Having a Healthy Life

In criminal investigations, motive and opportunity are essential factors. The same is true in cases of ministerial misconduct. While setting boundaries helps reduce opportunity, maintaining a healthy life helps reduce motivation. Many affairs begin because of unmet emotional or relational needs. For married ministers, a healthy marriage is the best safeguard against temptation. For single ministers, life-giving friendships, emotional health, and spiritual discipline are essential.

Leaders who care for themselves well are better positioned to lead others. Keeping one’s spiritual, emotional, and physical “tanks” full provides strength to resist temptation and make wise decisions. Preventing misconduct involves minimizing both motive and opportunity.

When the Minister Is Single

Even when both the minister and the congregant are single, clear boundaries are still necessary. In cases involving married individuals, the boundaries are often more obvious due to the covenant of marriage. However, being unmarried does not remove the responsibility to live a pure life. Single ministers are called to the same biblical standard as married ministers and must model God-honoring behavior in all relationships.

Scripture clearly teaches that sexual intimacy belongs within the covenant of marriage. This teaching stands in contrast to modern cultural norms where premarital sexual activity is often portrayed as normal and expected. If ministers are not careful, their understanding of sexuality can be shaped more by entertainment and public opinion than by the Word of God. A Christian who enters into a dating relationship must do so with intentionality, committing in advance to live with integrity and purity. It is important to have open conversations about abstinence, avoid situations that invite temptation, and remain accountable to trusted spiritual mentors.

Single ministers must also be mindful of the influence and authority they carry. Even if a romantic relationship is not forbidden, it should be approached with caution, prayer, and wise counsel. Power dynamics can affect how others perceive the relationship. If the relationship ends poorly, it may lead to discomfort or cause the other person to leave the church. In some cases, congregants may develop romanticized feelings toward a leader simply because that leader has been kind or helpful in a time of need. Ministers must remain discerning, humble, and accountable when navigating relationships within the church.

Children’s and Youth Ministry

Healthy boundaries are especially critical when serving in children’s and youth ministry. Creating and maintaining a safe environment for young people must be a top priority for every church. Sadly, one of the most common legal claims brought against churches involves sexual misconduct with minors. Youth and children’s workers are sometimes young, inexperienced, or undertrained, which increases the need for clear instruction and supervision.

Later in this course, we will examine how churches can implement systems to protect minors. For now, the focus is on how you, as a minister or leader, can personally establish solid, protective boundaries in your work with children and youth. The fact that you are taking this course demonstrates your commitment to honoring God and protecting others—and may prevent you from making a devastating mistake in this sensitive area.

A valuable resource on this topic is the 2018 publication Youth Ministry in a #MeToo Culture: Setting and Keeping Healthy Relational Boundaries by Church Law & Tax . In it, attorney Frank Sommerville outlines seven boundary guidelines that help protect both students and adult leaders. The summaries below are based on his recommendations (Church Law and Tax 2018, 48–53).

The “Never Alone” Rule

Leaders should avoid being alone with a student. Always meet minors in the presence of a third party. In one-on-one mentoring settings, choose open or public spaces. This principle applies to all forms of communication—including texting and social media. Avoid private messaging entirely.

One area where this rule is often overlooked is transportation. Do not ride alone with a teen in a vehicle, even if they need a ride. Doing so puts your future and the students’ safety at risk. In the case study, failure to follow this guideline led directly to misconduct.

The “Opposite Sex” Rule

While it may seem obvious, adult leaders must be intentional about maintaining appropriate emotional boundaries with teens of the opposite sex. What may feel like a friendly or harmless interaction with the adult can easily be misunderstood by a student as a romantic interest. To safeguard against this, leaders should regularly seek feedback from fellow team members about their communication and behavior. If a colleague expresses concern, the leader should be willing to adjust accordingly. This is especially important when working with teens who are emotionally vulnerable or seeking affirmation.

Adolescents often experience intense emotions and may form attachments to trusted adults. A teen might develop a crush on a leader simply because that leader has been kind, supportive, or spiritually influential. If those feelings are not returned, the teen may respond with hurt or anger, which can sometimes result in false or exaggerated accusations, even legal ones.

Leaders must also exercise caution when working with same-sex students, particularly when issues of identity or sexuality are involved. A teen who is uncertain or confused about his or her sexual identity may misinterpret attention as emotional or romantic support. While it is appropriate to provide biblical counsel, leaders must guard against forming emotionally dependent or inappropriate connections.

The “More Time” Rule

Pay attention to how much time you spend with any one student. Excessive or unstructured time outside of official ministry activities can blur boundaries. If a student begins to seek out your attention frequently, it may be a sign that something unhealthy is forming. Track these interactions, and when needed, involve supervisors and the student’s parents for accountability and clarity.

The “Conditional Confidentiality” Rule

Ministers and leaders must never promise absolute confidentiality. You are a mandatory reporter and must report any suspicion of abuse, self-harm, harm to others, or elder abuse as required by law. Confidentiality must yield to legal and ethical responsibilities. This protects both the students and the church.

The “Accountability” Rule

Youth leaders need systems of accountability. Every leader should follow the Child Safety Guidelines and Policies established by the church. Accountability promotes safety, provides clarity, and protects the reputation of the minister and the church. Leaders should welcome oversight and regular evaluation of their conduct.

The “No Porn” Rule

While this may seem obvious, it is worth stating clearly: students should be warned about the spiritual and emotional dangers of pornography. Leaders should model purity and speak truthfully about the destructive nature of pornography in a compassionate and honest manner.

The “No Fear” Rule

The decision-making center of the brain does not fully develop until around the age of twenty-five. This helps explain why many teenagers engage in risky behavior—they often feel invincible and underestimate potential consequences. While this is a normal part of adolescent development, it places a special responsibility on youth leaders to remain vigilant.

Leaders must be alert to unsafe activities, poor judgment, or impulsive behavior. Their role is not only to protect students from harm but also to guard themselves against accusations of negligent supervision. If a student begins engaging in risky behavior, the leader must respond promptly and firmly. In some cases, it may be necessary to cancel an activity entirely to ensure everyone’s safety. Taking decisive action, even when inconvenient, demonstrates wisdom and protects both the student and the ministry.

**Q:** What are some ways you can have a healthy life, which will help avoid unmet needs and the temptation to engage in unethical sexual behavior?

**A:** If you are single, build strong, supportive friendships and maintain emotional and spiritual health. If you are married, invest in your marriage by spending meaningful time together, communicating openly, and resolving conflicts in a healthy manner. Whether single or married, pay attention to emotional or spiritual emptiness and seek counseling or mentorship when needed.

#### 5.3.2 — Identify appropriate boundaries for single ministers and describe how they help avoid compromising situations.

This protects against temptation and helps others feel safe. It also guards against perceptions of impropriety. For example, some ministers choose not to ride in a vehicle alone with someone of the opposite gender who is not a family member—not because of distrust, but to protect the reputations of both.

This does not mean a minister should never meet alone with someone of the opposite sex. A pastor is called to serve the entire congregation, not only those of the same gender. A pastoral meeting in a church office is generally considered a professional and safe setting. However, even in such settings, it is wise to implement appropriate safeguards.

If possible, include a trusted third person in the meeting. This provides an added layer of protection for both the minister and the other individual, and it may also help the person feel more at ease. If a third party is not available or appropriate, be sure that others are present in the building and that the office used for the meeting has windows. Visibility is a key element in maintaining accountability.

Additionally, if you are offering pastoral care but are not a licensed professional counselor, it is advisable to refer the individual to a qualified counselor after the third visit and discontinue further one-on-one sessions. This step helps ensure that the person receives the support they need while also protecting the integrity of the pastoral relationship.

Men and women are both called by God to serve in ministry, and it is important to normalize working together in healthy, respectful ways. I have served alongside women on staff, have led teams that included women, and have also served under the leadership of a woman. These experiences have demonstrated that men and women can partner effectively in ministry when clear boundaries are in place.

Creating a culture where gender cooperation is normal requires intentionality. Appropriate guardrails help protect everyone involved, promote accountability, and support trust within the team. We should not shy away from hiring or serving with staff members of the opposite sex. Instead, we should celebrate the shared call to the ministry while remaining committed to practices that ensure integrity. General Secretary Donna Barrett will speak more on this important topic in a later chapter.

Pay Attention to Warning Signs

Ignoring warning lights on a vehicle can lead to a breakdown. Similarly, failing to recognize warning signs in ministry relationships can lead to disaster. The Holy Spirit often gives quiet prompts when boundaries are being crossed. Ministers must remain sensitive to His voice and open to correction from trusted people. If a spouse or close friend expresses concern about a relationship, it should be taken seriously, not dismissed.

Lebacqz and Barton provide a checklist of warning signs for ministers (Trull and Creech 2017, 169):

The publicity test: what would others think?

Physical arousal: one’s own or the other’s

Inordinate sexual fantasy

Sexual gestures or body language

Intuition, instinct, or not feeling right

Wanting to share intimacies that are not called for

A parishioner wanting too much time or attention

Wanting to shift the focus to sexual subjects

Recognizing and responding to these signs early can help prevent moral failure.

Having a Healthy Life

In criminal investigations, motive and opportunity are essential factors. The same is true in cases of ministerial misconduct. While setting boundaries helps reduce opportunity, maintaining a healthy life helps reduce motivation. Many affairs begin because of unmet emotional or relational needs. For married ministers, a healthy marriage is the best safeguard against temptation. For single ministers, life-giving friendships, emotional health, and spiritual discipline are essential.

Leaders who care for themselves well are better positioned to lead others. Keeping one’s spiritual, emotional, and physical “tanks” full provides strength to resist temptation and make wise decisions. Preventing misconduct involves minimizing both motive and opportunity.

When the Minister Is Single

Even when both the minister and the congregant are single, clear boundaries are still necessary. In cases involving married individuals, the boundaries are often more obvious due to the covenant of marriage. However, being unmarried does not remove the responsibility to live a pure life. Single ministers are called to the same biblical standard as married ministers and must model God-honoring behavior in all relationships.

Scripture clearly teaches that sexual intimacy belongs within the covenant of marriage. This teaching stands in contrast to modern cultural norms where premarital sexual activity is often portrayed as normal and expected. If ministers are not careful, their understanding of sexuality can be shaped more by entertainment and public opinion than by the Word of God. A Christian who enters into a dating relationship must do so with intentionality, committing in advance to live with integrity and purity. It is important to have open conversations about abstinence, avoid situations that invite temptation, and remain accountable to trusted spiritual mentors.

Single ministers must also be mindful of the influence and authority they carry. Even if a romantic relationship is not forbidden, it should be approached with caution, prayer, and wise counsel. Power dynamics can affect how others perceive the relationship. If the relationship ends poorly, it may lead to discomfort or cause the other person to leave the church. In some cases, congregants may develop romanticized feelings toward a leader simply because that leader has been kind or helpful in a time of need. Ministers must remain discerning, humble, and accountable when navigating relationships within the church.

Children’s and Youth Ministry

Healthy boundaries are especially critical when serving in children’s and youth ministry. Creating and maintaining a safe environment for young people must be a top priority for every church. Sadly, one of the most common legal claims brought against churches involves sexual misconduct with minors. Youth and children’s workers are sometimes young, inexperienced, or undertrained, which increases the need for clear instruction and supervision.

Later in this course, we will examine how churches can implement systems to protect minors. For now, the focus is on how you, as a minister or leader, can personally establish solid, protective boundaries in your work with children and youth. The fact that you are taking this course demonstrates your commitment to honoring God and protecting others—and may prevent you from making a devastating mistake in this sensitive area.

A valuable resource on this topic is the 2018 publication Youth Ministry in a #MeToo Culture: Setting and Keeping Healthy Relational Boundaries by Church Law & Tax . In it, attorney Frank Sommerville outlines seven boundary guidelines that help protect both students and adult leaders. The summaries below are based on his recommendations (Church Law and Tax 2018, 48–53).

The “Never Alone” Rule

Leaders should avoid being alone with a student. Always meet minors in the presence of a third party. In one-on-one mentoring settings, choose open or public spaces. This principle applies to all forms of communication—including texting and social media. Avoid private messaging entirely.

One area where this rule is often overlooked is transportation. Do not ride alone with a teen in a vehicle, even if they need a ride. Doing so puts your future and the students’ safety at risk. In the case study, failure to follow this guideline led directly to misconduct.

The “Opposite Sex” Rule

While it may seem obvious, adult leaders must be intentional about maintaining appropriate emotional boundaries with teens of the opposite sex. What may feel like a friendly or harmless interaction with the adult can easily be misunderstood by a student as a romantic interest. To safeguard against this, leaders should regularly seek feedback from fellow team members about their communication and behavior. If a colleague expresses concern, the leader should be willing to adjust accordingly. This is especially important when working with teens who are emotionally vulnerable or seeking affirmation.

Adolescents often experience intense emotions and may form attachments to trusted adults. A teen might develop a crush on a leader simply because that leader has been kind, supportive, or spiritually influential. If those feelings are not returned, the teen may respond with hurt or anger, which can sometimes result in false or exaggerated accusations, even legal ones.

Leaders must also exercise caution when working with same-sex students, particularly when issues of identity or sexuality are involved. A teen who is uncertain or confused about his or her sexual identity may misinterpret attention as emotional or romantic support. While it is appropriate to provide biblical counsel, leaders must guard against forming emotionally dependent or inappropriate connections.

The “More Time” Rule

Pay attention to how much time you spend with any one student. Excessive or unstructured time outside of official ministry activities can blur boundaries. If a student begins to seek out your attention frequently, it may be a sign that something unhealthy is forming. Track these interactions, and when needed, involve supervisors and the student’s parents for accountability and clarity.

The “Conditional Confidentiality” Rule

Ministers and leaders must never promise absolute confidentiality. You are a mandatory reporter and must report any suspicion of abuse, self-harm, harm to others, or elder abuse as required by law. Confidentiality must yield to legal and ethical responsibilities. This protects both the students and the church.

The “Accountability” Rule

Youth leaders need systems of accountability. Every leader should follow the Child Safety Guidelines and Policies established by the church. Accountability promotes safety, provides clarity, and protects the reputation of the minister and the church. Leaders should welcome oversight and regular evaluation of their conduct.

The “No Porn” Rule

While this may seem obvious, it is worth stating clearly: students should be warned about the spiritual and emotional dangers of pornography. Leaders should model purity and speak truthfully about the destructive nature of pornography in a compassionate and honest manner.

The “No Fear” Rule

The decision-making center of the brain does not fully develop until around the age of twenty-five. This helps explain why many teenagers engage in risky behavior—they often feel invincible and underestimate potential consequences. While this is a normal part of adolescent development, it places a special responsibility on youth leaders to remain vigilant.

Leaders must be alert to unsafe activities, poor judgment, or impulsive behavior. Their role is not only to protect students from harm but also to guard themselves against accusations of negligent supervision. If a student begins engaging in risky behavior, the leader must respond promptly and firmly. In some cases, it may be necessary to cancel an activity entirely to ensure everyone’s safety. Taking decisive action, even when inconvenient, demonstrates wisdom and protects both the student and the ministry.

**Q:** What are some ways you can have a healthy life, which will help avoid unmet needs and the temptation to engage in unethical sexual behavior?

**A:** If you are single, build strong, supportive friendships and maintain emotional and spiritual health. If you are married, invest in your marriage by spending meaningful time together, communicating openly, and resolving conflicts in a healthy manner. Whether single or married, pay attention to emotional or spiritual emptiness and seek counseling or mentorship when needed.

#### 5.3.3 — Identify essential boundaries for children and youth ministries, including the 'Never Alone' rule.

Ministers rarely begin ministry intending to fall into sexual misconduct. Yet failure often comes not from sudden decisions but from neglecting safeguards. Just as a ship’s captain does not plan to crash into rocks but may do so when tired or ignoring warning systems, ministers must not assume they are immune to temptation. Healthy boundaries are like lighthouses that protect ministers from hidden dangers and guide them safely in their calling.

Boundaries do more than prevent immoral actions. They also protect a minister’s reputation from false accusations and the appearance of impropriety. Scripture instructs ministers to be “above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2), which means living in such a way that others cannot reasonably question their character. When ministers recognize warning signs, respect others’ boundaries, and build a culture of accountability, they protect themselves and strengthen those around them.

This lesson will explore the value of boundaries, practical safeguards in ministry settings, and how to recognize potential warning signs. It will also address special considerations for single ministers and those working with youth and children.

The Value of Boundaries

Boundaries are essential tools for wise and effective ministry. They provide clarity about what is appropriate, protect emotional and relational health, and help ministers avoid compromising situations. Clear boundaries also build trust among those served, demonstrating that the minister can be relied upon to act with integrity and wisdom.

In addition to guarding against personal failure, boundaries protect others. They create safe spaces where people can receive care without confusion or concern. Boundaries also reduce the likelihood of misinterpretation or suspicion, which is especially important in settings where power dynamics are present. The call to be “above reproach” (1 Timothy 3:2) includes not only avoiding sin but also avoiding situations that could be misread by others.

Finally, when ministers are intentional about their own boundaries, they are better equipped to encourage healthy patterns in others. Being sensitive to signals of discomfort, inappropriate behavior, or emotional dependence helps foster a culture of accountability and grace. In this way, boundaries do not limit ministry; they strengthen it by preserving the credibility, safety, and spiritual health of both the minister and the community.

Safeguards Related to Being Alone with Another Person

One key boundary is to avoid being alone with a person of the opposite sex in a private setting, especially during pastoral care. When possible, it is better to meet in groups or public spaces. This protects against temptation and helps others feel safe. It also guards against perceptions of impropriety. For example, some ministers choose not to ride in a vehicle alone with someone of the opposite gender who is not a family member—not because of distrust, but to protect the reputations of both.

If possible, include a trusted third person in the meeting. Visibility is a key element in maintaining accountability.

Additionally, if you are offering pastoral care but are not a licensed professional counselor, it is advisable to refer the individual to a qualified counselor after the third visit and discontinue further one-on-one sessions. This step helps ensure that the person receives the support they need while also protecting the integrity of the pastoral relationship.

I have served alongside women on staff, have led teams that included women, and have also served under the leadership of a woman. These experiences have demonstrated that men and women can partner effectively in ministry when clear boundaries are in place.

Instead, we should celebrate the shared call to the ministry while remaining committed to practices that ensure integrity.
