Balancing ministry demands with the needs of your family requires biblical wisdom, self-awareness, and intentional effort. Remember, your children are not merely part of your ministry—they are your first and most important disciples. Guiding them to walk with God, especially under the unique weight of ministry life, is one of the most meaningful investments you can make.

**Q:** How did the author’s response to when his son pulled the fire alarm make his son feel? How would his son have felt if he had been publicly reprimanded?

**A:** The author’s gracious response made his son feel valued and secure. Had he responded with public anger or embarrassment, the child would likely have felt discouraged, shamed, and devalued. It was an opportunity to model grace in the face of an honest mistake.

**Q:** If you have children, what are some strategies you use to ensure there is quality family time? If you do not have children, how do you remain connected to your family and friends?

**A:** Answers will vary. They may include setting aside one evening each week for family night, protecting mealtimes together, and limiting ministry commitments during family vacations or special events.

**Q:** What are some practical ways a minister can protect the privacy of their children?

**A:** Avoid sharing stories that reflect negatively on your children. For other stories, seek their permission before speaking publicly. Respect their right to privacy, and allow them space to grow without unwanted attention.

### Single Adults and Ministry Ethics

#### 7.3.1 — Describe the Assemblies of God's position on employment issues such as fair pay, marital status, and employee rights, and explain how these reflect ethical commitments to justice.

Broadening the Church for the Unmarried

Current U.S. Census data indicates that about half of American adults are unmarried. Among Assemblies of God credentialed ministers, 5.9 percent are single—either never married, divorced, or widowed. With 94.1 percent of ministers being married, it is easy for church culture to cater primarily to married adults. This may show up in phrases like “a family of families,” sermons that assume a married audience, watch night services concluding with “family communion,” or informal norms that assume only married individuals are eligible for board or leadership positions. These practices, though often unintentional, can marginalize single adults and create barriers to both belonging and leadership.

Single ministers who are socially healthy can model for the wider church how to engage well with all demographics—married or single. Their presence can help break stereotypes, enrich the leadership culture, and demonstrate that unmarried individuals are not only capable of serving in significant roles, but can also attract and disciple other single adults who are seeking to follow Christ in a meaningful and faithful way.

Case Study

Since its founding, New Life Assembly had always maintained a church board composed of seven married men. This structure became familiar and comfortable. When the board was presented during prayer, installation, or recognition ceremonies, it was easy to refer to “our board members and their wives.” Social gatherings were equally natural, with seven couples conversing easily about married life, family, and shared routines. Over time, this configuration became a cultural norm—one that went unchallenged.

Tom, a respected church member, was nominated by a fellow congregant to serve on the board. He was a committed follower of Christ—wise, dependable, and deeply engaged in church life. He tithed faithfully, served consistently, and participated in overseas missions. However, Tom’s nomination did not advance. The reason given was that he was unmarried and therefore ineligible. To justify this, leaders pointed to 1 Timothy 3:2: “Now the overseer is to be above reproach, faithful to his wife.” They interpreted this to mean that being married was a requirement for leadership.

This interpretation reflected not a biblical standard, but a long-standing tradition shaped by personal comfort and familiarity. Using Scripture to uphold preferences or cultural assumptions is not only poor exegesis—it can be spiritually abusive. It denies individuals a fair opportunity to serve, disregards their calling, and sends the message that only a certain “type” of person is welcome in leadership. It also robs the church of the diverse gifts God has placed in the body. One must ask: what blessing did that congregation miss by sidelining a faithful, qualified leader?

Beyond the individual harm, this kind of exclusion sends a broader message to others in the same demographic. Imagine a single professional, eager to grow spiritually and serve faithfully, stepping into a church culture where leadership and visibility seem reserved for married couples. The result is often disillusionment, disengagement, or departure.

This kind of unethical prejudice or preferential treatment—whether based on marital status, race, socioeconomic status, age, gender, or family ties—must be confronted. Scripture calls us to honor every member of the body of Christ, not just those who match a preferred profile. Cultural norms in a church are often shaped by just a few voices. Ask God to make you one of those voices—someone who uses influence to create space for others and reflect the inclusiveness of the gospel.

Among those who respected Tom, the quiet commentary was striking: “It’s a good thing Jesus or the apostle Paul did not attend our church. They would not have been allowed to serve on the board.” May we never allow tradition to speak louder than truth. Lord, help us do better.

Misconceptions about Hiring Single Adults

Working in ministry differs greatly from many other jobs, where employees simply clock in, complete their tasks, and go home with little personal interaction outside the workplace. In ministry, relationships often extend beyond office hours, and personal dynamics carry weight. Because of this, it is essential for churches and ministry organizations to establish and follow clear human resource policies that uphold ethical standards and prevent bias. When hiring, leadership must be especially mindful not to let assumptions about marital status influence decisions. Unfortunately, several misconceptions about single adults continue to persist in ministry contexts.

1. Single adults pose a higher risk for moral failure.

This assumption is not only inaccurate—it is unfair. In reality, a single adult serving on a ministry team can provide a healthy example of how to maintain boundaries and walk with integrity as a Christ follower. Their presence offers the church a visible model of how men and women, regardless of marital status, can work together with mutual respect and shared purpose. Scripture refers to believers as brothers and sisters in Christ, which sets the standard for how we are to treat one another in every context—honorably, respectfully, and without partiality. When ministry colleagues relate as co-laborers rather than through assumptions tied to their marital status, they demonstrate to the wider church that spiritual maturity, not relationship status, qualifies someone for ministry. This kind of teamwork becomes a living witness of godly conduct and ethical relationships within the body of Christ.

2. Single adults should be willing to do the same work for less pay.

This misconception reflects an unethical approach to compensation. A fair wage should always be based on the responsibilities of the role—not on an employee’s marital status, perceived financial need, or personal circumstances. A healthy human resources process recognizes that pay is about the value of the work, not the demographic of the worker. When churches blur the lines between benevolence and compensation, they compromise both integrity and stewardship. Overpaying or underpaying someone based on who they are rather than what the job requires does a disservice to the ministry and the employee alike. Churches must resist the temptation to justify lower pay for single adults and instead commit to equitable compensation practices that reflect both biblical justice and professional ethics.

3. Single adults can work longer hours and weekends because they do not have a family to go home to.

This assumption is both unfair and unsustainable. It places an undue burden on single employees—sometimes imposed by leadership, and at other times self-imposed—without recognizing the long-term impact. Whether it is the single missionary expected to babysit while married colleagues go out or the single team member regularly asked to open and close the church because “families have to get their kids home,” such expectations are unethical. Ministry teams should operate with mutual respect and shared responsibility, regardless of marital status. Every staff member deserves to be treated with dignity and to have their time valued. Ethical leadership resists the urge to ask more of someone simply because they are single, recognizing that all employees—married or unmarried—need healthy boundaries and a balanced life.

Being Aware of Others When Looking at Your Marital Status

Married individuals—especially those who married young, have been married for many years, or mostly socialize with other married couples—may unintentionally carry assumptions about single adults. These blind spots can lead to inaccurate and sometimes hurtful beliefs, such as thinking something must be wrong with a person who is not married, assuming every single person is actively looking for a spouse, or believing that singles are less capable of forming deep relationships. Other misconceptions may include the idea that single adults lack meaningful hobbies, friendships, or personal fulfillment. While often unintentional, such assumptions can marginalize others and undermine their value within the body of Christ.

Grow in curiosity and respect for those who are different from you. One of the essential skills for ministers is the ability to ask thoughtful questions, listen well, and engage in meaningful relationships with people whose experiences differ from their own. This kind of awareness is especially important as we seek to represent Christ in diverse settings. When married couples build friendships with single colleagues or church members, it is wise to talk together as a couple about how those interactions feel and to remain aware of any insecurities that may surface. It is never appropriate to tease or embarrass your spouse in front of others. Instead, honor one another’s feelings and be cautious not to drift into situations that could create misunderstanding or distrust. Practicing this kind of mutual care not only strengthens your marriage but also communicates genuine respect to those around you—modeling Christlike behavior in both private and public relationships.

Boundaries to Consider for Single Ministers

Although many of these principles also apply to married ministers, single adults often carry a greater responsibility to manage boundaries intentionally. As someone who has served in ministry while remaining single throughout life (never married), I have learned that proactive self-awareness and planning are essential. The following insights reflect practical lessons that have helped me maintain integrity, build trust, and thrive in ministry settings. You may find them helpful as you navigate your own journey in ministry as a single adult.

Build a Diverse Team

Team selection should begin with assessing gifting, skills, knowledge, and experience. However, building a healthy ministry team also involves intentional diversity. Teams function best when members bring a variety of perspectives shaped by differences in age, race, gender, marital status, and life experience. While this is important for any ministry leader, it is especially valuable for single ministers who may need to be more intentional about creating balance and relatability within the team.

When I was first hired as a youth associate pastor, the church had never employed someone who was single nor female in that role. My lead pastor wisely advised me to surround myself with married couples who were respected by the congregation and who supported me personally. I chose couples from a variety of age groups and social circles within the church. That group of about a dozen individuals became a strong and trusted team.

Years later, when I planted a church, I carried that lesson forward. I deliberately built a board and staff that included people unlike me—married and single, younger and older, racially diverse, and from various backgrounds. My associate pastor, Gary, was a married man with four adult children and eight grandchildren. He led the men’s ministry and preached occasionally, offering perspectives I could not. While many team members were married, it was completely normal for single adults to serve in high levels of leadership as well.

A well-rounded team is one that can rely on one another—whether it is for a hospital visit, a counseling situation, or a sensitive conversation. When each person brings unique strengths, the ministry becomes more effective, and the congregation benefits from leadership that reflects the full body of Christ.

Be Mindful of the Highest Integrity as a Dating Minister

When showing Christian care or attention to someone, a minister must be aware that it can be easily misinterpreted—especially by a single parishioner who may believe there is romantic interest, even if no such message was intended. Always remember that you are a minister first. Take care not to unintentionally mislead someone, as clarifying misunderstandings is difficult in any situation, and even more so when spiritual leadership is involved.

If you are genuinely interested in someone socially—particularly someone within the church you serve—recognize how complicated that can become. Being known as a “dating pastor” may compromise your credibility, distract from your calling, and create discomfort or confusion within the congregation. If the relationship ends, it often results in the individual leaving the church, along with lingering opinions from others.

This dynamic is not unlike two schoolteachers dating in view of their students—what begins as private quickly becomes public. For this reason, it is wise to keep your dating life as private and separate as possible from your ministry context.

All Christians are called to pursue relationships with integrity and purity. Ministers carry an even greater responsibility to avoid even the appearance of impropriety. Consider what proactive boundaries you can set to avoid common missteps in dating. Some may consider your caution excessive, but spiritual maturity often requires restraint. The enemy would love nothing more than to discredit your witness or derail your ministry through sexual compromise. Safeguard your calling by conducting yourself with honor in every relationship.

Honor the Institution of Marriage in Others

Because God places high value on the covenant of marriage, we must also show respect for it—even if we are not married ourselves. As single adults in ministry, this means being mindful of the commitments and responsibilities that married colleagues carry and taking care not to interfere with or diminish those obligations. At times, single individuals may speak casually or critically about marriage which can unintentionally discourage or dishonor others. Instead, we should uphold marriage as a sacred calling, even if it is not part of our own story.

Additionally, whatever boundaries your church or organization has established for interactions between male and female coworkers, it is important—especially as a single adult—to follow them with intentionality. These boundaries are not just about policy; they protect relationships, preserve trust, and communicate honor. By demonstrating respect for marriage in how we speak and interact, we reflect godly maturity and strengthen the relational health of the ministry team.

Pay Attention to How You Speak of Your Status

At times, single adults—especially those who strongly desire to be married—can become consumed by that pursuit. They may frequently bring up their single status, request introductions or blind dates, or allow conversations to repeatedly return to the topic. When this happens, it can distract from their ministry and make others feel uncomfortable.

As leaders, our identity must be grounded in Christ, not in any one personal trait or status. The way we talk about ourselves—through illustrations, casual remarks, or the topics we repeatedly highlight—shapes how others perceive us. If we constantly reference a favorite sports team, political view, ethnic background, or desire for marriage, we risk being defined primarily by that lens. Scripture calls us to be “all things to all people” so that we might reach them for Christ (1 Corinthians 9:22). Staying aware of how we present ourselves helps ensure that our influence remains centered on the gospel rather than overshadowed by personal themes.

Social Media Trap

Modesty, appropriateness, and privacy are most easily compromised on social media. Before posting, pause and consider how your words or images might impact others—parents, young people, church members, leaders, or those who do not yet know Christ. Remember that online content lives indefinitely. What seems harmless today may become a source of regret years later—especially if your life circumstances change, such as marrying someone other than a person you previously dated publicly.

Sometimes integrity means surrendering your right to say or post something, choosing instead the higher values of ethics, honor, and discretion. If you are unsure, let the post sit overnight and revisit it with fresh eyes. Better yet, ask an accountability mentor for guidance. As you post, comment, or respond online, aim to reflect a public image that honors not only your ministry and church but most of all the Lord Jesus Christ. Social media is a powerful platform—use it in a way that builds trust and models the character of Christ.

Conclusion

Serving in ministry as a single adult presents both unique opportunities and distinct challenges. While many church environments are structured around the experiences of married individuals, single ministers bring valuable perspectives, gifts, and leadership to the body of Christ. By setting wise boundaries, practicing relational awareness, and committing to integrity in both private and public life, single ministers can thrive and lead with credibility.

At the same time, married ministers and church leaders are called to recognize and honor the contributions of their single colleagues. Creating space for diverse voices and experiences within ministry teams reflects the heart of Christ and strengthens the church’s ability to reach people from all walks of life.

Whether you are single or married, your calling remains the same: to serve the Lord with integrity, to respect others in every stage of life, and to pursue ministry that reflects both the compassion and the holiness of Jesus. In doing so, you help build a church culture where every believer is valued, equipped, and empowered to fulfill their God-given purpose.

**Q:** In what ways can a single minister be a model to the rest of the church of how men and women treat each other?

**A:** A single minister can demonstrate appropriate, ethical boundaries in relationships with the opposite sex. Their conduct can serve as a model of healthy interactions and integrity in dating and friendships, setting a godly example for others to follow.

**Q:** What are some benefits to having a diverse team on your staff, including both single and married individuals?

**A:** Diversity on a ministry team brings a variety of perspectives, experiences, and relational insights. This enables the team to connect more effectively with a broader range of people and increases the church’s ability to minister across life stages and situations. UNIT w 3

## 8

### Being a Good Teammate

#### 8.1.1 — Identify the types of discipline that dismissed or suspended ministers face and explain how these restrictions protect the integrity of the AG fellowship.

Ministry is not meant to be done alone. From Jesus and His disciples to the early church, we see the power of working together in unity. Healthy teams strengthen the church and help fulfill its mission.

This lesson focuses on how to serve well on a ministry team—through humility, shared vision, and mutual support.

Healthy Staff Dynamics

Church staff dynamics can either support longevity in ministry or derail it altogether. Many ministers have left their roles—or even their calling—after encountering broken trust, unfair treatment, or unresolved tension within a church team. Likewise, lead pastors may experience deep disappointment when a staff member is disloyal or fails to fulfill responsibilities. Ethical missteps often lie at the root of such breakdowns. Thankfully, many of these issues are preventable when both leaders and staff commit to honoring one another and communicating well.

Ethical Considerations for the Pastor or Ministry Lead

An ethical leader will always treat staff members with respect and dignity. When correction is necessary, it should be done privately and gently, in a way that preserves the individual’s sense of value. A wise principle to follow is to affirm publicly and correct privately. Unfortunately, some leaders choose to issue veiled rebukes in public settings—making general statements clearly directed at a particular staff member without naming them. This approach is harmful. Most people recognize who is being targeted, and the result is embarrassment and shame. This kind of public correction undermines trust and should be avoided entirely.

In the same way, a leader should never speak negatively about a staff member to others in the congregation or to ministry colleagues. Doing so damages relationships, harms the staff person’s reputation, and creates a culture of suspicion. A good leader sets the example by treating others the way they would want to be treated—with fairness, compassion, and discretion. As discussed in a previous chapter, spiritual abuse includes using authority in manipulative or controlling ways. Ethical leaders must reject such behavior and instead lead in a manner that reflects the heart of Christ.

It is also important to value your staff members as individuals, not just for the roles they fill. Show genuine care for their well-being and for the well-being of their families. Respect their time and establish healthy boundaries. For example, avoid sending messages or making nonurgent calls after work hours. Emergencies will happen, and ministry occasionally requires flexibility. However, it is wise to protect your staff’s personal time whenever possible. Leaders who demonstrate this kind of care earn the trust and loyalty of their team. Staff members will give their best when they know they are seen, appreciated, and supported.

Part of ethical leadership includes setting clear expectations. Provide each staff member with a job description that outlines their responsibilities, expected office hours, standards for behavior and appearance, and any other relevant policies. Open communication is essential. When a staff member is not meeting expectations, meet with them in a respectful and constructive manner. Clearly outline the areas that need improvement and offer a reasonable timeline. Provide any necessary training or support that may help them succeed. While there may come a time when the staff member must be released, every effort should be made to help them grow and meet their expectations before taking that step.

Investing in Staff Development

Healthy leaders are not threatened by the growth of others; they encourage it. Jesus mentored His disciples by walking closely with them. In the same way, ministry leaders should invest time in developing their staff. Use team meetings not only for logistics but for spiritual formation and leadership training. Encourage staff to read books, attend conferences, or engage in development opportunities. Involve them in strategic moments, such as hosting guest speakers, leading prayer times, or representing the church at key events.

Celebrate staff achievements both publicly and privately. A team member who feels valued is more likely to remain faithful and effective. When a pastor is known as an encourager and developer of people, others are drawn to serve alongside them. Conversely, if a pastor withholds praise or always takes credit, staff will grow discouraged.

If a staff member senses God’s leading to another ministry role, honor their discernment. When they come to you for guidance, recognize the trust they are placing in you. Even if it means losing a valued team member, it helps them process the decision and move forward with blessing. This posture honors God and builds a reputation for generosity and trust. You do not own your staff; they are entrusted to you for a season. A leader who stewards people well will always attract more gifted individuals.

Working Together on a Team

Every staff member must remember that they serve on two teams, the team they lead and the team they are part of. For example, a youth pastor leads the youth ministry team, which may include staff, volunteers, student leaders, and coordinators. Leading this team is vital to the health and growth of that ministry and its contribution to the broader church. At the same time, the youth pastor is also a member of the larger pastoral team, led by the lead pastor. This leadership team often includes roles such as the children’s pastor, worship pastor, business administrator, and others who share responsibility for the overall direction and unity of the church.

It is natural for a staff pastor to devote their best energy to the ministry they lead. After all, they want it to flourish. However, this focus can become a problem if it causes the ministry to operate in isolation. A team that functions independently from the rest of the church may begin to drift from the shared vision. Worse still, it may form a silo—operating with its own goals, priorities, and culture. When that happens, the staff members may start to feel they are competing with other ministries for space, budget, volunteers, or visibility.

This type of thinking undermines the health of the whole church. Ministries should not be in competition with one another; they are parts of the same body. There is wisdom in the saying, “The team you are on is more important than the team you lead.” As a staff member, your first loyalty must be to the leadership team of the church. This means fully supporting the senior pastor and embracing the church’s overall mission and vision. Your own ministry should grow out of that shared foundation, not apart from it.

Support your fellow staff members. Celebrate their victories. Offer help when they face challenges. The more unified the church’s leadership team is, the more unified the individual ministries will be. As a staff pastor, you need to support and buy into the overall vision of the church and allow the vision for the ministry you are leading to flow from that. When you invest in the success of your fellow team members, you also strengthen your own ministry. Unity among staff not only brings blessing to the entire church, but it also models Christlike leadership for the people you lead.

**Q:** What are some practical ways a lead pastor can encourage a staff person?

**A:** A lead pastor can encourage a staff person by speaking words of affirmation, providing opportunities for growth and training, spending intentional time together, and showing that their input is valued and respected.

**Q:** What would be the consequences of a staff person not valuing the team they are on?

**A:** If a staff person does not value the ministry team they are on, they may become disconnected from the vision of the lead pastor. Their ministry could become isolated, functioning independently rather than in alignment with the overall mission of the church. This may lead to tension or division among team members.

### Normalizing Men and Women Serving Together

#### 8.2.1 — Describe the role of the AG district and national leadership in overseeing ministerial integrity and explain the importance of accountability structures.

The church is at its best when men and women serve together as partners in ministry. This reflects God’s design and fulfills His purposes for the body of Christ. Unfortunately, some ministry environments have been slow to model this vision clearly. This lesson seeks to normalize the shared leadership of men and women, not only by offering theological support, but also by providing ethical guidelines and practical examples. The goal is to move beyond simply believing in inclusivity to actually demonstrating it at every level of ministry life.

Valuing Men and Women Serving Together

Before we can address the ethics of serving together, we must first affirm that it is biblical and intentional. The Assemblies of God supports the full participation of women in ministry and outlines this stance in its official position paper, “Women in Ministerial Leadership.” This document explains why we are doctrinally egalitarian, not complementarian. However, our belief must go beyond documents. What we teach should also be lived out in local churches, seen in leadership teams, and modeled at every level of ministry. As the saying goes, more is caught than taught.

If a minister is unclear on the biblical foundation for women in ministry, ethical cooperation between men and women will be difficult to achieve. Insecure environments hinder effective partnerships. For example, in Why Not Women? A Biblical Study of Women in Missions, Ministry, and Leadership , Loren Cunningham and David Hamilton offer a thorough biblical and cultural defense of women in leadership. Similarly, Dr. Craig Keener’s Paul, Women, & Wives: Marriage and Women’s Ministry in the Letters of Paul provides valuable insight into Paul’s writings on gender and ministry. These resources can help lay a strong theological foundation.

Early in my ministry, I was told by a local pastor, “I’m glad you were hired, but I don’t trust myself to work with women.” This kind of statement reveals a double standard. In almost every workplace—from hospitals to courtrooms—men and women work side by side. The church should be a model of ethical, respectful interaction. What better way to help them ethically interact as Christians than to see it modeled well at church. Teach from Scripture, then model what it looks like for your youth group or congregation for men and women to treat one another with respect and collegiality as coworkers.

Where can believers better learn how to interact with the opposite gender in the workplace than from the example set by their ministry leaders? When pastors and church staff model integrity, professionalism, mutual respect, and courtesy in their daily work, they offer a powerful testimony to their congregation. These behaviors not only preserve a morally safe environment in the church but also serve as a guide for how men and women in the congregation can honor Christ in their own places of employment. Ethical ministry leadership is not only about teaching from the pulpit; it is also about setting an example in how we work together. When men and women serve side by side in ministry in healthy and appropriate ways, the entire body of Christ benefits. Truly, we are better together.

After serving for ten years as a youth pastor at Highway Tabernacle, I was offered a position as an associate pastor at a church that, in its seventy-five-year history, had never included a woman on the pastoral staff. As we learned together what it meant to move beyond an all-male team, the lead pastor began receiving positive feedback from people inside and outside the church. Many expressed appreciation for the nontraditional step of hiring a female associate pastor. It became clear that a more diverse team brought fresh perspective and strength to our leadership.

There are many practical benefits to having both men and women on a pastoral team. In situations involving sensitive counseling or leadership conversations, it is helpful to involve a colleague of the opposite gender. When making hospital visits, for example, there are times when a male pastor may feel it is more appropriate for a female colleague to take the lead—such as when visiting a patient undergoing a procedure related to women’s health. These considerations reflect care, wisdom, and respect for those we serve.

It is also worth noting that moral failures involving inappropriate relationships tend to occur more frequently between a pastor and a congregant or counselee, not between ministry colleagues. When managed properly with appropriate safeguards, mixed-gender staff teams contribute to a healthier and more accountable environment. Gender-diverse leadership can actually increase moral safety, not decrease it.

The Assemblies of God Network of Women Ministers affirms this principle clearly: “The image of God is best reflected, and the church of Jesus Christ is healthiest when both men and women are empowered to fulfill their calling at every level of ministerial leadership.” When this belief is not only affirmed but practiced, it brings strength, balance, and integrity to the church.

Ethical Guidelines for Men and Women Serving Together

When I joined the executive leadership team at the General Council as the first woman to serve in that capacity, the Executive Presbyters—who function as the Board of Directors—requested that a formal policy be developed to guide male and female interactions in the workplace. Your ministry should consider doing the same. Developing clear policies is a proactive way to address questions and establish expectations before issues arise. A strong policy gives everyone a shared set of guidelines and provides accountability across the organization. It protects fairness and consistency by removing the need to make decisions based on personalities or react to individual incidents.

These policies should not be limited to male and female interaction. A healthy ministry culture provides written guidelines, training, and expectations for all leaders, regardless of gender. Everyone should be expected to follow practices that promote safety, integrity, and professionalism. Some common examples include leaving doors open during one-on-one meetings, avoiding private travel with only one other person, and always treating others with courtesy and respect. These principles reduce the opportunity for inappropriate behavior, eliminate the appearance of impropriety, and protect the reputation of the individuals and the ministry.

While some may prefer a detailed list of dos and don’ts—or sample policies ready to apply—it is often more helpful to focus on principles that can guide ethical decision-making. No policy manual can anticipate every situation. As culture continues to shift, ministers must be able to think biblically, apply sound judgment, and act in ways that reflect Christ’s character. Ethical leadership requires discernment and consistency more than rule-following alone.

In many church circles, the “Billy Graham Rule” has been widely adopted. As a traveling evangelist, Billy Graham established a personal standard that included never being alone with a woman who was not his wife. Over the years, I have been asked whether this rule should still be followed. Some wonder if it is outdated or impractical. Others see it as a necessary safeguard. Through reflection, I have come to see that while the rule served a specific purpose in its original context, it may not always be helpful—or fair—when applied universally. Like using the wrong tool for a task, applying a one-size-fits-all rule to every situation can create confusion or even cause harm. We need thoughtful, contextual solutions, not just inherited policies.

As we think about men and women serving together in ministry, here are four key considerations:

The workplace has changed.

Seventy years ago, men and women rarely worked together in fields such as law enforcement, the military, or higher education. Today, shared leadership roles are common across every sector—including the church. Ministry environments must adapt to this reality with grace and wisdom.

Past practices were shaped by a different theology.

In many settings, previous ministry standards were built on complementarian theology—the belief that Scripture does not allow women to hold leadership positions in ministry. In such environments, women often served in support roles rather than as co-laborers in leadership. As Assemblies of God ministers, we affirm an egalitarian view rooted in Scripture. This belief must be reflected in our structures and relationships.

Understand the difference in relationships.

Ministers must learn to distinguish between relationships with congregants and those with fellow staff members. In relationships with congregants or counselees, there is often a power imbalance that requires extra care, accountability, and protection. In relationships among ministry colleagues, mutual respect and professionalism should guide behavior.

Responsibility is shared by both men and women.

In any relationship where there is a difference in authority or power, the person in the higher position bears greater responsibility. All ministers—regardless of gender—must take this responsibility seriously. We are called to protect those we lead, guard the integrity of our ministry relationships, and ensure that no one is harmed by the misuse of influence or position.

How to Promote Inclusion

Having considered the biblical foundation for women in ministry and the ethical guidelines for serving together, it is important to explore practical steps to promote a ministry culture where men and women are empowered to lead side by side. The inclusion is not automatic. It must be pursued intentionally through self-awareness, thoughtful language, and consistent practice.

Being Self-Aware

Each person brings their own background, experiences, and assumptions into ministry. Leaders must regularly examine their personal biases, preferences, and history. This includes recognizing any tendencies that may hinder fairness or undervalue others based on gender. Self-awareness is the first step toward creating a culture that is biblical, inclusive, appreciative of differences, and honoring to God.

Failing to be self-aware can lead to subtle or even unintentional forms of exclusion. Consider this exaggerated but telling example: “Melissa, welcome to the church board. Would you make the coffee, bring some snacks, and take the meeting minutes for Tom? He hates being the secretary. We’re progressive—we have a woman on the board now.” While said jokingly, statements like this reflect an outdated mindset and can undermine the leadership and contribution of female team members.

Use Thoughtful and Accurate Language

Words shape culture. The language we use in ministry must reflect fairness and accuracy. For example, when speaking publicly, use the term ministers and spouses rather than pastors and wives . The word ministers include pastors, missionaries, college professors, ministry leaders, and many others. It is also more accurate, especially since nearly 30 percent of Assemblies of God credentialed ministers are women. Additionally, use “if your spouse is with you” to account for single ministers (nearly 6 percent of AG ministers) and for women who serve in ministry alongside their husbands.

Titles also matter. If a man and a woman both hold doctorates and serve in academic or ministry settings, do not refer to him as Dr. Johnson and her as Miss Debbie . Show the same professional respect to both. If a woman holds AG credentials, fulfills the responsibilities of a pastor, and serves in a role equal to others on staff who are called pastor, she should be referred to as such—even if her formal title is Children’s Director or Music Arts Director . Equal work and equal calling deserve equal recognition.

Special occasions like Pastor Appreciation Day should also be evaluated. If a male pastor receives a financial gift and a female pastor receives only flowers, this communicates a disparity in how their roles are valued. Be intentional and consistent in how you honor all staff, regardless of gender.

Open Doors for One Another—Not Just Literally

In ministry, the phrase “opening a door” refers to more than just a physical gesture. It often means making room for someone else to lead, contribute, or grow. Opening doors in leadership may involve advocating for a colleague, recommending someone for a role, or creating opportunities that allow their gifts to be recognized. For example, a woman may not be considered for a committee position simply because no one has suggested her. Taking time to learn about your colleagues, their skills, interests, and calling—equips you to speak up for them when opportunities arise.

Both men and women share the responsibility of helping others gain access to platforms where they can serve. One way to do this is by paying attention to who is being included and who is not. If all the voices at the table sound the same, it may be time to widen the circle. Help create a culture where qualified individuals are not overlooked simply because they have not always been visible.

It is also helpful to understand that men and women may respond differently to new opportunities. Research and experience have shown that men are often willing to say “yes” to a new challenge even if they feel only partially prepared. In contrast, women are more likely to decline unless they feel fully qualified and confident. This means that when we invite, train, and encourage others, we may need to use different approaches based on the individual. Taking time to provide reassurance and specific encouragement can help someone step into a calling they might otherwise doubt.

Effective ministry requires intentionality. When leaders are intentional about recognizing potential and affirming one another’s contributions, the entire church benefits. Whether it is through personal encouragement, formal mentoring, or public recommendation, opening doors for others strengthens the team and honors the calling God has placed on each life.

Mentor Equally and Thoughtfully

Mentoring is a key part of developing healthy ministry leaders. It should be available to both men and women, not based on gender, but on calling, interests, and ministry needs. Every minister benefits from intentional guidance, encouragement, and support.

At times, women in ministry struggle to find appropriate mentors. For example, a woman called to preach was once assigned to the pastor’s wife—who, while kind and faithful, had no experience in preaching or pastoral leadership. This mismatch discouraged her from continuing in her calling.

Effective mentoring must be purposeful. Assign mentors based on the mentee’s gifts, not assumptions. Women called to pastoral ministry should be mentored by those who understand that role. When mentors are chosen thoughtfully, both men and women can thrive in their calling and contribute fully to the work of the church.

Consider Creative Mentoring Options

Mentoring does not have to follow a one-on-one model. There are creative and effective ways to foster growth through shared mentoring structures. One example is a mentoring trio—three individuals who learn from and support each other through intentional conversation and shared experience. Another option is to form a cohort of leaders who have a common calling or ministry focus. These groups provide space for encouragement, accountability, and mutual development.

Cohorts can be especially helpful in empowering underrepresented groups. For instance, Rick Ross, General Treasurer of the Assemblies of God, established a cohort specifically to train women called to be lead pastors. This initiative not only opens doors but also provides the tools and support needed for long-term ministry success. Mentoring efforts like this help shape future leaders and reflect the inclusive heart of the gospel.

### Treating Your Ministry Leader with Respect

#### 8.3.1 — Explain how conflicts within the church should be handled using AG resources and the ethical decision-making process.

Ministry is often done in teams, and many ministers will serve under the leadership of another pastor or ministry leader at some point in their calling. In these situations, it is essential to approach the relationship with humility, integrity, and a commitment to ethical conduct. Serving well under authority reflects both our spiritual maturity and our understanding of biblical leadership.

This lesson explores the attitudes and practices that lead to healthy staff-leader relationships, including loyalty, communication, and respect for decision-making authority. It also provides guidance on how to handle disagreements or conflict with a ministry leader in a way that honors Christ and protects the unity of the church. The final sections address how ministers should interact with neighboring churches and leaders in the broader body of Christ. Ethical ministry does not end at your own church door—it extends to how you speak about, collaborate with, and support others in the kingdom.

Serving Under Leadership

In ministry, you will often find yourself serving under the leadership of someone else. There are several important practices that will help you thrive in this role and serve your leader in an ethical and honorable way.

Work Diligently

Ministry demands your best effort, whether you are leading or serving under someone else’s leadership. It is unethical to give less effort simply because the responsibility does not ultimately rest on you. Paul instructed believers, “Whatever you do, work at it with all your heart, as working for the Lord. . . . It is the Lord Christ you are serving” (Colossians 3:23–24). Approach your assignment with excellence. Invest in your own development and commit to doing your part well.

Support Your Ministry Leader

One of the most helpful things you can do is keep your leader informed. If a problem arises in your ministry area, make sure the pastor hears about it from you directly—not from someone in the congregation. Catching a leader off guard puts them in a difficult position and limits their ability to support you. Also, do not entertain gossip or criticism about your leader from others. In our case study, Jared failed in this area. Instead, kindly redirect others to speak directly with the leader if they have concerns.

Stay Within Your Role

You may have creative ideas and insights to share. When you do, approach your ministry leader with humility and respect. Share your thoughts constructively, but if the leader decides not to move forward with your suggestion, accept the decision without resentment. The lead pastor or ministry supervisor carries responsibility for the entire church and likely sees things from a broader perspective than you can. Honor their leadership even when you may not fully agree.

Respond Well When in Conflict with Your Leader

Conflict with a pastor or ministry leader is not uncommon. You may disagree about the vision of the church, feel overlooked in decisions, or struggle with personality differences. At times, you may even feel hurt or mistreated. These tensions can arise in any ministry setting, but how you respond can make all the difference. There are many ways a relationship can go sideways between a ministry leader and a staff person. It is important to handle these situations in an ethical manner. Ethical responses protect the unity of the church and the integrity of your own calling.

The first and most important step is to seek the Lord in prayer. Ask for clarity, humility, and a heart that honors God. As the Psalmist wrote, “Search me, God, and know my heart; test me and know my anxious thoughts. See if there is any offensive way in me, and lead me in the way everlasting” (Psalm 139:23–24). Through prayer, God may reveal blind spots, offer peace, or redirect your perspective before you say or do anything else.

In most situations, your next step should be to talk directly with your ministry leader. There may be a simple misunderstanding or miscommunication that can be resolved through conversation. This step is consistent with our earlier model of “Clarifying the Situation.” Honest and respectful communication is often the bridge to restoration. Once you have had this conversation, continue praying for wisdom and clarity.

If, after prayer and conversation, you still feel unable to support your leader, it may be time to consider stepping away. Staying in a role where you cannot support the leadership may damage your health and the health of the church. However, if the situation involves serious mistreatment or abuse, your next step should be different. Rather than confronting the leader directly, seek counsel from a district official or a trusted, spiritually mature mentor. They can help assess whether confrontation is advisable or whether another course of action would be better for everyone involved.

In the midst of conflict, it is essential to avoid actions that can harm the church or your testimony. Acting from a place of pain or frustration can lead to regrettable decisions. Do not speak negatively about your leader or church on social media, in public gatherings, or in private conversations with congregants. Avoid trying to win others to your side or build a following. That approach may feel validating in the moment, but it ultimately creates division.

The story of Absalom is a powerful warning. He won favor with the people of Israel by subtly criticizing his father, King David, at the city gate. His goal was to undermine David’s leadership and take the throne for himself. His actions led to rebellion, sorrow, and judgment. Let this story serve as a reminder: do not be an Absalom. Even when you disagree with your leader, strive to maintain loyalty, humility, and integrity.

**Q:** When a minister is in conflict with a leader, what are some steps to take before making a decision to leave?

**A:** The minister should first seek the Lord in prayer for wisdom and clarity. It is important to have an honest and respectful conversation with the ministry leader and also seek counsel from a trusted and spiritually mature advisor. After receiving guidance, the minister should continue to pray and evaluate the situation before making a final decision.

### Exercising Matthew 18 Principles and Ministerial Courtesy

#### 8.5.1 — Describe the AG's complementarian position on gender and explain how it is grounded in a biblical view of human sexuality.

Conflict is inevitable in ministry, but how we respond to it reveals much about our maturity, ethics, and faith. Rather than responding with gossip or retaliation, ministers are called to follow Jesus’ model for reconciliation, as outlined in Matthew 18. This passage provides a wise and grace-filled path for addressing offenses between believers, including fellow ministers.

However, certain situations require additional sensitivity, particularly when abuse, power imbalances, or ethical violations are involved. Scripture never calls us to overlook justice or place someone at risk. Alongside Matthew 18 principles, ministers must also observe established ethical expectations, such as those outlined in the Assemblies of God Constitution and Bylaws regarding ministerial courtesy. When practiced together, these principles promote unity, protect the church, and glorify Christ.

Following Matthew 18 Principles

When a fellow minister sins against you, your first instinct might be to tell others about it. This is a common human reaction, but Jesus offers a better way. In Matthew 18:15–17, He instructs us to first go privately to the person who has wronged us:

“If your brother or sister sins, go and point out their fault, just between the two of you. If they listen to you, you have won them over. But if they will not listen, take one or two others along, so that ‘every matter may be established by the testimony of two or three witnesses.’ If they still refuse to listen, tell it to the church; and if they refuse to listen even to the church, treat them as you would a pagan or a tax collector” (Matthew 18:15–17).

Ken Sande, in his book The Peacemaker: A Biblical Guide to Resolving Personal Conflict , offers this insight: “A general principle taught in Matthew 18 is that we should try to keep the circle of people involved in a conflict as small as possible for as long as possible” (1991, 186).

Sande also notes that the first step is often deciding whether the issue is serious enough to warrant confrontation. Some offenses may be minor or unintentional, and it may be more loving and mature to overlook them. This decision should be made prayerfully, considering whether the offense involves clear sin or simply hurt feelings. A good resource for developing a biblical culture of peacemaking in your ministry is Ken Sande’s organization at https://www.rw360.org, which focuses on relational wisdom and conflict resolution.

If private conversation does not lead to reconciliation, Jesus instructs you to take one or two others with you. These individuals should be mature, trusted leaders who can facilitate healthy, biblical dialogue. Their purpose will be to help offer biblical counsel and facilitate communication.

If the conflict still remains unresolved, the matter may need to be brought to church leadership. This should be done discreetly—not from the pulpit or in public forums. When both ministers are in the same church, the pastoral staff or church board may help mediate. If they serve in different churches, reaching out to district leadership may be more appropriate.

There are cases in which Matthew 18 principles should not be applied in the traditional sense. For example, victims of sexual, physical, or spiritual abuse should not be expected to confront their abuser directly. Nor is it appropriate to apply these principles when there is a significant power imbalance between parties. These sensitive situations require discernment, pastoral care, and proper intervention by leadership.

Violations of Ministerial Courtesy

In addition to Matthew 18 principles, ministers credentialed with the Assemblies of God are also held accountable to the ethical expectations found in the General Council Constitution and Bylaws. Article X lists “Violation of Ministerial Courtesy” as grounds for disciplinary action, and Article IX provides further detail:

All discourteous conduct is disapproved, and all ministers are advised against interfering with pastors in charge of assemblies, whether it be by going in upon their work without consent or by such correspondence with members of the assembly as will hurt the influence of the leader. All correspondence which concerns the whole assembly should be addressed to the one in charge and not to individual members. Where there is no pastor, letters concerning the work shall be addressed to the officers of the assembly. Any minister who so offends shall be subject to discipline. (Article IX, Section 8)

This ethical violation summarizes much of what has already been addressed in this chapter. Ministers are not permitted to “interfere with pastors in charge of assemblies.” This principle is broad and can apply to many different situations. While it is not possible to list every example, several common scenarios illustrate what this interference might look like. In general, ministers should demonstrate ministerial courtesy in every interaction with their fellow ministers. This includes showing respect for their leadership, refraining from divisive behavior, and upholding the unity and integrity of the church.

If you are a credentialed minister attending a church where you are not serving as the pastor, it is unwise and inappropriate to create division or stir up trouble for the current pastoral leadership. This includes any effort to sway members of the congregation against their pastor or to question the pastor’s authority or vision. If you disagree with the direction the pastor is leading, you must not promote an alternative vision that competes with or undermines theirs. Likewise, if you have personal grievances or concerns about the pastor’s leadership, it is unethical to voice those concerns publicly or to share them with members of the congregation. Such behavior disrupts the unity of the church and disrespects the authority God has placed in that ministry setting.

This type of violation can also be seen in a practice already discussed earlier in this chapter. Ministers must not attempt to recruit congregants from another church to attend their own. Doing so not only damages relationships between churches but also causes confusion and spiritual harm to the individuals involved. Similarly, it is unethical to interfere with or disrupt the ministry of a nearby pastor. Ministers should seek to build up the body of Christ, not draw people away from the care of another shepherd or hinder the effectiveness of their ministry.

One of the most common violations of ministerial courtesy occurs during or after a pastoral transition. While this topic will be explored more fully in a later chapter, it is important to acknowledge that outgoing pastors may be tempted to maintain influence over members of their former congregations, especially if the transition is difficult or the church is slow to embrace new leadership. However, if a former pastor interferes with the ministry of the new pastor, this is considered a violation of ministerial courtesy. Such interference may include speaking negatively about the new pastor, questioning their leadership, or openly criticizing the new direction or vision of the church. These actions are harmful to both the incoming pastor and the congregation. Ministers must intentionally step away from leadership roles once they have transitioned out and resist the urge to shape the church from a distance.

Case Study Analysis

Jared faced a difficult situation when members of his congregation began approaching him with complaints about their pastor. Unfortunately, his first mistake was entertaining those complaints rather than redirecting them. Instead of listening and agreeing with the criticism, Jared should have told those individuals that he could not accept negative reports about the pastor and encouraged them to follow the biblical model—speaking directly to the pastor with their concerns. By failing to uphold this standard, Jared allowed a spirit of division to take root and undermined his pastor’s authority.

Jared’s second critical misstep was secretly making plans to plant another church in the same community, recruiting discontented members to join him. This decision was unethical and divisive. Unless a minister receives clear permission from the lead pastor and the church leadership, it is inappropriate to establish a new church so close to one’s former place of service—especially when doing so draws people away from their current spiritual home. Planting a church should never be an act of rebellion or personal ambition; it should be rooted in unity, transparency, and spiritual calling.

To make matters worse, Jared pretended to support his pastor while using the trust and access granted to him in his leadership role to build relationships for the purpose of future recruitment. He misused his position and influence and even leveraged the church’s mailing list to invite members to his new congregation. His announcement came while the pastor was out of town, making the act not only divisive but also deeply dishonorable. These actions were a betrayal—not just of his pastor but of the congregation’s trust.

The outcome of Jared’s decisions was painful but predictable. Division does not produce long-term spiritual fruit, and the Lord does not bless efforts rooted in manipulation or disunity. The result was a fractured congregation, wounded members, and a weakened witness to the surrounding community.

There was a far better path Jared could have chosen. If he had sensed a calling to plant a church, he could have approached his pastor openly and worked with him to create a partnership. Together, they might have explored the Parent-Affiliated Church (PAC) model, allowing the original church to become the sending body for the new plant. The pastor could have released individuals who felt called to join the church plant, and both churches could have grown through mutual support and shared vision. In such a scenario, relationships would have been preserved, trust would have remained intact, and the mission of reaching more people for the kingdom of God would have been advanced through cooperation, not division.

**Q:** What are the benefits of exercising Matthew 18 principles and going directly to the person who has offended you?

**A:** When you go directly to the person who has offended you, it allows the issue to be resolved privately and quickly. This approach often leads to better understanding and reconciliation. It also prevents others from being unnecessarily pulled into the conflict and helps avoid gossip and division. 9 CHAPTER

## 9

### Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship

#### 9.1.1 — Explain how the Assemblies of God functions as a Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship rather than a denomination, and describe the role of local church autonomy.

The Assemblies of God is structured not as a traditional denomination but as a Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship as described in its Constitution and Bylaws. This foundational principle has shaped our identity from the earliest days of the Fellowship. In the aftermath of the early twentieth-century Pentecostal revival, believers sought a way to join together for missions and ministry while maintaining a structure that honored the work of the Holy Spirit and the autonomy of local churches.

This lesson explores the nature of voluntary cooperation, the benefits and responsibilities of belonging to the Fellowship, and the ethical commitments required of credentialed ministers.

A Voluntary Fellowship, Not a Denomination

When the Assemblies of God formed, its leaders intentionally chose the language of a Cooperative Fellowship rather than a denomination. Their purpose was to advance global missions and preserve the Spirit-led ministry already happening in the United States. However, they wanted to avoid creating an overly rigid or hierarchical system of governance.

In this Cooperative Fellowship, both ministers and sovereign churches choose to affiliate voluntarily. Churches that affiliate with the General Council retain their local autonomy—they elect their own pastors, select board members, and direct their ministries. Similarly, ministers voluntarily apply for credentials. This partnership is not forced but chosen. With that choice comes both benefits and responsibilities.

Benefits of Belonging to the Fellowship

One benefit of holding ministerial credentials is the affirmation of one’s calling. God alone calls people into ministry, but the credentialing process helps confirm that calling by assessing the candidate’s character, doctrine, and readiness for service. This process includes references who testify to the applicant’s calling and conduct. Credentialing also communicates to churches and ministries that the individual upholds sound doctrine and is accountable to others.

Credentialed ministers are eligible to serve in nearly thirteen thousand Assemblies of God churches across the United States. Credentials open doors for pastoral roles, guest speaking, and other ministry opportunities.

One of the best benefits is found in the name itself, Cooperative Fellowship . Through participation in local, district, and national events, as well as collaborative ministry efforts, ministers can develop a meaningful network of friends and colleagues. These friendships and ministry relationships often become a vital part of a minister’s support system, providing encouragement, wisdom, and accountability that contribute to long-term effectiveness in ministry. As I reflect on my own journey, it is difficult to place a dollar value on the friendships I have formed throughout my years in the Fellowship. These relationships have sustained me through both joyful and challenging seasons. Some of the strongest bonds are built while serving the Lord together, shoulder to shoulder in the work of the ministry.

Being part of a Fellowship provides access to valuable resources and best practices that would be difficult to develop independently. With over thirty-eight thousand ministers and thirteen thousand churches working together, the Assemblies of God is able to produce high-quality ministry tools, including curriculum, training events, camps, and specialized resources for children’s, youth, and other ministries. Some of these resources are created at the national level, while others are developed by districts to meet local needs. An often-overlooked benefit is access to leadership. Sectional presbyters and district leaders are available to offer guidance, especially in complex or sensitive situations. They help ministers navigate challenges and apply proven ministry practices.

Responsibilities of Belonging to the Fellowship

Joining the Assemblies of God Fellowship is a voluntary decision, but it carries clear responsibilities. These responsibilities are not imposed by outside authorities. Rather, they reflect the values and commitments shared by the ministers and churches that make up the Fellowship. Ministers who choose to belong are expected to participate, cooperate, and show respect for the decisions made together.

The General Council Bylaws clearly describe the expectations for those who join the Fellowship. Article VII outlines the qualifications for the ministry and includes the following statement:

Voluntary cooperation and commitment to the Fellowship . An active loyalty to our constitutional agreements, a cooperative spirit, and a readiness to seek and receive the counsel of older mature Christians and those in positions of authority.

By voluntary it is meant that, upon learning the principles, doctrines, and practices of the Assemblies of God, and by seeing the benefits to be derived from being associated with such an organization, persons of their own free choice decide to become members, thus subscribing to all that for which the organization stands.

For the minister, by cooperation, it is meant, to the best of one’s ability, complying with all decisions setting forth and defining duties and responsibilities incumbent upon members of the organization. It includes active participation and respect for the will of the majority expressed through constitutional processes.

Hence, for the minister, voluntary cooperation means that when the minister decides to become a cooperating member of the Assemblies of God, this cooperation and participation thereby becomes obligatory and not optional.

The bylaw cited above emphasizes the importance of “respect for the will of the majority expressed through constitutional processes.” The agreements that ministers are expected to follow were not imposed by denominational leaders. Instead, they were approved through a constitutional process involving fellow ministers and church delegates. Elected leaders at the district and national levels are entrusted with the responsibility of upholding those agreements. While some may assume that rules come from a rigid hierarchy, in the Assemblies of God, these decisions are the result of the broader Fellowship working together in unity and accountability.

Agreement with Doctrines and Practices

When a person applies for ministerial credentials with the Assemblies of God, they certify on the application that they agree with the Statement of Fundamental Truths. Each year, ministers also confirm their agreement with these truths when they complete the credential renewal form. Because of this commitment, it becomes an ethical issue—not just a doctrinal one—if a minister begins to promote beliefs that are contrary to the Assemblies of God’s Sixteen Fundamental Truths. Integrity requires consistency between one’s beliefs and one’s stated commitments. If a minister begins to question certain doctrines, the appropriate step is to seek guidance from a trusted Assemblies of God leader or educator. During this time of seeking, the minister should avoid creating confusion by teaching views that conflict with official Assemblies of God doctrine. If, after prayer and counsel, the minister concludes that they can no longer affirm and teach the Sixteen Fundamental Truths, they should voluntarily resign their credentials. This is a serious decision that should be made prayerfully and with input from wise spiritual mentors.

There are additional doctrinal and practical commitments ministers agree to when they join the Fellowship. Many of these are outlined in Article IX of the General Council Bylaws. One example concerns the use of alcohol and other substances. Section 14 of that article states: “The General Council of the Assemblies of God disapproves of the use of substances, such as but not limited to alcohol, tobacco, and recreational drugs, that are harmful to the body or mind, or that detract from a Christian’s testimony, or that are a cause of stumbling to others, or that impair good judgment.”

Ministers agree to abstain from alcohol, tobacco, and recreational drugs. Although Scripture forbids drunkenness rather than moderate use, the Assemblies of God has adopted total abstinence as a leadership and cultural standard. This agreement is a matter of honesty and ethics. To remain in good standing, ministers must uphold this shared commitment. A helpful resource is the Assemblies of God position paper on abstinence from alcohol, which can be found at: https://www.ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Abstinence-from-Alcohol. This paper is also included in the appendix of this course.

Another example of disapproved doctrines appears in Section 3: Eschatological Errors. Ministers are expected to avoid teaching Universalism, date-setting for Christ’s return, denial of His imminent return, and Amillennialism (which denies Christ’s bodily reign). Section 3.e. warns: “ Intent to lead others astray . A credentialed minister who embraces any of these teachings must refrain from intentionally leading other people to adopt the positions. Should they persist in emphasizing these doctrines to the point of making them an issue, their standing in the Fellowship will be seriously affected.”

Ministers must approach doctrine with both conviction and humility, choosing to honor the agreements they have made with the broader Fellowship.

**Q:** How is your church’s governance structure similar to or different from other churches you know? Consider how leaders are chosen, who makes decisions, and how your church relates to other churches or a larger network.

**A:** Churches affiliated with the General Council of the Assemblies of God are sovereign. They elect their own pastors and board members, make decisions locally, and govern themselves. In contrast, churches with more hierarchical structures may have leaders assigned by denominational authorities, with less local control over governance. The Assemblies of God model values local autonomy within a spirit of cooperative fellowship.

**Q:** When you have credentials with the Assemblies of God, what assurances does that give to a church or ministry that desires to select you for a ministry position?

**A:** Holding Assemblies of God credentials provides churches with assurance that a minister’s doctrine is sound, that the minister is accountable to a larger body, and that the minister has made intentional preparation for ministry. This includes spiritual, educational, and ethical formation. It is also a sign that the minister takes their calling seriously.

**Q:** Describe the value you have received from friendships that developed through doing ministry with others.

**A:** Answers will vary. However, many ministers experience deep encouragement, accountability, and shared joy through friendships formed in ministry. These relationships often become a vital part of sustaining long-term service.

**Q:** What are some reasons Assemblies of God ministers agree to abstain from alcohol?

**A:** Assemblies of God ministers agree to abstain from alcohol as part of their commitment to high ethical standards and leadership responsibility. Alcohol has had a long history of causing harm to individuals and families. Since alcohol use can lead to addiction and is a potential stumbling block for others, ministers choose total abstinence as a matter of wisdom, witness, and integrity.

#### 9.1.2 — Identify key benefits of holding ministerial credentials with the Assemblies of God, including affirmation of calling, access to ministry opportunities, and relational support.

The Assemblies of God is structured not as a traditional denomination but as a Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship as described in its Constitution and Bylaws. This foundational principle has shaped our identity from the earliest days of the Fellowship. In the aftermath of the early twentieth-century Pentecostal revival, believers sought a way to join together for missions and ministry while maintaining a structure that honored the work of the Holy Spirit and the autonomy of local churches.

This lesson explores the nature of voluntary cooperation, the benefits and responsibilities of belonging to the Fellowship, and the ethical commitments required of credentialed ministers.

A Voluntary Fellowship, Not a Denomination

When the Assemblies of God formed, its leaders intentionally chose the language of a Cooperative Fellowship rather than a denomination. Their purpose was to advance global missions and preserve the Spirit-led ministry already happening in the United States. However, they wanted to avoid creating an overly rigid or hierarchical system of governance.

In this Cooperative Fellowship, both ministers and sovereign churches choose to affiliate voluntarily. Churches that affiliate with the General Council retain their local autonomy—they elect their own pastors, select board members, and direct their ministries. Similarly, ministers voluntarily apply for credentials. This partnership is not forced but chosen. With that choice comes both benefits and responsibilities.

Benefits of Belonging to the Fellowship

One benefit of holding ministerial credentials is the affirmation of one’s calling. God alone calls people into ministry, but the credentialing process helps confirm that calling by assessing the candidate’s character, doctrine, and readiness for service. This process includes references who testify to the applicant’s calling and conduct. Credentialing also communicates to churches and ministries that the individual upholds sound doctrine and is accountable to others.

Credentialed ministers are eligible to serve in nearly thirteen thousand Assemblies of God churches across the United States. Credentials open doors for pastoral roles, guest speaking, and other ministry opportunities.

One of the best benefits is found in the name itself, Cooperative Fellowship . Through participation in local, district, and national events, as well as collaborative ministry efforts, ministers can develop a meaningful network of friends and colleagues. These friendships and ministry relationships often become a vital part of a minister’s support system, providing encouragement, wisdom, and accountability that contribute to long-term effectiveness in ministry. As I reflect on my own journey, it is difficult to place a dollar value on the friendships I have formed throughout my years in the Fellowship. These relationships have sustained me through both joyful and challenging seasons. Some of the strongest bonds are built while serving the Lord together, shoulder to shoulder in the work of the ministry.

Being part of a Fellowship provides access to valuable resources and best practices that would be difficult to develop independently. With over thirty-eight thousand ministers and thirteen thousand churches working together, the Assemblies of God is able to produce high-quality ministry tools, including curriculum, training events, camps, and specialized resources for children’s, youth, and other ministries. Some of these resources are created at the national level, while others are developed by districts to meet local needs. An often-overlooked benefit is access to leadership. Sectional presbyters and district leaders are available to offer guidance, especially in complex or sensitive situations. They help ministers navigate challenges and apply proven ministry practices.

Responsibilities of Belonging to the Fellowship

Joining the Assemblies of God Fellowship is a voluntary decision, but it carries clear responsibilities. These responsibilities are not imposed by outside authorities. Rather, they reflect the values and commitments shared by the ministers and churches that make up the Fellowship. Ministers who choose to belong are expected to participate, cooperate, and show respect for the decisions made together.

The General Council Bylaws clearly describe the expectations for those who join the Fellowship. Article VII outlines the qualifications for the ministry and includes the following statement:

Voluntary cooperation and commitment to the Fellowship . An active loyalty to our constitutional agreements, a cooperative spirit, and a readiness to seek and receive the counsel of older mature Christians and those in positions of authority.

By voluntary it is meant that, upon learning the principles, doctrines, and practices of the Assemblies of God, and by seeing the benefits to be derived from being associated with such an organization, persons of their own free choice decide to become members, thus subscribing to all that for which the organization stands.

For the minister, by cooperation, it is meant, to the best of one’s ability, complying with all decisions setting forth and defining duties and responsibilities incumbent upon members of the organization. It includes active participation and respect for the will of the majority expressed through constitutional processes.

Hence, for the minister, voluntary cooperation means that when the minister decides to become a cooperating member of the Assemblies of God, this cooperation and participation thereby becomes obligatory and not optional.

The bylaw cited above emphasizes the importance of “respect for the will of the majority expressed through constitutional processes.” The agreements that ministers are expected to follow were not imposed by denominational leaders. Instead, they were approved through a constitutional process involving fellow ministers and church delegates. Elected leaders at the district and national levels are entrusted with the responsibility of upholding those agreements. While some may assume that rules come from a rigid hierarchy, in the Assemblies of God, these decisions are the result of the broader Fellowship working together in unity and accountability.

Agreement with Doctrines and Practices
