When a person applies for ministerial credentials with the Assemblies of God, they certify on the application that they agree with the Statement of Fundamental Truths. Each year, ministers also confirm their agreement with these truths when they complete the credential renewal form. Because of this commitment, it becomes an ethical issue—not just a doctrinal one—if a minister begins to promote beliefs that are contrary to the Assemblies of God’s Sixteen Fundamental Truths. Integrity requires consistency between one’s beliefs and one’s stated commitments. If a minister begins to question certain doctrines, the appropriate step is to seek guidance from a trusted Assemblies of God leader or educator. During this time of seeking, the minister should avoid creating confusion by teaching views that conflict with official Assemblies of God doctrine. If, after prayer and counsel, the minister concludes that they can no longer affirm and teach the Sixteen Fundamental Truths, they should voluntarily resign their credentials. This is a serious decision that should be made prayerfully and with input from wise spiritual mentors.

There are additional doctrinal and practical commitments ministers agree to when they join the Fellowship. Many of these are outlined in Article IX of the General Council Bylaws. One example concerns the use of alcohol and other substances. Section 14 of that article states: “The General Council of the Assemblies of God disapproves of the use of substances, such as but not limited to alcohol, tobacco, and recreational drugs, that are harmful to the body or mind, or that detract from a Christian’s testimony, or that are a cause of stumbling to others, or that impair good judgment.”

Ministers agree to abstain from alcohol, tobacco, and recreational drugs. Although Scripture forbids drunkenness rather than moderate use, the Assemblies of God has adopted total abstinence as a leadership and cultural standard. This agreement is a matter of honesty and ethics. To remain in good standing, ministers must uphold this shared commitment. A helpful resource is the Assemblies of God position paper on abstinence from alcohol, which can be found at: https://www.ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Abstinence-from-Alcohol. This paper is also included in the appendix of this course.

Another example of disapproved doctrines appears in Section 3: Eschatological Errors. Ministers are expected to avoid teaching Universalism, date-setting for Christ’s return, denial of His imminent return, and Amillennialism (which denies Christ’s bodily reign). Section 3.e. warns: “ Intent to lead others astray . A credentialed minister who embraces any of these teachings must refrain from intentionally leading other people to adopt the positions. Should they persist in emphasizing these doctrines to the point of making them an issue, their standing in the Fellowship will be seriously affected.”

Ministers must approach doctrine with both conviction and humility, choosing to honor the agreements they have made with the broader Fellowship.

**Q:** How is your church’s governance structure similar to or different from other churches you know? Consider how leaders are chosen, who makes decisions, and how your church relates to other churches or a larger network.

**A:** Churches affiliated with the General Council of the Assemblies of God are sovereign. They elect their own pastors and board members, make decisions locally, and govern themselves. In contrast, churches with more hierarchical structures may have leaders assigned by denominational authorities, with less local control over governance. The Assemblies of God model values local autonomy within a spirit of cooperative fellowship.

**Q:** When you have credentials with the Assemblies of God, what assurances does that give to a church or ministry that desires to select you for a ministry position?

**A:** Holding Assemblies of God credentials provides churches with assurance that a minister’s doctrine is sound, that the minister is accountable to a larger body, and that the minister has made intentional preparation for ministry. This includes spiritual, educational, and ethical formation. It is also a sign that the minister takes their calling seriously.

**Q:** Describe the value you have received from friendships that developed through doing ministry with others.

**A:** Answers will vary. However, many ministers experience deep encouragement, accountability, and shared joy through friendships formed in ministry. These relationships often become a vital part of sustaining long-term service.

**Q:** What are some reasons Assemblies of God ministers agree to abstain from alcohol?

**A:** Assemblies of God ministers agree to abstain from alcohol as part of their commitment to high ethical standards and leadership responsibility. Alcohol has had a long history of causing harm to individuals and families. Since alcohol use can lead to addiction and is a potential stumbling block for others, ministers choose total abstinence as a matter of wisdom, witness, and integrity.

### Active Participation and Loyalty in the Fellowship

#### 9.2.1 — Explain the importance of active participation and loyalty in strengthening the Fellowship and responding ethically in times of disagreement.

Belonging to the Assemblies of God is not just a matter of holding credentials. It means joining a spiritual family that is committed to mutual encouragement, accountability, and shared purpose. The Fellowship is strengthened when every minister contributes their time, wisdom, and spiritual gifts. Ministers are not only called to receive from the Fellowship but also to actively invest in it.

This lesson will explore two related ideas: active participation and active loyalty. These are essential qualities for those who choose to be part of a cooperative fellowship. As ministers contribute and stay engaged—even in times of disagreement—they help build a culture of unity, health, and maturity within the body of Christ.

Active Participation

The General Council Bylaws state that voluntary cooperation includes “active participation.” This means ministers should look for ways to give, not just receive. President John F. Kennedy once said, “Ask not what your country can do for you, but what you can do for your country.” That same spirit applies to the Fellowship. T. Burton Pierce challenged ministers to ask themselves, “Is my Fellowship stronger because I am a member?” (Pierce 1996, 193). When each member grows in their relationship with God and strengthens their ministry, the entire Fellowship benefits. The Assemblies of God recently emphasized this with the theme: Our Church, Our Family.

Ministers can participate actively by attending sectional, network, and national events whenever possible. Building relationships with other ministers creates a network of support and encouragement. Ministers should also be willing to serve in leadership roles when invited. Some choose not to advance their credentials out of concern that they may be asked to lead. However, stepping into leadership when called is one way to bless the Fellowship. Participation also includes supporting key initiatives such as missions, church planting, and other cooperative efforts.

Active Loyalty

The previously quoted bylaws on Voluntary Cooperative Fellowship include the phrase: active loyalty to our constitutional agreements, a cooperative spirit, and a readiness to seek and receive the counsel of older mature Christians and those in positions of authority . This statement highlights one of the essential responsibilities of belonging to the Fellowship, demonstrating loyalty to one another and to the organization as a whole. Loyalty is easy to express when we agree with the decisions being made. However, true loyalty is most clearly revealed during times of disagreement or personal frustration. How we respond in such moments reflects both our character and our commitment to the Fellowship.

There may be times when a minister disagrees with a decision made by their local church, district, or national leadership. When such situations arise, it is important to follow the proper relational and constitutional steps to seek resolution. Ministers should feel the freedom to express their concerns respectfully and privately to those in leadership. The Assemblies of God provides ways for members to participate in the decision-making process through voting, attending business meetings, and, in some cases, appealing decisions that have been made according to the Constitution and Bylaws. These opportunities reflect the cooperative and participatory nature of our Fellowship.

However, there are also unethical ways to express disagreement. At times, a minister who is frustrated or disagrees with the Fellowship’s doctrine or leadership may choose to go public rather than follow the established processes. In today’s digital culture, this often happens through social media. A minister may share a post, video, or public comment criticizing leaders or doctrines. This behavior not only violates the principle of active loyalty but also contradicts Scripture. Proverbs warns: “There are six things the Lord hates, seven that are detestable to him: . . . a false witness who pours out lies and a person who stirs up conflict in the community” (Proverbs 6:16, 19). While such posts may attract attention or gain support from others, they damage unity and do not advance the cause of Christ. When a minister publicly criticizes the Fellowship, it rarely stops with leadership; it affects the entire body and can create unnecessary division and confusion.

**Q:** What are some ways you personally can contribute to add value to the Fellowship?

**A:** Answers will vary. Possible responses may include mentoring younger ministers, attending district / network events, serving in leadership roles, supporting missions, or strengthening unity among local churches.

**Q:** When a minister criticizes the Fellowship publicly, how does that affect others in the Fellowship?

**A:** Public criticism of the Fellowship can create confusion and division. Since we are a cooperative fellowship, such comments often feel personal to others who are part of it. Public negativity, especially outside the context of relationship or accountability, can damage trust and hinder the unity and reputation of the broader body.

### Accountability and Ministerial Discipline

#### 9.3.1 — Explain the purpose of the Assemblies of God's ministerial discipline process and the responsibilities of ministers within that structure.

Accountability is essential to healthy spiritual leadership. Ministers who serve within the Assemblies of God are part of a Fellowship that not only encourages participation but also expects mutual responsibility. One of the clearest expressions of this responsibility is the system of ministerial discipline outlined in the General Council Bylaws. Though discipline may be difficult, it exists to protect the church, uphold the integrity of ministry, and provide a path for restoration.

Many ministers in other settings do not have this kind of structure in place. When leaders fall into sin or make unethical decisions, there may be no formal process to correct the situation or restore the individual. The Assemblies of God offers a model that balances justice, protection, and grace. In this lesson, we will explore why this process matters, how it functions, and what ministers must understand about their role in supporting it.

The Assemblies of God’s approach to ministerial discipline is described in Article X of the General Council Bylaws. This system is both a benefit and a responsibility. Galatians 6:1 reminds believers, “If someone is caught in a sin, you who live by the Spirit should restore that person gently. But watch yourselves, or you also may be tempted.” The goal of discipline in the Assemblies of God is that “God may be honored, that the purity and welfare of the ministry may be maintained, and that those under discipline may be brought to repentance and restoration” (GC Bylaws, Article X, Section 1).

When a minister serves in a church that is not part of a structured Fellowship, there may be no system of accountability in place. If that minister falls into sin, there is often no formal process for correction, restoration, or protection. As a result, both the minister and the church are left vulnerable. In some cases, a pastor may have an affair, divorce their spouse, marry the person involved in the affair, and continue pastoring the same church with no consequences. These situations reveal the serious need for clear accountability. They also highlight the value of the Assemblies of God’s approach to ministerial discipline. Knowing that our Fellowship provides a structure for both correction and restoration deepens our appreciation for its integrity and care.

One of the key responsibilities of belonging to the Assemblies of God is to honor the decisions made through its discipline process. This responsibility is stated clearly in Article IX, Section 10 of the General Council Bylaws. That section identifies “An Improper Attitude toward Those Removed from the Fellowship” as a disapproved practice. The Bylaws explain:

In order to render effective decisions made in the interest of proper discipline and for the protection of our assemblies, all who hold credentials, and local churches holding certificates of affiliation, shall refrain from taking an attitude toward offenders that would tend to nullify or set at naught the solemn verdict of those entrusted with this responsibility. Those who fail to support said verdict shall be subject to reprimand or, if persisted in, appropriate discipline.

In practical terms, this means that if a minister has been dismissed from the Fellowship, a credentialed pastor or affiliated church cannot disregard that decision by inviting the dismissed person to preach, lead, or serve in ministry. Doing so undermines the disciplinary process and disrespects the leadership God has placed within the Fellowship. Upholding these decisions helps protect the integrity of the Fellowship and reinforces a shared commitment to ethical ministry.

Of course, it is not always easy to see discipline as a benefit, especially when one is the subject of it. In those moments, it is wise to respond with humility. A minister may disagree with how their case is being handled. However, the Bylaws provide a clear appeal process that allows for concerns to be addressed respectfully and fairly. There may also be situations where someone hears about another minister’s discipline and disagrees with the outcome. In these cases, it is important to remember that leaders often have access to information not available to others. We must choose to trust the process established in our governing documents and the leaders elected to carry it out. As Hebrews 12:11 reminds us, “No discipline seems pleasant at the time, but painful. Later on, however, it produces a harvest of righteousness and peace for those who have been trained by it.”

Ohio Network Secretary Josh Willaford uses the image of a playground to help explain ethics. Many ministry courses teach students how to use the different pieces of “playground equipment,” such as doctrine, interpretation, or leadership skills. Ethics, on the other hand, focuses on the fence around the playground. It defines the boundaries that keep everyone safe and secure within the Fellowship. The Assemblies of God’s discipline process is one part of that fence. It helps protect ministers, churches, and the Fellowship as a whole.

The General Council Bylaws Article X, Section 3 lists specific causes for disciplinary action. These include misconduct committed either in person or through digital means. The categories and examples are as follows:

a. Sexual Misconduct.

(1) Moral transgression involving sexual misconduct

(2) Moral transgression involving pornography

b. Financial Misconduct.

(1) Immoral, unethical, or illegal practices related to personal finances

(2) Immoral, unethical, or illegal practices related to church or ministry finances

c. Relational / Ethical Misconduct.

(1) Blatant misrepresentation of our Pentecostal testimony

(2) A declared open change in doctrinal views

(3) A contentious or noncooperative spirit

(4) An assumption of dictatorial authority over an assembly

(5) An arbitrary rejection of counsel from district leadership

(6) Violations of ministerial courtesy or ethics (See Bylaws, Article IX, B, Section 8.)

(7) An improper attitude toward those dismissed from the Fellowship (See Bylaws, Article IX, B, Section 10.)

(8) General inefficiency in the ministry

(9) Moral or ethical transgression other than sexual misconduct

(10) Ministry in a non-Assemblies of God church without proper approval from district leadership (See Bylaws, Article IX, B, Section 9.)

(11) A marriage in violation of our standards for either a recognized divorce or a biblical marriage (See Bylaws, Article IX, B, Section 5, paragraphs d and e.)

d. Substance Use and Abuse.

Violations of General Council Bylaws Article IX. DOCTRINES AND PRACTICES DISAPPROVED, B, Section 14. Substance Usage; including:

(1) Use or abuse of alcohol or recreational drugs

(2) Abuse or addiction involving other legal substances

(3) Use, abuse, or addiction involving other illegal substances

These guidelines provide clarity and consistency for addressing issues in the ministry. They serve to protect the minister, the church, and the integrity of the gospel.

Case Study Analysis

Tyler’s missteps began long before he openly embraced doctrinal error. One of his first unwise choices was to distance himself from the Fellowship. He stopped attending district events and made little effort to build relationships with neighboring Assemblies of God ministers. Instead, he began to associate more closely with leaders from other theological backgrounds. While there is nothing inherently wrong with participating in a local ministerial association or learning from those outside the Fellowship, doing so in isolation—without continued engagement with those who share your core beliefs—can be spiritually and ethically dangerous. Ministers benefit from the accountability, encouragement, and doctrinal clarity that come from maintaining close relationships with others in the Fellowship.

As Tyler began to develop questions about the Sixteen Fundamental Truths, his first step should have been to seek counsel from a trusted Assemblies of God leader. His district or network may have been able to connect him with someone who could walk with him through those questions. Instead, he kept his doubts to himself and eventually began teaching doctrines that were inconsistent with the official beliefs of the Fellowship. By doing so, he violated the constitutional agreement he made when joining the Assemblies of God. According to the decision-making model presented earlier in this course, Tyler failed to consider the rules and commitments that applied to his situation.

Even more concerning, Tyler signed his annual credential renewal form stating that he continued to agree with the Sixteen Fundamental Truths. By that point, he had already begun teaching a contrary doctrine in the church. Signing the renewal form while actively rejecting core doctrines was dishonest and unethical. A more honorable course of action would have been to pause his teaching, seek wise counsel, and reflect on his calling within the Assemblies of God.

If, after receiving counsel, Tyler remained convinced that he could not in good conscience uphold the Sixteen Fundamental Truths, the ethical response would have been to quietly resign his ministerial credentials and step down as pastor. This path, though difficult, would have demonstrated personal integrity and minimized harm to the church body.

Unfortunately, Tyler chose a different route. When confronted by leadership about his doctrinal violation, he defended himself and continued to promote his views both within the church and on social media. His actions caused confusion, division, and led several families to leave the congregation. Ministers who take this approach often underestimate the broader consequences of their behavior. Violating doctrinal agreements and turning disagreement into public conflict not only damages personal credibility—it also deeply impacts the people they are called to serve.

**Q:** Why is it necessary to have an ecclesiastical discipline process?

**A:** A discipline process is essential to honor God, restore fallen ministers, and protect the church. It ensures that ethical and doctrinal standards are upheld and provides a pathway for redemption and healing when sin or failure occurs. UNIT 4

## 10

### Communicating the Message

#### 10.1.1 — Explain the ethical responsibility of ministers to honor both civic authorities and church leadership, drawing on Romans 13:7.

One of the most sacred responsibilities of a minister is to communicate God’s Word to His people. Preaching and teaching are not just weekly tasks—they are spiritual acts of service that require careful preparation, biblical faithfulness, and ethical integrity. Ministers must be committed to growing in their communication of Scripture and take seriously the calling to preach the truth with accuracy and humility. This lesson explores three essential areas related to ethical preaching: preparation, content, and the proper use of other sources.

Adequate Preparation

Preaching and teaching are vital functions of ministry. They flow from a life of prayer and consistent study of the Bible. As Trull and Creech state, “A call to preach is a call to prepare” (Trull and Creech 2017, 91). Ministers today face many demands, including pastoral care, church administration, media, outreach, conflict resolution, and emergencies. With so many urgent tasks competing for attention, sermon preparation can easily be delayed or neglected. However, ministry of the Word must be prioritized.

Ministers should protect time throughout the week for preparation. This time should be treated like any other important appointment. Effective preaching begins with time in prayer and in the Scriptures. Listening to sermons and podcasts, reading books, and studying effective communicators are all ways a minister can grow in skill and depth. The investment made in preparing sermons is an investment in the spiritual growth of those who will hear them.

The Content of the Message

The apostle Paul instructed Timothy to “preach the Word” (2 Timothy 4:2). While ministers may speak on a variety of subjects, true power and transformation come from the Word of God. Sermons must be rooted in Scripture. It is not enough to deliver personal opinions or motivational speeches and then add a few verses for support. Trull and Creech caution against sermons that resemble secular talks rather than Spirit-led proclamation. They write, “How ethical is it for a pastor to sprinkle proof texts through three points that television pop psychologists could have delivered with a straight face?” (Trull and Creech 2017, 93).

Instead, preaching should emerge from a spiritual conversation between God, the pastor, and the congregation. Ministers should approach preaching as an opportunity to faithfully present God’s truth to His people. Paul also told Timothy to be one who “correctly handles the word of truth” (2 Timothy 2:15), which is a clear ethical charge. Faithful interpretation begins with understanding what the biblical text meant to its original audience and then applying that meaning to contemporary life (Grey and Lewis 2024, 4). In doing so, ministers’ model how to understand and apply Scripture.

When ministers preach with clarity, accuracy, and relevance, they equip the congregation to do the same. Two helpful resources for further study are Introduction to Biblical Interpretation by Jacqueline Grey and Paul Lewis (2024) and How to Read the Bible for All Its Worth by Gordon Fee (2014, 4th Edition).

Plagiarism has become a significant concern in ministry ethics. In recent years, some pastors have lost their positions after it was discovered they used other people’s material without proper credit. There are differing opinions on what constitutes plagiarism in preaching, but the ethical issues are real.

Craig Brian Larson addresses this perceived ambiguity in an excellent article titled “Plagiarism, Shmagiarism,” in which he said, “For one thing, we don’t always know when to credit other sources. Preaching is like blocking defensive linemen in football: the line between blocking and holding can be as fuzzy as between research and reliance. At times the need for crediting sources is perfectly clear. Tell someone else’s story as though it happened to you? Use an entire sermon without giving credit? Penalty, ten yards and loss of down, major chewing out from the coach. Most everyone says that’s a foul. At other times, citing a source is a judgment call” (Larson 2025, https://www.preachingtoday.com/skills/2005/august/plagiarism-shmagiarism.html).

Romans 13:7 gives a helpful guiding principle: “Give to everyone what you owe them: If honor, then honor.” When applied to preaching, this means giving appropriate credit for the work of others. There are some firm rules that should be followed. For example, it is never acceptable to tell someone else’s story as if it happened to you. That misleads the congregation and damages your integrity.

There are several reasons why failing to give credit is unethical. Larson notes that it misleads the audience, misrepresents your abilities, steals another’s work, and risks destroying trust if discovered. Congregants naturally assume that the message they are hearing is the result of the preacher’s own study and spiritual insight. If they learn otherwise, it can damage credibility and spiritual authority.

Ministers should give credit when quoting a source directly or when using a unique idea from another writer or speaker. A simple, brief mention of the author or source is often enough. This not only honors the original source but also strengthens the message by showing that the preacher has done proper study. If a sermon or outline has been purchased or freely shared by another minister, it is still best to mention the source. Never tell a story that you have borrowed as if it is a personal story that happened to you. To do so is disingenuous.

Ministers should avoid relying too heavily on prepackaged sermons. While many pastors freely share their sermons and say, “Use it like it’s yours,” the primary responsibility of preaching belongs to the leader God has appointed in the local church. God calls each pastor or ministry leader to seek Him for a message tailored to their congregation. There is strong value in using structured curriculum for Christian education and in listening to various sermons for inspiration. However, the sermon delivered from the pulpit should reflect the spiritual needs of the local church and the voice of its pastor. On occasion, it may be appropriate to use a sermon developed by someone else, especially for a particular purpose or event. If so, take time to pray over it, adapt it to your setting, make it your own, and give proper credit. Doing so not only honors the original source but also reflects ethical preaching and maintains trust with the congregation.

At the same time, it is also important to avoid the opposite extreme—citing every idea, story, or phrase. Doing so can interrupt the flow of the sermon and distract from the message. Tim Keller offers a balanced perspective: “Nevertheless, we must be careful not to over-react. I do not think anyone expects oral communication to have the same amount of detailed attribution as written communication. To cite where you got every allusion or basic idea or general illustration in a sermon would be tedious. A certain amount of leeway must be granted” (Keller 2024).

Ethical preaching requires both honesty and wisdom. Ministers should honor the work of others while also fulfilling their calling to speak God’s Word clearly and faithfully to the people they serve.

**Q:** Describe your current sermon preparation plan and how you can make improvements to better prepare. If you are not in a position where you are preaching or teaching, describe what your plan will be when you have the opportunity.

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** In what ways does a minister model for the congregation how to best interpret and apply the Bible through their preaching? Think about how the minister uses Scripture, explains its meaning, and connects it to everyday life.

**A:** A minister models this by properly handling the biblical text and not manipulating Scripture to fit a predetermined message. When the main points of a sermon naturally emerge from the text, the minister teaches congregants how to interpret and apply Scripture in their own lives.

**Q:** Plagiarism in Preaching

**Q:** Describe your practice of working out the verse, “Give to everyone what you owe them: If honor, then honor,” in giving proper credit to sources in preaching and teaching.

**A:** Answers will vary.

### Pastoral Counseling

#### 10.2.1 — Describe appropriate ways for ministers to engage with social media and digital tools while maintaining integrity and avoiding compromise.

One of the roles of a faithful minister is to care for people through the many seasons of life. Whether someone is facing a crisis, struggling in their marriage, or walking through loss, the minister is often one of the first people they turn to. Pastoral counseling or guidance can be a powerful ministry tool when done ethically and with humility. This lesson addresses ethical considerations that every minister should understand before offering spiritual counsel.

Defining the Role

A good pastor takes time to care for the spiritual and emotional needs of their congregation. This includes listening, providing biblical wisdom, and offering guidance in times of distress. There are some important ethical considerations to be aware of as you carry out these important duties.

Language matters. Using the term counseling may imply a level of training, supervision, and licensure that many ministers do not possess. Trull and Creech caution: “Untrained, unsupervised, and without a license, pastors who purport to do ‘counseling’ may be overstating their claims and could find themselves legally liable for the outcomes. Presenting oneself as a ‘counselor’ in a context where that profession is licensed and regulated is ethically questionable if the pastor lacks those credentials” (2017, 85).

Some ministers are comfortable using the term pastoral counseling , while others prefer pastoral guidance to avoid confusion. It may be wise to consult your insurance provider to see what terminology they recommend in your context.

Referral and Session Limits

Ministers should also set healthy boundaries in how they offer care. Jason Tourville, chairperson of the Assemblies of God Mental HealthCare Committee, offers this advice: “It is considered best practice for ministers, who are operating in a pastoral role, to limit the scope and length of their sessions. It is common for pastors to meet with individuals or couples for a limited 3–5 sessions. If circumstances are not able to be resolved through biblical guidance in the limited sessions, having adequate referral sources is a tremendous resource” (personal email).

Pastors should develop relationships with licensed Christian counselors and be prepared to refer individuals when issues extend beyond the minister’s expertise. Mental health struggles, trauma, abuse, addiction, or marriage crises may require professional care. A pastor’s role in these cases is to provide initial spiritual support and then guide the person to qualified help.

Confidentiality and Legal Obligations

Pastoral confidentiality refers to the ethical and legal responsibility of clergy to protect private information ( privileged communication as addressed in the General Council Bylaws in Article VII, Section 14) shared by individuals during ministry conversations. This concept is closely related to what is legally known as clergy-penitent privilege —a rule that protects confidential communications between a spiritual advisor and the person receiving counsel. Like attorney-client privilege, this legal protection is designed to encourage open and honest conversations within a spiritual context. Ministers must handle such sensitive matters with care and discretion.

However, confidentiality is not absolute. In certain situations, disclosure may be necessary to protect the individual from serious harm. These exceptions typically include cases where a serious crime has been committed, is in progress, or is being planned. Disclosure may also be required in situations involving child abuse, a person harboring a dangerous contagious disease, or when someone poses an immediate threat to themselves or others.

It is important to understand that not all church-related conversations are legally protected. For example, public statements made during a church service or group setting may not be considered confidential. According to Church Law & Tax, “Not every communication made to a minister is privileged and thereby protected from disclosure. The typical statute applies only to (1) communications (2) confidentially made (3) to a minister (4) acting in his or her professional capacity as a spiritual advisor. Some states add a fifth requirement—the communication made in confidence to a clergyman must be made ‘in the course of discipline’” (Church Law & Tax, n.d).

Because laws vary by state, ministers should become familiar with the legal expectations in their local jurisdiction and seek legal guidance if unsure. Upholding confidentiality is a sacred trust—but it must be exercised with wisdom and within legal boundaries.

Informed Consent and Setting Expectations

Before undergoing a medical procedure, you may have signed an “informed consent” form that explained the nature of the procedure, along with its risks and benefits. In a similar way, when providing pastoral counseling or biblical guidance, it is recommended that ministers verbally review a few key items at the outset. This conversation helps the person understand the nature and scope of the care being offered. Ministers should clearly state that they are not licensed counselors and that the guidance being provided is pastoral in nature. It is also important to affirm a commitment to confidentiality while explaining its legal and ethical limits. These limits typically include mandatory reporting in cases involving physical or sexual abuse of a minor, threats of harm to self or others, or disclosure of a violent crime that has not previously been resolved.

Dual Relationships and Boundaries

Another important ethical issue is the potential for dual relationships . These occur when a minister provides pastoral counseling to someone with whom they have a close personal relationship, such as a family member or close friend. In casual spiritual conversations, this may be unavoidable and appropriate. However, in more formal situations involving sensitive or complex issues, it may be best to refer the person to another trusted leader on the ministry team or perhaps from another church. This ensures objectivity and helps prevent emotional entanglement.

In all pastoral care settings, ministers must establish appropriate boundaries. These boundaries protect the person receiving care and also guard the minister’s credibility and emotional well-being.

Sexual Misconduct in Pastoral Counseling

One of the most serious ethical violations a minister can commit is engaging in sexual misconduct with someone they are counseling. The power imbalance between the minister and the person receiving care makes any form of sexual involvement deeply unethical and spiritually harmful.

The American Association of Christian Counselors Code of Ethics provides a clear standard:

Christian counselors refrain from all forms of sexual misconduct with clients in professional, pastoral, coaches, or lay relationships and view such behavior as unethical and forbidden. This includes any kind of sexual exploitation, abuse, deception, manipulation, harassment, relationships where sexual involvement is invited, and relationships where informed consent presumably exists. Due to the inherent power imbalance of helping relationships, as well as biblical principles related to sexual behavior outside of marriage, such apparent consent is considered illusory and illegitimate. Forbidden sexual activities and deceptions include, but are not limited to: (1) direct sexual touch or contact; (2) seductive sexual speech or nonverbal behavior; (3) solicitation of sexual or romantic relations; (4) erotic contact or behavior as a response to the sexual invitation or seductive behavior of clients; (5) unnecessary questioning and / or excessive probing into the client’s sexual history and practices; (6) inappropriate counselor disclosures of client attractiveness, sexual opinions, or sexual humor; (7) advocacy of counselor-client sexual relations; (8) secretive sexual communications and anonymous virtual interaction via the internet, sexting or other electronic and informational means; (9) sexual harassment by comments, touch, or promises / threats of special action; and (10) sexual misconduct as defined by all applicable laws, ethics, and church, organizational, or practice policies. (2023)

The Code lists examples of unethical conduct, including sexual touch, seductive speech, romantic solicitation, inappropriate disclosures, electronic communications of a sexual nature, and sexual harassment. All such actions are not only unethical but also often illegal, and the ministry disqualifies anyone who partakes in them. Ministers must hold themselves to the highest standards of sexual purity in all ministry contexts, especially where vulnerable people are seeking help.

**Q:** What is an example of when a pastor may want to refer a person to a licensed professional counselor?

**A:** In general, it is advisable to refer someone to a licensed professional counselor when their needs fall outside the pastor’s scope of training or expertise. One example would be when the person shows symptoms of a psychological disorder.

### Conducting Weddings and Funerals

#### 10.3.1 — Identify best practices for using technology to enhance rather than replace personal faith and ministry relationships.

Weddings and funerals are significant life events, and ministers are often called upon to lead these sacred moments. These occasions bring with them meaningful opportunities for ministry, but also serious ethical responsibilities. Whether helping a couple prepare for marriage or walking with a grieving family through loss, the minister must act with integrity, wisdom, and compassion. This lesson explores the ethical considerations that guide ministers in officiating weddings and funerals.

Ethical Boundaries in Officiating Weddings

One of the most important ethical decisions a minister will make in regard to weddings is whether to accept the invitation to officiate. Not every request should be granted. The General Council Bylaws of the Assemblies of God prohibit credentialed ministers from officiating at same-sex weddings or covenant ceremonies. The Bylaws state clearly that, “Officiating at or performing a marriage or similar ceremony for persons of the same sex is grounds for dismissal of ministerial credentials” (General Council Bylaws, Article IX, Section 5, d).

In addition, ministers are strongly discouraged from conducting weddings where one or both individuals have a former spouse who is still living, unless the divorce meets one of the exceptional circumstances outlined in the Bylaws. In these situations, it is wise for the minister to seek counsel and review the relevant guidelines before proceeding.

Beyond eligibility concerns, ministers have a responsibility to help couples prepare not just for a wedding day but for a lifetime of marriage. It is strongly recommended that the minister personally provide or require premarital counseling. If unable to offer it directly, the minister should refer the couple to a qualified pastor or Christian counselor. While the couple may focus on the ceremony and celebration, the minister must emphasize the importance of building a foundation for a healthy and lasting marriage.

Ethical Responsibilities at Funerals

Funerals offer a sacred opportunity to provide comfort to those who grieve. As a minister, your presence and words can bring peace, hope, and reassurance during one of life’s most difficult seasons. However, funerals also require careful ethical attention.

Ministers must resist the temptation to speak dishonestly about the deceased. There may be subtle pressure to present the individual in a more favorable light than truth allows, or even to imply that the person is in heaven regardless of their known beliefs or spiritual condition. While it is never appropriate to declare someone to be in hell, it is equally unwise to offer false assurance. Instead, focus the message on the comfort and hope found in Christ, and point the living toward the gospel.

Ministers should also avoid pretending to have had a close relationship with the deceased if that was not the case. Sincerity and humility should guide every word spoken. It is better to speak briefly and truthfully than to overstate your connection or offer empty sentiments. Truth, spoken with grace and compassion, will be far more meaningful to those who are grieving.

**Q:** What are some of the benefits of premarital counseling?

**A:** Couples benefit from premarital counseling by discussing topics such as family backgrounds, differing views on parenting, financial expectations, conflict resolution, and communication styles. Addressing these issues in advance can strengthen a marriage.

### Pastoral Transitions

#### 10.4.1 — Explain the ethical responsibility of ministers to protect their ultimate allegiance to the Kingdom of God over political affiliation.

Pastoral transitions are significant moments in the life of a church. They affect not only leadership but also the emotional and spiritual health of the congregation. A well-handled transition can strengthen a church and prepare it for future growth. A poorly managed one, however, can leave lasting damage. This lesson outlines ethical principles that help ensure pastoral transitions are conducted with wisdom, honor, and a spirit of cooperation.

Planning Before the Transition

Wise pastors understand that preparation for a transition begins long before the decision to resign. In an interview with retired Alaska District Superintendent Bill Welch, he emphasized that developing strong leadership within the church is one of the best ways to prepare for a future transition. A healthy church is not built solely around the pastor but includes capable and spiritually mature lay leaders who can help guide the church through times of change.

Henry and Richard Blackaby write, “One of the most common failures of leaders is that they spend little time or effort preparing their organization for their departure. . . . But one test of great leaders is how well their organizations do after they leave” (Blackaby 2001, 135). A church with strong lay leadership will weather transitions more smoothly and remain focused on its mission.

Timing and Communication

Once a pastor discerns that it is time to transition, careful attention should be given to the process. The first step is to consult the church’s bylaws to determine whether they offer specific guidance on resignations. Pastors should give enough notice to allow for a healthy handoff of leadership responsibilities but not so much notice that it leaves the congregation in a prolonged state of uncertainty. An extended farewell can prevent the church from moving forward effectively.

After reviewing the bylaws, the pastor should communicate with district officials. District leaders may have a recommended transition plan, be able to suggest qualified interim pastors, and help provide résumés or support for the church during the search for a new leader. Their involvement can offer structure, accountability, and encouragement to both the pastor and the congregation.

It is important to communicate the decision in the right sequence. Board members, staff, and key ministry leaders should be informed before a public announcement is made. It can be deeply disheartening for a committed leader to hear of the pastor’s departure at the same time as the general congregation. A clear, respectful communication plan helps avoid confusion and unintended hurt, and it demonstrates honor to those who serve alongside the pastor.

Ethical Conduct After Departure

Once the transition is complete and the pastor has stepped away, it is essential to allow the new pastor to lead without interference. This can be challenging, especially if reports of struggles or criticism begin to surface. However, it is not the role of the former pastor to critique the new pastor’s leadership or to entertain complaints from former congregants. Doing so violates ministerial courtesy and undermines the authority of the new leader. This type of interference could be considered a violation of ministerial courtesy.

Even when a former pastor is approached to perform a funeral or a wedding, it is best to direct the request to the current pastor. These events are important pastoral moments that help the new leader build bonds with the congregation. If the former pastor chooses to participate, it is the highest ethical standard to first seek the permission and blessing of the new pastor. This shows humility, respect, and a continued commitment to the well-being of the church.

**Q:** Explain the harm that can be done to the congregation if a former pastor interferes with the ministry of the new pastor.

**A:** It can be very confusing for congregants when a beloved former pastor undermines the current pastor. This creates tension and forces congregants to feel as though they must choose sides—either remain loyal to the former pastor or embrace the new one. Congregants should not be placed in that position.

### Friendships in Ministry

#### 10.5.1 — Describe how Scripture and AG commitments guide the use of AI in ministry, including the need to review AI-produced materials for doctrinal alignment.

Friendship is a vital part of life, including the life of a minister. Yet many leaders struggle with the question: Is it wise—or ethical—to be close friends with those I lead? Past generations of pastors often took a professional, distanced approach to ministry relationships. However, ministers today are increasingly aware of the value and need for authentic, supportive friendships. This lesson explores how ministers can develop healthy friendships with both peers and parishioners while maintaining appropriate boundaries and ethical integrity.

Friendships with Ministry Peers

Ministers do not have to walk alone. One of the best ways to stay emotionally and spiritually healthy is to build intentional friendships with other ministry colleagues. These may include other pastors, chaplains, counselors, campus ministry leaders, or staff members who serve in similar contexts. These peers understand the pressures and joys of ministry in ways others may not. They can offer valuable perspective, encouragement, and a safe space for honest conversation.

You can cultivate these relationships by reaching out to others at district and regional events, setting regular times to meet for coffee or conversation, or connecting through phone calls and prayer. Having trusted friends outside of your local church or ministry provides a lifeline, especially when internal issues arise that cannot be processed publicly.

Friendships with Those You Lead

The question of whether a minister should develop close friendships with members of their own church or ministry remains a topic of debate. Trull and Creech note, “A previous generation of pastors sometimes warned against this, opting for a more distanced, professional relationship with their parishioners” (2017, 81). However, such isolation can lead to loneliness and emotional strain. Ministry is not meant to be done in isolation.

In many cases, close relationships naturally form when ministers serve alongside others in the life of the church. These friendships can be deeply meaningful and spiritually enriching for both the minister and the layperson. That said, they require discernment and wisdom. Not every person who seeks friendship with a pastor does so with the right motives, and not every friendship will be healthy.

Potential Pitfalls and Healthy Boundaries

While friendships within the church are not unethical, they must be managed with integrity. One danger is the appearance—or reality—of favoritism. Ministers must be intentional about showing love and care to all members of the congregation, not just a select few. If a pastor only socializes with a specific group of friends, it can be perceived as exclusive or cliquish, causing others to feel left out or undervalued.

Ministers must also be prepared to make difficult decisions that may affect friends in the church. Leadership requires prioritizing what is best for the entire congregation, even if that means making a choice that disappoints a friend. Ministers must be willing to uphold confidentiality and avoid sharing sensitive information, even with those they trust personally. Friendship does not override pastoral responsibility.

If the minister eventually transitions out of the church or ministry, maintaining those friendships may still be possible—but requires careful navigation. Speaking negatively about the new pastor or interfering with their leadership damages the church and violates ethical boundaries. Friendship can continue, but it must not become a channel for division or criticism.

Balancing Friendship and Leadership

With the right boundaries, it is possible—and even healthy—to maintain friendships with those you lead. It is important to remember that in most cases, you will always be their pastor first and their friend second. For this reason, your closest and most transparent friendships should also include individuals who are not part of your current ministry setting. These outside friendships help you stay grounded and offer you places of rest, encouragement, and accountability.

God created us for relationship. Healthy, Christ-centered friendships support us in both life and leadership. With discernment, balance, and ethical care, ministers can enjoy the blessings of meaningful friendship while remaining faithful to their calling.

**Q:** Do you feel the benefits of developing a close friendship with someone under your leadership outweigh the pitfalls? Why or why not?

**A:** Answers will vary.

### Ethics in Missions

#### 10.6.1 — Identify the benefits of AI for ministers (e.g., automating administrative tasks) while recognizing its limitations in replacing spiritual discernment.

Missions has been a central focus of the Assemblies of God since its founding in 1914. One of the driving motivations for organizing the Fellowship was to more effectively support missionaries who were carrying the gospel around the world. Today, that commitment remains strong. The Assemblies of God encourages every minister and church to support both U.S. and world missions. This lesson explores the ethical responsibilities involved in supporting and participating in missions work—whether locally or internationally.

Supporting Missions Through Prayer and Finances

The first ethical duty related to missions is to provide faithful support through prayer and financial giving. The General Council Bylaws affirm that every Assemblies of God church should be involved in missions. Our General Superintendent has likewise emphasized that each church has a responsibility to support Assemblies of God World Missions (AGWM) and Assemblies of God U.S. Missions (AGUSM). This support is not merely organizational—it is a spiritual and ethical commitment to participate in fulfilling the Great Commission.

Voluntary Cooperation in Overseas Ministry

The principle of voluntary cooperation, emphasized throughout this course, is especially important in missions. When a church or minister plans to conduct ministry overseas, it is highly recommended that they work in partnership with Assemblies of God World Missions (AGWM). This usually begins with an invitation from an AGWM missionary serving in that region.

There are several reasons this partnership is essential. From a logistical perspective, an AGWM missionary can provide guidance on cultural sensitivities, local customs, language barriers, and safety issues. Their expertise helps ensure a more fruitful and respectful ministry experience. From a spiritual perspective, working through the AGWM structure prevents confusion and fosters unity among missionaries and churches. The presence of a local missionary also increases the likelihood that the fruit of the ministry trip will be long-lasting.

Partnership in U.S. Missions Contexts

These same principles apply to missions’ efforts within the United States. When churches or ministers desire to conduct local outreach in areas where Assemblies of God U.S. Missions is active, cooperation is encouraged. For example, if a church plans to send a short-term team to a college campus and a Chi Alpha missionary is already ministering there, it would be wise to coordinate with that missionary. Such collaboration avoids duplication, strengthens relationships, and contributes to greater impact. Communication is key—we are all part of the same team.

Cultural Awareness and Respect

Whether ministering in another country or in a cross-cultural setting within the United States, ministry participants must be intentional about showing respect for other cultures. This begins with humility and a desire to learn. Take time to understand the culture before you go. Ask questions, seek input from the local missionary, and avoid making assumptions.

A missionary once shared a story about a team visiting a culture where it was considered rude to shake hands while wearing gloves. Although the team had been warned, one member forgot and extended a gloved hand. While the recipient responded graciously, the action created a barrier that hindered ministry. Situations like this remind us that thoughtful preparation and cultural sensitivity are essential to an effective ministry.

Credentialing and Accountability

The General Council Bylaws also include a provision related to credentialed ministers who live or work outside of the United States. It states:

“Credentialed ministers who are living and working outside the United States, not under the auspices of Assemblies of God World Missions, are required to communicate their residency and ministry status to the appropriate Assemblies of God World Missions regional director for the purpose of credential renewal” (General Council Bylaws, Article XIII, Section 7, p. 78).

This policy exists to foster accountability and ensure that AGWM is aware of ministers operating in its areas of responsibility. It helps prevent confusion, builds unity, and promotes the value of working in cooperation with the broader Assemblies of God family.

Case Study Analysis

In this case study, students will apply the ethical decision-making process from Chapter 2 to evaluate how Pastor Russell could have responded differently when he learned of the potential sale of the homeless shelter.

Step 1: Clarify the Situation

Pastor Russell was contacted by several former parishioners expressing concern about the possible sale of the homeless shelter he had helped to establish. At that moment, he had a decision to make. Unfortunately, he chose to respond emotionally and reactively, without gathering all the relevant information. He assumed the new pastor was abandoning the ministry without justification.

What Pastor Russell likely did not know was that those serious structural issues—specifically with the foundation and plumbing—had made the shelter unsustainable for the church to maintain. Nor did he realize that the city intended to continue operating the shelter and serving the community. If Pastor Russell had taken time to clarify the facts before forming conclusions, he might have seen that the new pastor was not being careless but was facing a difficult decision.

The appropriate course of action would have been to reach out directly to Pastor Richard with humility and respect. A phone call or message, simply asking for clarity about the shelter’s situation, could have opened the door for understanding. However, if Pastor Russell chose to initiate that conversation, he would also need to be mindful of boundaries—not to pressure or attempt to influence the decisions of the new pastor.

Step 2: Understand the Values and Principles Involved

Several important biblical and ethical principles were at stake in this scenario. One key principle is the unity and health of the church. By calling former members and encouraging them to oppose the new pastor’s leadership, Pastor Russell damaged unity, disrupted trust, and stirred division. His actions undermined the spiritual and organizational health of the congregation.

Another important value is the ability of the current pastor to lead without interference. Pastor Richard had been entrusted with the responsibility of shepherding the church. Interfering with his leadership—even indirectly—violates this principle and makes it more difficult for the congregation to move forward under his care.

Had Pastor Russell taken time to reflect on these values—and to pray—he may have been more aware of the potential harm his actions could cause. A spirit of prayer could have helped him recognize any personal motivations rooted in nostalgia, pride, or emotional attachment that were not in alignment with God’s greater purpose for the church.

Step 3: Know the Rules

In addition to scriptural warnings against gossip, slander, and disrespect toward spiritual authority, the Assemblies of God General Council Bylaws explicitly address this kind of situation. Under Article IX, a “Violation of Ministerial Courtesy,” it is listed as a chargeable offense that can result in disciplinary action. This includes interfering in the ministry of another Assemblies of God pastor.

By contacting former congregants, urging them to oppose the sale, and criticizing the new pastor, Pastor Russell clearly violated this standard. His actions were not only unwise but also contrary to the ethical expectations of credentialed ministers.

Step 4: Consider the Options

If Pastor Russell had taken the time to follow the steps above—clarifying the facts, reflecting on core values, and reviewing biblical and organizational guidelines—he would have had a clearer path forward. He could have chosen to trust that God was leading the new pastor and the church through a difficult decision. He could have offered quiet support or simply chosen to remain silent, allowing the church to move forward in unity.

Though it was understandably difficult for him to see the shelter sold, Pastor Russell could have found peace in knowing that the shelter had served its purpose well during its season. Rather than allowing nostalgia or a sense of ownership to drive his actions, he could have celebrated what God had done and entrusted the future of the ministry to God’s ongoing work through new leadership.

In such moments, it is essential that every minister examine their motives before the Lord. Pride, sentimentality, or the desire to be needed can easily override what is best for the kingdom of God. Humble prayer and self-examination can help us surrender personal preferences and support the ministries God raises up—even when they are led by someone else.

## 11

### Technology as a Tool

#### 11.1.1 — Explain how churches should navigate their role as a voice for justice without becoming politicized, including the legal boundaries around endorsing candidates.

In today’s digital world, technology is a central part of both life and ministry. It can strengthen relationships, increase ministry reach, and provide new opportunities for discipleship. However, it also introduces potential distractions and dangers that require intentional reflection. Like power tools in a workshop, technology must be used with discernment and handled with care. Whether its effects on our lives remain neutral depends on how we use it and the values that guide our decisions.

This lesson will explore the opportunities and risks that come with using technology in the ministry. As spiritual leaders, we must learn to evaluate whether our digital habits are serving our calling or becoming a barrier to spiritual growth. With wisdom, we can use technology as a powerful servant of the gospel without allowing it to shape our priorities or distract us from God’s purposes.

The Power and Danger of Tools

Over the past few years, I have developed a love for woodworking. It is a therapeutic hobby that also allows me to produce beautiful and functional pieces of furniture. Each new power tool I have added to my collection has made it possible to accomplish tasks with greater precision and ease. I marvel at how these tools can turn raw materials into something useful and meaningful.

At the same time, I have learned to respect the danger of these tools. One moment of inattention—a hand too close to the blade or a piece of wood not secured properly—can have catastrophic consequences. Every time I power up my saw or drill, I remind myself of the responsibility that comes with using these tools. I love them because of what they enable me to create, but I handle them with great care, knowing their potential for harm.

In much the same way, technology is a powerful tool for the ministry. It can be a force for incredible good, enabling connection, creativity, and efficiency. Yet, just like my power tools, technology carries significant risks if used carelessly. Proverbs 4:23 reminds us, “Above all else, guard your heart for everything you do flows from it.” In the digital realm, guarding our hearts involves discerning what we consume and how we engage with technology to ensure it aligns with God’s purposes for our lives and ministries.

Opportunities in the Digital Age

When approached with wisdom, technology presents immense opportunities to enhance our ministry efforts. It breaks down geographic barriers, allowing us to share the gospel globally through tools like live-streamed sermons. Digital resources help ensure that worship services and ministry content are accessible to those unable to attend in person, whether due to illness, disability, or distance.

The digital age also fosters new avenues for online discipleship. Virtual Bible studies and social media platforms provide innovative ways to engage younger generations and facilitate spiritual growth through short devotionals, live question and answer sessions, and outreach.

Technology strengthens our ties to our church family by using communication tools like email, messaging apps, and church management software, making it easier for us to stay connected and foster a sense of community. Digital prayer communities allow believers to share prayer requests and support one another, forming vibrant spiritual networks.

Moreover, technology allows us to share resources, such as sermon videos, worship music, and e-books, equipping ministries and individuals seeking spiritual growth. It empowers younger generations to step into leadership roles in ways that resonate with their digital-first approach to life. In times of crisis, technology facilitates immediate support through online counseling, live prayer services, and coordinated relief efforts.

Finally, technology connects believers across cultures and geographic boundaries, fostering a sense of global unity in the body of Christ and expanding the reach of our ministry worldwide.

However, with these opportunities come risks. Constant digital engagement can detract from essential spiritual disciplines like prayer and Bible study. Overreliance on technology can stifle our creativity and undermine our engagement with the Holy Spirit, especially in tasks like sermon preparation. Digital communication can also weaken personal connections and reduce the depth of pastoral care, while social media’s addictive nature may lead to burnout and neglect of family and ministry duties.

Additionally, the ease of accessing harmful content can damage our integrity, and missteps online—such as insensitive posts—can permanently affect our reputation. Lastly, the temptation to prioritize entertainment over doctrinal purity can dilute the gospel message, making it essential to navigate these challenges with discernment.

Balancing Opportunity and Risk

Technology, like the tools in a workshop, is neither inherently good nor evil. Its impact depends on how we use it. As ministry leaders, we must approach technology with intentionality and wisdom, ensuring it remains a servant of the gospel rather than a distraction or stumbling block.

Here are a few questions we should reflect on as we engage with technology in the ministry:

What purpose does this tool serve in my ministry? Does it help fulfill God’s calling on my life, or is it simply a convenience or distraction?

How am I guarding my heart and mind in the digital realm? Am I allowing technology to shape my values, priorities, or spiritual health in unhealthy ways?

Am I leveraging technology for connection, or is it replacing personal interaction? How can I use it to complement, rather than compete with, real-life relationships?

Technology holds immense potential to advance God’s kingdom. By treating it with care and keeping it in proper perspective, we can harness its power for good while avoiding its pitfalls. When wielded with wisdom and respect, technology can be a means of creating beauty and utility in our ministry.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister can guard their heart “above all else” regarding the use of technology?

**A:** A minister can be mindful of both the quantity and quality of digital media they are exposed to. The minister should be wise in not becoming consumed with media and allowing it to replace spiritual disciplines.

**Q:** What are some ways you have used technology as an effective tool for ministry?

**A:** Answers may vary.

**Q:** Are you leveraging technology for connection, or is technology replacing personal interaction in your life? Explain.

**A:** Answers will vary. A thoughtful reflection might recognize the convenience of digital communication while acknowledging the importance of maintaining in-person relationships and being fully present with others.

### Healthy Communication Boundaries

#### 11.2.1 — Describe what it means for ministers to communicate with clarity and compassion, especially when delivering emotionally charged messages.

Digital communication has opened doors for ministry like never before, allowing leaders to connect, disciple, and shepherd more efficiently. Yet this increased access also introduces new vulnerabilities. Without intentional boundaries, online conversations can lead to misunderstandings, compromise, and even harm. Maintaining healthy communication habits is vital for protecting our integrity, our relationships, and the witness of the church.

In this lesson, we will explore practical ways to ensure our digital communication reflects wisdom and discretion. From choosing the right platform to setting time boundaries and involving accountability partners, ministry leaders must navigate this area with purpose. By setting limits and guarding our interactions, we model godly character and foster a culture of trust in every aspect of our communication.

In today’s interconnected world, technology has revolutionized the way we communicate, providing ministry leaders with unprecedented opportunities to share the gospel, build relationships, and connect with people. However, this connectivity also comes with potential challenges. Without wisdom and intentionality, it can blur boundaries and lead to unintended consequences. Setting healthy communication boundaries is essential to protect personal integrity, maintain meaningful relationships, and ensure our ministry efforts remain God-honoring.

Ministry is inherently relational, but technology can distort the dynamics it is meant to enhance. A seemingly harmless private message to a church member can inadvertently lead to misunderstandings or compromise over time. As leaders, we must be intentional in choosing the methods and platforms we use to interact.

Transparency is vital, especially when engaging with minors. Including parents or another leader in communication offers accountability and reassures families of the church’s integrity. Similarly, we must avoid overly casual or private interactions with the opposite sex to protect trust and prevent potential harm.

Establishing boundaries for when and how we engage with technology is essential. Constant notifications and emails can create pressure to always be available, disrupting the healthy rhythms of life and ministry. Ecclesiastes 3:1 reminds us, “There is a time for everything,” including rest. Setting specific hours for digital communication helps protect personal and family time. Turning off notifications during meals or creating screen-free zones in the home reinforces our priorities. These intentional habits strengthen well-being and model a balanced lifestyle for others.

For leaders, discretion should be exercised in choosing the proper mode of communication. Text messages are best used for quick, concise updates or to share brief information. For more detailed discussions or matters requiring additional context, emails are generally more appropriate as they allow for a well-thought-out explanation. However, when delivering emotionally charged messages or negative news, it is often best to opt for a phone call or, if possible, an in-person conversation.

Tone can be easily misinterpreted in written form, and what may seem straightforward in a text or email can come across as blunt or harsh—especially when the message involves sensitive topics. A phone call or face-to-face conversation provides a more personal connection. It allows for empathy and understanding to be conveyed in real-time and ensures that the intended tone and context are clear. These personal interactions help prevent misunderstandings and allow for more effective communication in difficult situations.

Leaders must also be discerning in the content they share online. Social media platforms serve as public spaces where words and images reflect our values (more on this in the next section). James 3:5 reminds us, “The tongue is a small part of the body, but it makes great boasts. Consider what a great forest is set on fire by a small spark.” Likewise, careless posts can have lasting repercussions. Our online presence must align with the gospel we preach.

The addictive nature of digital engagement, especially with continuous content like reels and video streams, is more than a matter of poor habits. It produces measurable effects on the brain. When a person scrolls through social media or watches short-form content, the brain releases dopamine—a chemical linked to pleasure and reward. This creates a feedback loop that makes the user want to continue engaging. Over time, the brain becomes conditioned to seek this quick reward, making it more difficult to focus on tasks that require sustained attention, such as Bible study or prayer. What begins as entertainment can subtly reshape patterns of thinking, priority, and spiritual sensitivity.

Because this is not just a personal discipline issue but a physiological one, ministry leaders must respond with intentionality and care. Understanding the brain’s reward system helps explain why simply deciding to stop is not always enough. Setting limits, using apps that monitor screen time, and choosing accountability partners are all helpful strategies. The wise leader will seek reliable resources on digital wellness and help others navigate this challenge with grace and truth. Addressing digital addiction is part of being a good shepherd—caring for both one’s own soul and the souls of others in a media-saturated world.

Accountability plays a significant role in maintaining healthy digital habits. We should invite trusted mentors or colleagues to review our online activity and ensure that we stay focused on our calling. Just as iron sharpens iron (Proverbs 27:17), accountability helps us remain grounded in our mission.

Healthy communication boundaries are not just about avoiding pitfalls but about intentionally using technology to glorify God and serve His people. By prioritizing transparency, rest, and accountability, we model integrity and navigate the digital age with grace, ensuring our interactions reflect Christ’s love and truth.

**Q:** What are some practices a minister can put into place to establish boundaries around digital communication to help maintain balance?

**A:** Have time set aside when you are not constantly checking your notifications. It is not necessary to be available all the time. On your Sabbath Day, do not answer emails unless it is an emergency.

### Social Media and the Minister

#### 11.3.1 — Explain how social media should be used with authenticity and intentionality by ministers, avoiding misuse for personal promotion.

Social media is one of the most influential platforms in today’s culture, shaping how people communicate, connect, and receive information. For ministers, it presents an opportunity to extend their voice beyond the church walls and engage the world with the message of the gospel. However, this powerful tool also presents unique challenges. Without clear boundaries and spiritual discernment, social media can easily lead to distraction, envy, or the pursuit of personal validation.

Social media has become a defining feature of modern communication, offering unparalleled opportunities for connection, influence, and outreach. As ministers, we recognize that these platforms provide a valuable tool for sharing the gospel, encouraging our congregations, and engaging with a broader audience. However, like any tool, social media must be used with authenticity and intentionality. Without healthy boundaries and practices, it can become a source of distraction, discouragement, or even spiritual harm.

The Dangers of Social Media

Social media can be a double-edged sword for us as ministers. On the one hand, it enables us to extend our reach and amplify our message. On the other hand, it presents significant risks that can compromise our integrity and ministry.

One of the most common pitfalls we face is the temptation to compare. Social media often highlights the successes and achievements of others, whether it’s another church’s rapidly growing congregation or a pastor’s polished sermon video that has gone viral. While these moments can inspire, they can also lead to envy or feelings of inadequacy. Proverbs 14:30 warns, “A heart at peace gives life to the body, but envy rots the bones.” As ministers, maintaining a spirit of contentment and gratitude is essential when navigating these platforms.

Another danger is the constant pursuit of validation. The culture of likes, shares, and comments can subtly shift our focus from serving God to seeking approval from followers. Galatians 1:10 reminds us, “Am I now trying to win the approval of human beings, or of God? Or am I trying to please people? If I were still trying to please people, I would not be a servant of Christ.” This verse emphasizes the importance of seeking God’s approval over human praise. We must continually guard our hearts against this temptation and remain centered on our calling.

Social media can also be a significant distraction. The endless scroll of posts, stories, and videos can consume hours that would be better spent in prayer, sermon preparation, or quality time with family. Additionally, engaging in debates or controversial topics can damage our reputation and create unnecessary division. Ephesians 4:29 encourages us to let our communication build “others up according to their needs, that it may benefit those who listen.” For us, this means exercising restraint and wisdom in our online interactions.

Healthy Practices for Social Media Use

Social media can be a powerful tool for ministry when used with discernment. We should prioritize authenticity by sharing transparent glimpses of our journey, including challenges and lessons learned, which foster trust and connection. This vulnerability reminds our followers that we, too, are human and dependent on God’s grace.

Social media should be approached with intentionality and purpose. Each post should reflect careful thought and align with God’s truth. Before sharing content, it is wise to ask whether the message builds others up and supports our ministry goals. Proverbs 16:23 states, “The hearts of the wise make their mouths prudent, and their lips promote instruction.” Thoughtful communication honors God and strengthens our witness.

Setting boundaries is another key practice for using social media in a healthy and God-honoring way. Without limits, digital engagement can easily consume time that should be devoted to rest, relationships, and spiritual discipline. Establishing designated screen-free hours, such as during family meals or personal devotional time, helps create balance and reinforces healthy rhythms of life. Ministry leaders should also monitor the total amount of time spent on social media each day and consider using tools that track or restrict usage if necessary.

In addition to time limits, it is essential to set clear boundaries around communication. Private or overly personal messaging—especially with minors or members of the opposite sex—can damage trust, invite misunderstanding, and compromise a leader’s integrity. Accountability in this area not only protects the leader but also models wisdom and transparency. Healthy boundaries serve as guardrails, helping ministers use social media responsibly while staying focused on their calling.

Accountability plays a significant role in healthy social media use. We should invite trusted mentors, colleagues, or spouses to review our social media activity. This helps us maintain focus on our calling and protect against digital temptations.

Ultimately, the goal of our social media presence should be to glorify God and serve His people. By practicing wisdom, humility, and discernment, we can effectively use social media to extend our ministry and make a positive impact in the digital age. Social media is a powerful tool, but it must remain a servant, not a master. When approached with wisdom, humility, and discernment, it can amplify our impact and serve as a valuable extension of our ministry in the digital age.

**Q:** What are some ways a minister can manage social media to avoid the trap of seeking approval and validation from people?

**A:** A minister can help address this by not always posting highlights about their ministry. Make posts that celebrate and encourage other people and lift up Christ and His mission.

### The Church and Politics
