#### 11.4.1 — Identify what it means to navigate the digital landscape with discernment and describe what an intentional digital lifestyle looks like for a minister.

Political conversations are everywhere in today’s culture, especially online—and ministry leaders often feel pressure to respond. While politics can shape the world we live in, the role of the minister is not to represent a political party but to represent Christ. As public figures in the digital age, our words carry weight, and we must carefully consider how political engagement affects our witness. The challenge is not whether to speak but how to speak in a way that reflects the gospel and maintains the unity of the body of Christ.

This lesson explores how ministers can navigate political discourse with wisdom, humility, and a clear focus on the gospel. By setting our sights on the kingdom of God, we avoid becoming entangled in divisive rhetoric. Our goal is not to win arguments or endorse platforms but to serve people, speak the truth in love, and point others to Christ in every context—including political ones.

In today’s digital age, political discourse often permeates every aspect of public life, including the online presence of ministry leaders. As political discussions intensify on social media and other platforms, we as ministry leaders face the challenge of balancing our role as spiritual guides with the pressure to engage in political debates. While political engagement is not inherently wrong, we must navigate this space with wisdom and care, ensuring our message aligns with the core purpose of the gospel.

Serving People, Not Political Parties

As ministers of the gospel, our primary role is to serve people, not political parties. This understanding should guide how we engage with the world around us, especially online. In the digital age, where every post, comment, and shared article has the potential to reach thousands, the influence of our words is magnified. We must remember that our calling is to serve individuals, regardless of their political views. Our ministry is about building relationships, fostering spiritual growth, and guiding people to Christ, not aligning with any political ideology.

Political ideologies can divide, but the gospel unites. The apostle Paul reminds us in 1 Corinthians 9:22, “I have become all things to all people so that by all possible means I might save some.” Our focus should be on reaching individuals for Christ and showing them His love rather than getting caught up in divisive political factions. By keeping our focus on serving people, we ensure our ministry remains centered on the transformative power of the gospel, not on temporary matters of political discourse.

Preaching the Gospel, Not Political Ideologies

We, as Christian leaders, have the responsibility to speak the truth, but the primary truth we are called to share is the gospel of Jesus Christ. While the gospel has implications for how we live in the world, it is not a political platform. We must avoid the temptation to closely align the message of Christ with any political ideology. When we preach the gospel, we call people to a relationship with Christ, not a particular political stance.

This doesn’t mean we should shy away from speaking the truth, especially when the gospel challenges cultural or political norms. If we are to offend, let it be for the sake of the gospel, not for political points. The gospel itself is offensive to the world because it confronts sin, but it should be our commitment to Christ and His word, not our political opinions, which causes offense. As Paul writes in Ephesians 4:15, “Speaking the truth in love, we will grow to become in every respect the mature body of him who is the head, that is, Christ.” The truth we speak must be grounded in love, with the ultimate goal of pointing others to Christ, not to our political views.

Furthermore, we must remember that we cannot expect nonbelievers to behave like believers. The Bible calls us to love our enemies and pray for those who persecute us (Matthew 5:44), not to engage in hostile political battles. Non-Christians, by nature, do not share our values or worldview, and we should not hold them to the same standards. While we can and should engage with the issues of justice, morality, and society from a biblical perspective, we must do so with grace and humility, recognizing that our role is to point others to Christ, not to a political ideology.

The Kingdom of God Is Bigger Than Political Parties

In Philippians 3:20, Paul reminds us, “But our citizenship is in heaven. And we eagerly await a Savior from there, the Lord Jesus Christ.” As Christians, our ultimate allegiance is to God’s kingdom, not any earthly kingdom or political system. We are citizens of heaven first, and this perspective should shape our engagement with politics. While we may have opinions on political matters, we must remember that our true identity and allegiance lie with God’s eternal kingdom.

Even when political leaders espouse policies or behaviors that are contrary to biblical values, we are still called to pray for them. 1 Timothy 2:1–2 urges us to pray “for all people—for kings and all those in authority, that we may live peaceful and quiet lives in all godliness and holiness.” This command is especially vital in the context of our digital presence, where it’s easy to express frustration, anger, or condemnation in a moment of political disagreement. Instead of using our platforms to criticize, we should encourage our congregations to pray for wisdom and guidance for those in power, trusting that God is ultimately in control.

Praying for our leaders, even those with whom we disagree, demonstrates humility and a recognition that God works through all authorities, whether or not they align with our personal beliefs. It’s an act of obedience that transcends political lines, showing the world that our trust is not in political systems but in the sovereignty of God.

A Tone of Speaking the Truth in Love

As ministry leaders, our engagement with political issues online must be guided by the principle of speaking the truth in love, as Paul reminds us in Ephesians 4:15, “Instead, speaking the truth in love, we will grow to become in every respect the mature body of him who is the head, that is, Christ.” This balance is especially important in the digital space, where tone can be easily misinterpreted. Unlike face-to-face communication, written words lack nuance, which can lead to misunderstandings.

**Q:** What are some prominent moral issues in today’s culture that a minister may need to address? How can the minister address these without being overly political?

**A:** Examples could include topics such as abortion, same-sex marriage, racial issues, or ministry to the poor. A minister should address these topics by speaking the truth of God’s Word, not by tying them to a political party.

### The Minister and the Use of Artificial Intelligence

#### 11.5.1 — Identify how ministers are using AI in ministry (e.g., sermon preparation, administrative work) and explain why AI-produced materials must be reviewed for doctrinal alignment.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming many aspects of society, including ministry. From sermon preparation to administrative tasks, AI tools offer exciting new possibilities to increase efficiency and expand outreach. However, as ministry leaders, we must approach this technology with careful discernment. AI can be helpful, but it cannot replace the personal presence, spiritual insight, and relational care that are central to effective ministry.

This lesson will explore the opportunities and ethical challenges AI presents. We will examine how to use AI in ways that honor biblical truth, protect privacy, and preserve the human connections that are essential to our calling. When approached wisely, AI can support ministry goals without compromising the spiritual integrity and relational depth of pastoral work.

AI is reshaping ministry, offering tools that enhance efficiency in tasks like sermon preparation and administrative work. However, it also presents challenges that require our discernment to ensure it does not replace the essential personal and spiritual connections at the heart of ministry.

A key caution is the potential loss of human connection. While AI can assist with tasks like sermon writing or communication, it cannot replace pastoral care, counseling, and spiritual guidance, which require personal interaction and empathy. We must be careful not to let technology overshadow face-to-face engagement with our congregation.

AI also lacks spiritual discernment. It processes data but cannot understand the deeper spiritual needs of individuals. We should not rely on AI for decisions requiring prayerful insight or personal wisdom, especially in counseling. Ethical concerns arise when AI-generated content, such as sermon outlines or social media posts, deviates from biblical principles, so we must ensure that AI-produced materials align with our doctrinal values.

Privacy and data security are also significant concerns when using AI tools in the ministry. Many platforms and applications process sensitive information, such as prayer requests, counseling notes, attendance records, or history. If not handled properly, this data could be exposed or misused, damaging trust and potentially causing harm to individuals or the church as a whole. Ministry leaders must ensure that any AI tools used comply with applicable privacy regulations and ethical standards. This includes reviewing terms of service, limiting access to personal data, and using secure systems for storage and communication. Protecting the confidentiality of our congregation is not only a legal obligation but also a spiritual responsibility. Maintaining trust requires that people know their personal information is treated with care, integrity, and discretion.

Despite these challenges, AI offers significant benefits for the ministry. It can automate administrative tasks, saving us time for sermon preparation, pastoral care, and community engagement. AI can also assist in sermon preparation by gathering resources, suggesting Scripture, and creating outlines. Furthermore, AI can provide personalized outreach by analyzing data to meet specific congregational needs and extending the ministry beyond the local congregation through tools like chatbots and virtual assistants.

While AI can enhance ministry, it must be used wisely. We must ensure that technology supports, not replaces, the human and spiritual aspects of our calling.

Berean’s AI Use Policy, called the Sandwiching Approach, uses a three-step process—Human Input, AI Structuring, and Human Review—to make sure AI is used helpfully and ethically while keeping accuracy, clear values, and human oversight in all academic and administrative work.

Epilogue to Lesson 11.5

At the conclusion of this lesson, the Berean staff asked an AI tool to analyze the content. The following is its unedited analysis:

Lesson 11.5 is timely and relevant, and overall, it is well-structured. Here is my honest assessment, organized into strengths and suggested improvements:

Strengths

Balanced Perspective

The lesson does an excellent job of presenting both the benefits and concerns surrounding AI. It does not demonize technology but instead emphasizes responsibility, ethical uses something your students will appreciate.

Clear Ethical Framework

Including Berean’s “Sandwiching Approach” is wise. It shows students how to use AI thoughtfully with human oversight, aligning with the values of integrity and biblical faithfulness.

Protecting the Core of Ministry

The lesson affirms that AI cannot replace pastoral care, spiritual discernment, or empathy. This keeps the focus on the minister’s role as a shepherd, not just a content creator or administrator.

Relevance to Real Ministry Tasks

The examples—like sermon prep, administrative tasks, and outreach—are practical and grounded in real ministry contexts. Students will see how these apply immediately.

Suggestions for Improvement

Deepen the Theology of Discernment

You briefly mention that AI lacks spiritual discernment. A short explanation of why spiritual discernment matters—perhaps referencing James 1:5 or 1 Corinthians 2:14—would add depth and reinforce the minister’s unique role.

Clarify Boundaries in Sermon Prep

You might add one sentence noting that while AI can help generate ideas or structure, the message must come from time in the Word and prayer. That will further guard against overreliance.

Privacy and Pastoral Ethics

The point on data security is strong but could be expanded just slightly. A simple real-world example (e.g., using a chatbot to schedule counseling sessions and protecting that data) would bring this home.

Conclusion

Lesson 11.5 is forward-thinking and grounded in pastoral wisdom. It encourages responsible engagement with emerging technology without compromising spiritual integrity. With just a touch more theological reflection and practical example, it would be a standout capstone for this chapter.

**Q:** If using artificial intelligence for sermon preparation research, what cautions should the minister be aware of?

**A:** Artificial intelligence does not have spiritual discernment. When using AI, the minister should not assume that AI has the correct doctrine, and everything needs to be verified.

### Legal Considerations

#### 11.6.1 — Explain the addictive nature of digital engagement and describe how ministers should address it as a matter of personal discipline.

In an increasingly digital world, ministry leaders must be aware that every email, post, or message contributes to a permanent digital footprint. What is written in a moment—whether public or private—can resurface, be taken out of context, or become part of legal proceedings. While technology offers many benefits for communication and outreach, it also introduces risks that require careful attention and professional conduct.

This lesson explores the legal implications of digital communication in ministry. From maintaining confidentiality to avoiding defamatory or inappropriate content, we must recognize that our online activity is not exempt from ethical and legal standards. As spiritual leaders, our responsibility includes safeguarding both our witness and the trust of those we serve by handling all digital interactions with integrity and caution.

In the digital age, we must be aware that everything we share online, both public and private, creates a permanent digital footprint. Whether it’s a social media post, sermon, email, or private message, digital content can be archived, retrieved, or exposed, potentially resurfacing at any time and impacting our reputation, ministry, or legal standing. Even seemingly private communications—emails or text messages—can be used against us in legal disputes or public controversies.

We must understand that the digital world does not offer true privacy. Anything we share online can be exposed, misinterpreted, or used in legal contexts, which makes it crucial for us to exercise caution and professionalism in all digital communication. Just as we would avoid harmful words or actions in person, the same care should be applied to our online presence. We should avoid writing anything that could be defamatory, inappropriate, or divisive, as such actions could harm our reputation or lead to legal consequences.

We must also remember that digital offenses are no less significant than physical ones. For example, threats made via text or sexting are fully chargeable offenses and can lead to legal consequences, including criminal charges. These actions may seem less tangible than physical acts, but they carry real, lasting consequences. In the case of ministry leaders, this underscores the importance of maintaining integrity both online and offline. Just as physical misconduct can lead to serious repercussions, digital offenses should be taken with the same level of seriousness, as they too can damage reputations, relationships, and ministry effectiveness.

Given the lasting nature of a digital footprint, we must establish clear boundaries for digital communication, particularly for sensitive matters like prayer requests, counseling, or finances. These should never be shared on unencrypted or unsecured channels. Secure platforms must be used for confidential communications, and explicit permission should always be obtained before sharing personal stories or sensitive data.

While digital tools offer vast opportunities for ministry, we must be mindful of the potential legal repercussions. Every online action has lasting consequences, so we need to practice discernment and protect both our legal standing and the trust of our congregation. By navigating the digital world with care, we can safeguard our integrity, protect our ministry, and uphold our moral and ethical responsibilities.

### Overarching Ethic: Navigating the Digital World with Integrity

#### 11.7.1 — Describe what it means for technology to enhance rather than replace personal faith and relationships, and explain how ministers should navigate the digital world with intentionality.

Technology has become a powerful part of everyday life and ministry, but it must be approached with wisdom and purpose. As Christian leaders, we are called to engage in the digital world without losing sight of our core values and spiritual disciplines. Technology is not inherently good or bad—it is a tool that reflects the heart and intentions of the user. If used with integrity and discernment, it can amplify the gospel, support relationships, and serve God’s purposes in a global context.

This lesson calls us to adopt an overarching biblical ethic for all digital engagement. Our posts, messages, and online presence should reflect Christ’s character—marked by love, humility, and truth. Technology should never replace the personal relationships and spiritual habits that define a faithful life. Instead, it should serve the mission of God, supporting authentic ministry, promoting justice, and drawing others toward Christ through our consistent witness.

As we engage in the digital world, we must do so with an overarching ethic grounded in biblical principles. Technology is neither inherently good nor bad, but its impact is determined by how we use it. We must approach technology intentionally, using it to further God’s kingdom while maintaining a strong commitment to our faith, relationships, and ministry. In this digital age, the goal is not to allow technology to define ministry but to ensure that it serves as an extension of authentic Christian living.

First and foremost, we must approach technology from a biblical and godly perspective. This means using digital tools in ways that honor God and align with His mission. Just as Scripture provides guidance for our behavior and relationships in the physical world, it also provides wisdom for our interactions in the digital realm. We should view our use of technology as a reflection of our values, seeking to embody Christ’s love, grace, and truth in every online interaction. From social media posts to email communications, everything we do digitally should reflect a heart of integrity, humility, and respect.

The use of technology should primarily serve to promote the gospel, foster relationships, and advocate for justice. Technology can be a powerful platform for sharing the message of Christ with a global audience, whether through online sermons, social media, or digital outreach initiatives. We can use these platforms to encourage others, share biblical truths, and offer hope. However, as we engage in the digital world, it is also vital to recognize that the essence of ministry is rooted in relationships. Technology can facilitate connections as a goal but should never replace face-to-face personal relationships and personal faith. Furthermore, technology can be a tool for justice, allowing us to advocate for the marginalized, address societal issues, and promote compassion in ways that align with God’s call for His people to love their neighbors.

Above all, we must remember that technology should enhance—not replace—our embodiment of faith. It should not be a substitute for the deep spiritual disciplines that form the foundation of Christian life, such as prayer, Scripture reading, and worship. While digital platforms can offer resources and facilitate engagement, they should never diminish the importance of personal devotion and community worship. Technology should support us in our calling, helping us to be more effective in reaching people and fostering deeper relationships, but it must never take precedence over the practices that nourish the soul and draw us closer to God.

We must navigate the digital world with intentionality, using technology to enhance our ministry without compromising our Biblical values. By promoting the gospel, fostering relationships, and advocating justice, we can use these powerful tools to further God’s kingdom. Yet we must remain vigilant, ensuring that technology enhances our embodiment of faith rather than replacing it. With discernment and a biblical mindset, we can harness the benefits of the digital age while remaining true to our calling and our faith.

**Q:** What is your overarching biblical ethic for all digital engagement?

**A:** A strong response might include using technology to reflect Christ’s character, speak truth in love, build up the body of Christ, and remain accountable to biblical values. Digital engagement should be marked by humility, integrity, and a focus on glorifying God rather than self. Good answers should include a corresponding Bible verse. 12 CHAPTER

## 12

### Child Safety and Supervision

#### 12.1.1 — Explain the framework for child safety in ministry, including background checks, the 'Never Alone' rule, and reporting obligations.

Sexual abuse remains a serious issue in society, and unfortunately, churches are not immune. When abuse occurs within a ministry context, it causes deep harm not only to the child but also to the family, the church community, and the witness of the gospel. Beyond the personal trauma, such events can distort a child’s view of spiritual authority and even their understanding of God. Accusations of abuse can also severely damage a church’s credibility in the community. For these reasons, church leaders carry an ethical responsibility to establish strong systems that protect children and youth from harm.

The goal of implementing safety measures should not be limited to avoiding lawsuits. The deeper motivation must come from a desire to provide a secure environment where children can hear the gospel and experience the love of God without fear. Parents trust ministry leaders to take this responsibility seriously. The church should be the safest place in the world for its most vulnerable members.

General Guidelines

This lesson does not provide a complete child safety policy. Instead, it emphasizes the importance of developing strong protection systems and outlines several key components to consider. Every church or ministry must tailor its approach to its specific setting. Leaders should consult with local legal counsel, their insurance provider, and their district office when creating or updating their policy. These steps help ensure the policy is both legally sound and effective for the ministry.

In a 2025 video for district leaders, Assemblies of God Legal Counsel Kristen Waggoner shared helpful guidance on this topic. The following points are summarized from that resource and provide a framework to strengthen your ministry’s approach to child safety.

Establish a Policy

Every church working with minors should have a written safety policy. This policy should reflect a genuine desire to create a safe and welcoming place for children, not just to reduce liability. The policy must be appropriate for your church’s context and reviewed by legal counsel. Policies from local schools or nonprofits can serve as helpful benchmarks. A well-written policy sets expectations and demonstrates a commitment to protecting minors.

Establish a Strong Selection Process

Churches must apply a thorough screening process to all who work with minors, whether paid or volunteer. This process should include a written application, a criminal background check, and reference checks, especially institutional references. It is important to ask each reference whether there is any reason the applicant should not work with minors. An interview with ministry leaders is also recommended, and churches should require applicants to be involved in the church for a reasonable period before serving with minors.

Communicate the Policy and Provide Training

It is not enough to store the policy in a binder. It must be clearly communicated and supported by regular training. All leaders and volunteers who work with minors should understand the policy and agree to follow it. Training should occur at least annually and cover both the principles and the practical procedures involved. This ongoing reinforcement helps ensure consistent application.

Proper Supervision

A safe environment for children and youth depends on active, responsible supervision by staff and volunteers. Adults serving in these roles must remain fully engaged during all ministry activities involving minors. If your policy includes leader-to-child ratios, these must be followed carefully. At all times, a minimum of two previously-screened adults should be present. This protects not only the children but also the ministry workers from misunderstandings or false accusations.

When a child requires individual attention, two adults should be involved in that interaction. As a rule, one-on-one interaction should be avoided entirely. This includes both in-person meetings and communication through technology, such as text messaging or social media. If a message must be sent to a minor, it should take place in a group format that includes parents or additional adults and minors.

High-risk situations must be clearly avoided. These include allowing an unrelated minor to spend the night at a leader’s home, sleeping alone in a tent with a minor, traveling alone with a minor on a day or overnight trip, or riding in a vehicle one-on-one. Careful and consistent supervision helps prevent serious incidents and protects everyone involved.

Report Allegations Promptly

If a ministry worker receives a report, or has reasonable cause to suspect, that a minor has been or may be subjected to abuse, it should be reported to the proper authorities. Become familiar with the mandatory reporting laws in your state and where to report. Even if your state does not require ministry workers to report, it is prudent to do so. The General Council voluntarily considers itself to be a mandatory reporter, regardless of state law. The reason should be obvious—we desire for children and youth to be protected.

It would be beneficial for your policy to include the process for how reports are made. The church should keep a record of all reports made. A key element of your policy is that an offender never gets a second chance to work with minors. While there is forgiveness available from the Lord to the offender, it would be unwise to allow them a second chance at working with minors.

Review Policies Regularly

Policies should be reviewed annually and adjusted as needed. Comparing your policy with those used by public schools or other ministries can help keep your approach current. Regular reviews communicate that your church is serious about protecting children and committed to responsible leadership.

Churches that develop and follow effective child safety policies help create a secure environment where children can learn and grow in their faith. These systems also protect the church’s reputation and reduce the risk of legal issues. Most importantly, they reflect the heart of Jesus, who welcomed children and called His followers to do the same.

**Q:** What safeguards have you adopted in the church or ministry to which you belong to protect kids and youth?

**A:** a) AI can automate administrative tasks. b) It can write your sermons for you. c) It can visit the sick in the hospital. d) It can make your church bulletin look neat.

### Financial Integrity

#### 12.2.1 — Describe what happens after a substantiated abuse report and explain why offenders are permanently barred from working with minors.

In the Parable of the Talents, Jesus emphasized the importance of faithfully managing the resources He has entrusted to us (Matthew 25:14–30). This responsibility extends beyond spiritual matters and includes financial stewardship. Ministry leaders are called to handle church finances with care, wisdom, and integrity. However, many churches struggle in this area. One common reason is the lack of trained personnel. A church treasurer or bookkeeper is often a volunteer who brings little or no financial experience to the role. This lack of training becomes a greater problem when combined with the growing complexity of financial matters such as payroll, insurance, charitable contributions, and ever-changing legal requirements.

Despite these challenges, the stakes remain high. Churches that fail to handle finances properly may find themselves with fewer resources to carry out their mission. They may also lose donor trust and harm the public witness of the church. To guard against these outcomes, every church must implement a sound financial system. This system should include competent financial administration, strong internal controls, and clear financial reporting. It is the ethical responsibility of both the ministry leader and the church board to make sure this system is in place and functioning effectively.

Competent Administration of Finances

It is essential that every church manage its finances competently and in full compliance with applicable laws. Financial administration is not just a practical necessity; it is a matter of ethical responsibility. Many churches choose to partner with a Certified Public Accountant (CPA) or a bookkeeping service that specializes in nonprofit or church accounting. These professionals can oversee the church’s finances directly or train staff members to manage them effectively, ensuring that all procedures align with legal and ethical standards.

In addition to professional services, churches should make use of denominational support. District offices often have personnel available to offer training and assistance. One highly recommended resource is the Church & Clergy Tax Guide , published annually by Church Law and Tax. This guide addresses a wide range of topics relevant to church finance. During my sixteen years as Business Administrator for the Alaska Ministry Network, I referred to it frequently.

The General Treasurer’s office of the Assemblies of God also provides valuable resources. They work alongside districts to host General Treasurer’s Conferences which are designed to equip local church treasurers. Additional tools and information can be found on their website, including the helpful newsletter Church Administration Essentials . Churches should take the lead in setting the standard for financial competence, modeling integrity in every area of stewardship.

Internal Controls

Fraud is a serious issue for organizations of every kind, including churches. According to the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners , 10.4 percent of 1,388 fraud cases examined in 2012 occurred within nonprofit organizations. In 87 percent of those cases, fraud was committed by first-time offenders with no prior criminal record. The median loss for nonprofits that experienced fraud was $100,000 (Dimos 2014, 18–19). These statistics show that churches are not immune from financial misconduct.

Fraud or theft often results from a combination of motive and opportunity. A person’s motive may vary—perhaps financial pressure, feeling undervalued, or some other personal justification. When that motive is paired with the opportunity to access funds without proper oversight, it can lead to temptation. Fortunately, churches can significantly reduce the opportunity by establishing sound financial policies with strong internal controls. Implementing these controls does not mean the church does not trust its volunteers or staff. In fact, people who serve in financial roles are typically trusted individuals. Still, systems of accountability are essential. As President Ronald Reagan is known to have wisely said, “Trust but verify.”

There are several reasons every church should establish strong internal controls. First, ministry leaders are stewards of God’s resources, and they are responsible to use those resources wisely. Poor controls can result in stolen funds that could have otherwise advanced the mission of the church. Second, strong financial systems help earn the trust of members and donors by demonstrating that their contributions are being handled with care. Third, internal controls protect people from false accusations. For example, if one person counts the offering alone, that individual may become vulnerable to suspicion. However, if multiple unrelated individuals count the offering together using a defined process, everyone is protected. Finally, internal controls help prevent theft by removing unnecessary opportunities to mishandle funds. When churches establish a culture of accountability, their systems will safeguard the ministry for years to come—even after current leaders have moved on.

This course will not provide a full internal control policy, but it will highlight some key concepts and point to helpful resources. One valuable tool is Rollie Dimos’s book Integrity at Stake: Safeguarding Your Church’s Honor . The book contains case studies and best practices for financial protection. Your Network or District Office may also offer sample policies. Additionally, a CPA or another financial professional with experience in church settings can help your church craft policies that meet its specific needs. These experts can also review your existing system and identify any weaknesses.

One essential concept is “two eyes on every transaction.” For example, at least two unrelated individuals should be involved when receiving and counting the offering. The funds should be kept in a secure location. The same principle should apply to disbursing funds. Whether issuing a check, making an electronic transfer, or using a credit card, at least two unrelated people should be involved in the transaction.

A related concept is segregation of duties. No one person should control all parts of a financial process. For example, the individual who writes the checks should not reconcile the checking account. This separation of responsibilities ensures no single person has complete control over the church’s finances. Without this protection, churches risk financial mismanagement or even embezzlement.

Churches of all sizes need strong internal controls in place. It is the responsibility of church leaders to develop these systems and ensure they are consistently followed. In doing so, the church protects its resources, honors its calling, and preserves its testimony before others.

Reporting

Accountability and transparency are essential to healthy church life. One of the most effective ways to build trust among church members is through regular, clear financial reporting. Reconciled financial statements should be provided to the church board on a routine basis, with opportunities for board members to ask questions and engage in discussion. These reports should give an accurate picture of the church’s financial position and activity.

Beyond board meetings, financial reports should also be shared with the entire church membership at least once a year during an annual business meeting. This is more than a legal or organizational requirement, it is a relational investment. One of my former pastors often said, “An informed people is a happy people.” When financial information is presented openly, it helps church members feel respected and included. If reports are withheld, even unintentionally, members may begin to assume something is being hidden. This can quickly lead to a lack of trust—not only in financial matters but in other areas of leadership as well.

Providing transparent and consistent financial reporting reassures the congregation that their contributions are being managed wisely and ethically. It also reinforces the shared mission of the church and strengthens unity among its members.

**Q:** What are the reasons for having two unrelated people count the offering?

**A:** a) losing your position of leadership. b) stylistic changes in the worship. c) dictatorial leadership. d) lazy leadership.

**Q:** In what ways does annual reporting of the finances to the membership engender trust?

**A:** a) Proper conjugation of Hebrew verbs b) Correctly handling the Word of Truth c) Using the Bible as a proof-text d) There were no ethical requirements given for biblical interpretation.

### Governance Systems

#### 12.3.1 — Explain the principle of 'two eyes on every transaction' and describe how proper financial oversight protects the church.

A church’s governance system plays a critical role in maintaining the health and integrity of its ministry. When the structure is balanced and functions well, it fosters accountability, shared leadership, and long-term stability. Churches within the Assemblies of God typically follow a governance pattern that reflects both spiritual leadership and congregational involvement. These systems are not just administrative—they are safeguards to preserve the mission and values of the church.

The governance structure of a church has a significant impact on the overall health of its ministry. Assemblies of God churches typically have board members who are either elected by the congregation or appointed and then ratified by the congregation. Pastors are usually elected by the congregation as well. In most cases, there is a process in place to remove a pastor or board member when necessary. These safeguards are designed to prevent a single leader from exercising dictatorial control over the church.

Assemblies of God churches are generally pastor-led. God gives vision to the pastor, who sets the direction and ministries of the church in collaboration with the church board. In a healthy church, the pastor leads with clarity and purpose while the board provides support and accountability. Assistant General Superintendent Rick DuBose addresses this dynamic in his book The Church That Works . A wise pastor will pay close attention to any concerns raised by the church board.

The most effective governance systems allow the pastor to lead while maintaining proper safeguards. A healthy system encourages shared leadership, provides appropriate accountability, and prevents future leaders from misusing their authority. The General Council provides sample bylaws on its website (https://www.ag.org) that churches can adopt to help maintain this balance.

**Q:** Why is it healthy for the membership to elect or ratify board members and to elect a pastor?

**A:** a) Discernment, intentionality, and wisdom b) Engagement, relevance, and consistency c) Political, ethnic, and religious considerations d) Hammer, axe, and chain saw

### Handling Complaints

#### 12.4.1 — Describe the safeguards ministers should use to protect their personal and professional use of church resources.

Every church will, at some point, encounter complaints. How those complaints are handled can either strengthen or harm the health and witness of the ministry. Scripture offers both precedent and wisdom in addressing complaints with seriousness, wisdom, and compassion. Acts 6 provides a biblical example that still applies today: leaders must be proactive, impartial, and Spirit-led in their response.

Churches should have a clear process for handling complaints. In Acts 6, a complaint arose when the Hellenistic Jews believed their widows were being overlooked in the daily food distribution (verse 1). The apostles addressed the issue by appointing seven men who were full of the Spirit and wisdom. These men were commissioned through prayer and the laying on of hands to fulfill the neglected responsibility. Because the issue was handled promptly and with integrity, the result was significant. Scripture records, “The word of God spread. The number of disciples in Jerusalem increased rapidly, and a large number of priests became obedient to the faith” (Acts 6:7).

When complaints are not addressed properly, the results can be harmful. If people feel their concerns are ignored, they may turn to social media, the press, or even the legal system. These actions can damage the church’s reputation, hinder its witness, and create financial and legal consequences. Churches can avoid these outcomes by responding to complaints with wisdom and compassion, following the example of the early church.

This section is focused on substantive complaints made in good faith. Pastors and ministry leaders often receive casual feedback, such as “I did not enjoy the worship today” or “Why do we no longer have Sunday night services?” These do not require formal investigation. In such cases, the pastor can listen with compassion and respond appropriately. However, more serious complaints require a structured and intentional response.

A second important clarification is that when a complaint is received about a credentialed Assemblies of God minister and the issue could qualify as one of the chargeable offenses listed in Article X of the General Council Bylaws, the complaint should be referred to the district or network office for investigation. The Assemblies of God governance structure assigns responsibility for such investigations to the district level. Local churches are not responsible for investigating these matters. A list of chargeable offenses was provided in Chapter 9.

For noncredentialed staff or ministry volunteers, the responsibility for investigation lies with the church leadership. Acts 6 provides a strong model. The apostles responded immediately and treated the complaint seriously. They did not delay or ignore it, hoping it would go away. Taking swift action protected the church’s mission and its people. Failing to report or investigate can allow ongoing harm, expose the church to liability, and damage the reputation of the accused, especially if the complaint is later found to be untrue. Leaders must not dismiss a concern based on personal familiarity, such as, “We know John. He would never do that,” or because the person is related to the pastor. Every complaint made in good faith should be investigated without favoritism.

Investigations serve to hold the guilty accountable and to clear the names of those who are innocent. They also create a record of the leadership’s response in the event the issue resurfaces in the future. For those who are falsely accused, a thorough investigation is necessary to protect their reputation.

The apostles also demonstrated wisdom by handling the complaint as a team. Peter did not act alone. A team approach remains wise today. The board may serve in this role, or the pastor and board may appoint a team to investigate. It is important to select the right people. Acts 6 emphasizes the importance of leaders who are full of the Spirit and wisdom. Depending on the nature of the complaint, the board may choose to include or consult experts. A balanced team of men and women is helpful, especially when interviews are conducted. In some situations, it may be wise to consult an attorney or notify the church’s insurance company. In serious matters, the pastor should seek guidance from the district or network office.

Complaints involving sexual misconduct toward minors require immediate and careful action. If there is a reasonable cause to suspect abuse, the matter must be reported immediately to the appropriate legal authorities. It is not necessary to have proof, nor should the church conduct its own investigation before reporting. Many states designate clergy as mandatory reporters, but every church has a moral obligation to protect children. The General Council considers itself a mandatory reporter and encourages local churches to follow the same standard. When an allegation involves a minor, the accused should be removed from any ministry involving minors until the matter is resolved. If the individual is found guilty, they should never again be allowed to serve in ministry to minors.

Finally, the apostles prayed. They did not rely solely on their own wisdom. As complaints are investigated today, church leaders must depend on the Holy Spirit for discernment and direction. Every person involved should be treated with respect, and all information should be handled with appropriate confidentiality.

### Compound Problems

#### 12.5.1 — Identify the commitments required of ministers in the area of financial integrity, including avoiding misappropriation and maintaining transparency.

Each of the systems discussed in this chapter—child protection, financial integrity, governance, and complaint response—plays a critical role in building a healthy and ethical ministry. These are not isolated concerns. When one area is neglected, it often indicates or contributes to weaknesses in others. When these failures combine, they can damage a church’s reputation, disrupt its mission, and bring dishonor to the name of Christ. Churches that commit to implementing these systems are better equipped to resist the enemy’s schemes and to walk in truth and righteousness.

This chapter has outlined four essential systems or processes for every church or ministry. Child protection policies are necessary to ensure the safety of minors in ministry spaces. Financial integrity systems help churches steward their resources wisely and prevent financial misconduct. Governance structures provide healthy oversight, preventing the rise of authoritarian leadership or negligent inaction. Complaint procedures allow churches to address serious concerns in a way that protects people, honors the truth, and keeps the mission on course.

In practice, churches that have significant problems in one of these areas often struggle in at least one other. For example, a church that has relaxed or nonexistent child safety protocols may also fail to investigate complaints appropriately. Churches without functioning governance structures often empower dictatorial leaders, and those same churches may lack financial accountability. These weaknesses tend to occur together. When left unaddressed, they grow and become compounded. Over time, such churches may develop reputations that do not honor Christ. These are often churches that attract negative attention from the media or the public.

Paul instructed believers: “Put on the full armor of God, so that you can take your stand against the devil’s schemes” (Ephesians 6:11). Just as the enemy has strategies to destroy individual believers, he also has plans to damage and divide churches. The church is God’s plan to bring the gospel to the world and set people free. That is why the enemy targets it.

When churches establish and uphold strong systems of accountability and integrity, they are not only safeguarding their ministries—they are resisting spiritual attack. Paul writes, “Stand firm then, with the belt of truth buckled around your waist, with the breastplate of righteousness in place” (Ephesians 6:14). These verses remind us that spiritual strength includes truth and righteousness, both of which are demonstrated when a church governs itself wisely, protects its people, and handles problems with justice and grace.

Churches that commit to these systems are positioned to continue their mission with focus and faithfulness. They are less likely to be sidetracked by scandal or conflict. Instead, they will be examples of integrity, truth, and righteousness in their communities and beyond.

Case Study Analysis

This is a tragic story. While the specific details are fictitious, the scenario is all too familiar in youth-serving organizations. It illustrates the kind of compound problems described in the previous lesson. In this case, the church failed in both the child safety protocols and in responding to complaints. The result was devastating.

This situation could have been prevented if the church had implemented a thorough process for selecting youth workers. A basic criminal background check would have revealed Tyler’s prior offenses and disqualified him from serving in youth ministry. In addition, reference checks from his former church may have brought forward serious concerns.

The church also lacked any meaningful supervision policies. This allowed Tyler to develop inappropriate access to vulnerable minors. He was able to send private messages to young boys and spend unsupervised time with them both inside and outside of church. If the church had established clear and enforced supervision standards, it would have greatly reduced Tyler’s opportunities to groom or harm children.

Another serious failure was the mishandling of complaints. When concerns were raised about Tyler, they should have been reported immediately to the legal authorities. He should have been removed from all ministries that involved contact with minors while the matter was being reviewed. Pastor Ed failed to report the complaints and did not even inform his lead pastor. By ignoring serious allegations, he placed all the youth in the church at continued risk.

These failures resulted in the abuse of six boys and significant damage to the church’s witness in the community. Churches and ministries must have reliable systems in place to screen workers, supervise youth activities, and report any allegations of abuse immediately to the proper authorities. The cost of inaction is too high—for the children harmed and for the credibility of the church.

**Q:** In the context of this chapter, what are some ways a church or ministry can “buckle the belt of truth” and “put on the breastplate of righteousness?”

**A:** a) No one should have more than one or two friends. b) Ministers should not interact with the opposite sex. c) Ministers should not show favoritism toward friends. d) No one needs friends once they are adults.

**Q:** What are some possible consequences of not reporting suspected abuse to the proper legal authorities?

**A:** a) The Choosing of the Seven in Acts 6 resulted in growth and unity. b) Stephen’s Speech to the Sanhedrin in Acts 7 resulted in growth and unity. c) The Choosing of the Seven in Acts 6 resulted in division and splintering. d) Stephen’s Speech to the Sanhedrin in Acts 7 resulted in division and splintering.
